Chapter 1a — Marketing Orientations & Fundamentals (Dr. Alaa "BASIC CONCEPTS in Strategic Marketing" deck, 14 slides)

Source deck: 1- BASIC CONCEPTS_0.pptx (Dr. Alaa Elgharbawy, 14 slides — Kotler-derived content but local deck, not the Kotler Ch.1 textbook deck). Source lectures: Lec 1, Lec 2, parts of Lec 3-2 for relationship marketing. All claims below cite [Slide ...] or [Lec N @ mm:ss].

💡 In Plain English — Chapter 1a, told as one story

Same running example we'll carry across the whole course: you're about to open BrewHaus, a coffee shop. Chapter 1a answers the most basic question of all: "What does it even mean to do marketing?" Everything else in the course is built on the answers in this chapter.

1. Selling mindset vs. Marketing mindset

Two completely different ways to run BrewHaus:

  • Selling mindset = make the coffee YOU like, then chase people to buy it. If sales are slow → shout louder, drop the price, push harder.
  • Marketing mindset = walk around the neighborhood FIRST. Who lives here? What do they drink in the morning? What time? At what price? THEN design BrewHaus around what you learned.

Marketing = customer first, product second. Selling = product first, customer second.

2. Needs, Wants, Demand — three different things

The vocabulary marketers use to describe customers:

  • Need — biological/basic. "I'm tired and need caffeine." You can't invent needs; they're hard-wired.
  • Want — the cultural/personal SHAPE the need takes. "I want an Arabic-style mazbout coffee" vs. "I want an oat-milk cappuccino." Same need, different wants. Marketers create wants out of existing needs.
  • Demand — a want backed by money + willingness to pay. Wanting a 60 EGP cappuccino is just a want; being able and willing to pay = demand. BrewHaus only earns revenue from demand, not from needs or wants.

3. Value, Satisfaction, Exchange — the engine of all marketing

Customers run a quick math in their head every time they buy:

Value = Benefits the customer gets / Cost the customer pays

Cost isn't just money — it's also time, effort, switching pain. BrewHaus example: if your cappuccino tastes great (high benefit) but takes 15 minutes (high time cost) and you're rude (high effort cost), value drops — even if you charge 40 EGP. Two ways to increase value: raise benefits (better coffee, cozier space) or lower costs (faster service, friendlier staff, lower price).

Satisfaction = the gap between what customer EXPECTED and what they got. Promise too much → disappointment. Promise less and deliver more → delight.

Exchange = both sides give something to get something. Customer gives 50 EGP; BrewHaus gives a cappuccino + a smile + WiFi. If either side feels they gave more than they got, the exchange fails.

4. The 5 marketing orientations — five different "philosophies" a company can have

These are five different answers to "What should drive our business?" Marketing evolved through them historically:

  1. Production concept — "make it cheap and available." Customers will buy whatever is affordable. Works when demand > supply. BrewHaus version: sell the cheapest coffee in town, focus on efficiency. Risk: you ignore what customers actually want.
  2. Product concept — "make the BEST product." Customers will pay for quality. BrewHaus version: obsess over bean origin and barista training. Risk: "marketing myopia" — building a great product nobody wants. (Kodak made the best film just as digital killed film.)
  3. Selling concept — "push hard." Customers won't buy unless you aggressively promote. BrewHaus version: heavy ads, pushy upselling. Risk: short-term sales, no loyalty.
  4. Marketing concept — "understand the customer FIRST." Win by knowing the target customer better than competitors and delivering more value. BrewHaus version: survey the neighborhood, design around their actual lifestyle. This is the modern winning concept.
  5. Societal marketing concept — "serve the customer AND society." Add a third concern: long-term welfare of community/planet. BrewHaus version: recyclable cups, fair-trade beans, support local farmers. The customer wins, the planet wins, BrewHaus wins.

Memory trick: P → P → S → M → S (Produce, Perfect product, Sell hard, Marketing concept, Societal). They move from "us-focused" to "customer + world-focused."

5. The 4 P's — the marketer's toolkit (we'll spend the rest of the course on these)

Once you adopt the marketing concept, you have 4 levers to design BrewHaus's offer:

  • Product — what to sell. (Ch.8)
  • Price — how much to charge. (Ch.10)
  • Promotion — how customers hear about you. (Ch.11)
  • Place — how the product physically reaches them. (Ch.14)

This chapter just NAMES the 4 P's. Later chapters drill into each.

6. Relationship Marketing — keep the customer, don't just acquire them

Old marketing: chase new customers every day. Modern marketing: KEEP the customers you have because retaining one loyal customer is ~5× cheaper than acquiring a new one. BrewHaus example: loyalty stamp card → free 10th cup, "Good morning Ahmed, the usual?" by name, birthday-month free pastry. Each touch grows the relationship.

How chapter 1a connects together:

Marketing = customer-first thinking (not selling-first) → customers come with needs, wants, and demand (different things!) → they buy when they perceive Value (benefit/cost) and they stay when they feel Satisfaction → companies can run on 5 different orientations, but the winning one is the marketing concept (or societal marketing) → once you adopt it, you use the 4P toolkit to design the offer → and you build LONG-TERM relationships, not just one-off sales. BrewHaus's whole strategy will be built on top of these foundations.

In one sentence

Marketing is everything a company does to understand a customer first and then design the product, price, distribution and communication around that customer. Selling is pushing a product that already exists. Marketing is the opposite: pulling a product into existence because customers told you what they need. [Slide: Kotler Ch.1 — Definition of Marketing]

In plain language

Imagine you want to open a bakery. Two different mindsets:

  • The selling mindset — bake whatever bread you like, then chase people down the street shouting about how fresh it is. If sales are slow, shout louder or drop the price.
  • The marketing mindset — walk around the neighbourhood first. Ask: who lives here? Are they diabetic? Do they want low-carb? Do they want french bread or baladi? What do they pay now? When do they buy? Then design the bakery around what you learned.

This is called outside-in thinking (start from the market) versus inside-out thinking (start from the factory). Dr. Alaa hammers the point: marketing starts before production, selling starts after production [Lec 1 @ 1:01:30].

Needs vs wants vs demand. A need is a basic requirement — food, shelter, transport, status. A want is a specific desire shaped by culture and personality — shawarma vs. pizza, Toyota vs. BMW. Demand is a want backed by the ability to pay. Marketing doesn't invent needs; it reshapes wants and converts them into demand.

Why study Strategic Marketing Management? [Slide 2]

Slide 2 of the deck — often skipped because it sets up the rest — lists 8 forces that make strategic marketing thinking necessary today:

  • Changes in market structure — markets fragment, new segments appear, channels shift online.
  • Competition — both more competitors and tougher ones (global, digital, low-cost disruptors).
  • Cultural dynamics — tastes, values and norms shift over time; what sold in 1990 doesn't sell today.
  • Micro & Macro Factors — internal (firm capabilities, finances) and external (PESTEL: political, economic, social, technological, environmental, legal).
  • Impact of Technology — every product/channel/communication is touched by tech change (e.g. e-commerce, social, AI).
  • Marginal Propensity to Save/Consume (يطبق على السلع الأساسية و الرفاهية) — applies differently to essential goods vs luxury goods. In recessions, MPC on luxuries falls sharply; on staples it barely moves.
  • Changes in Buyers' Behavior — buyers research online, switch brands faster, expect personalisation.
  • Heterogeneity (تنوع المنتجات) — product variety has exploded; one-size-fits-all is dead.

Plain-English version: Strategic Marketing isn't a textbook ritual — it exists because the world keeps changing, and any company that doesn't deliberately re-think who it sells to, what they want, who else is chasing them, and how technology is reshaping the game will get crushed. The 8 forces are the reason every firm needs a marketing strategy, not just marketing.

Thinking strategically when estimating market size [Slide 3]

Slide 3 introduces a four-tier funnel for sizing a market that you must keep clear in your head:

TierWhat it isExample (cars in Egypt)
Market Potential EstimateThe theoretical maximum — everyone who could conceivably buy this category if everything aligned.All adult Egyptians who could one day own a car ≈ 60M people.
Industry Sales ForecastWhat the industry as a whole is realistically expected to sell next period.~250,000 new cars sold across all brands next year.
Company Sales ForecastWhat your company expects to sell — your forecasted slice of the industry.Toyota Egypt forecasts 40,000 units.
Unrealized PotentialThe gap between Market Potential and Industry Sales — buyers who could have bought but didn't (the addressable headroom).Millions of Egyptians who want a car but can't afford one or can't get financing — that's the unrealized layer.

Plain-English version: "How big is the market?" has four answers, not one. The biggest mistake juniors make is conflating Market Potential (the dream) with Industry Sales (this year's reality) — they pitch business cases off the wrong number. Strategic marketers always show all four tiers and the gap between them, because the unrealized potential is where growth strategies live.

Core concepts

1.1 What marketing really is

Kotler's one-line definition: "Marketing is managing profitable customer relationships." [Slide: Kotler Ch.1 — Definition of Marketing] Everything else in the chapter (orientations, mix, segmentation) is a sub-procedure of that single sentence.

Dr. Alaa's verbatim Arabic in class:

قبل أن أصنع ملابس أريد أن أرى أزواء الناس، أزواء الألوان، أزواء الموديلات، أزواء الأسعار، أزواء التصميمات

"Before I make clothes, I first need to see people's tastes — in colours, models, prices, designs." [Lec 1 @ 1:01:30] This is the marketing mindset in one line.

السيلنغ أنا ببيع منتج قد صنعته

"Selling means I'm selling a product I already made." [Lec 2 @ 36:40] Note the past tense — the product existed before the customer was even considered.

The interview question. Dr. Alaa tells job candidates: "What is the difference between Selling and Marketing? Between Production and Marketing? Ninety percent of people can't answer." [Lec 1 @ 2:10:57]. Treat this as exam-bait — expect a True/False or short-answer on it (proven on midterm Essay #3).

1.2 The five marketing management orientations

Companies have historically taken one of five stances toward the market. These stances evolved as markets matured: Production → Product (quality) → Selling → Marketing → Societal. [Slide: Kotler Ch.1 s.6-10] The first three are inside-out (factory-first); the last two are outside-in (customer-first).

Orientation Core belief Real-world icon
Production Make it cheap and available. If you can produce it, it will sell. Used when demand outstrips supply. Henry Ford's Model T; Egyptian Nasr-era factories — Ideal, Atlas, Harbeya refrigerators, Fajr stoves.
Product (Quality) Make it better. Consumers will pay more for higher quality, performance, features. Constant improvement. Post-war Japan — Toyota, Sony, Honda. Rebuilt through obsession with quality.
Selling The market is saturated; the customer won't buy unless we shout. Invest in promotion, discounts, payment facilities. Insurance salespeople, encyclopedia door-knockers, real-estate cold-calls.
Marketing Start with the customer's needs; design everything around them. Build a long-term relationship. Apple, Starbucks, IKEA — companies obsessed with understanding the user's life.
Societal Balance customer wants with society's long-term welfare: environment, health, vulnerable groups. Patagonia (environment), Dove (body image), Tesla (sustainability).

Why do you need all five? Because different markets and different eras demand different stances. Egypt in 1960 had a production-concept market (demand > supply). Egypt in 2026 has a marketing-concept market (consumer is overwhelmed by choice). A single company can evolve through stages as its industry matures.

Important slide bullet Dr. Alaa emphasised: the first three orientations (Production, Product, Selling) do NOT support long-term customer relationships — they end up creating dissatisfaction and switching. Only Marketing and Societal build loyalty. [Slide: Kotler Ch.1 s.6-10]

Dr. Alaa's verbatim Arabic anchoring these:

يمكنهم إنتاج كميات كبيرة ويقللوا الأسعار... ما كانت أكثر البرنجات التي تعمل في مصر؟ هيديال... وقبل هيديال كان هناك أطلس مثلاً، وكان هناك شيء يسمى برجاز فجر... مثل مصنع الحربية

"They could produce large quantities and lower prices. What were the most famous Egyptian brands? Ideal. Before Ideal there was Atlas. And things like Fajr stoves and the Harbeya factory." [Lec 2 @ 50:00] These were classic Production-concept brands — the market wanted goods, any goods, at an affordable price.

لو اخذت دولة زي اليابان دي اتنسفت مرتين ورجع التاني قوة عظمى، وايه اللي زود قوة في اليابان؟ اعلى جودة انتاج

"Japan was bombed twice and came back a superpower. What restored Japan's strength? The highest production quality." [Lec 2 @ 14:24] That's the Product concept — win with superior quality.

1.2.1 The Selling and Marketing Concepts Contrasted [Slide 9 — figure]

Slide 9 is the classic Kotler four-axis figure that lays the two concepts side by side. The point: they aren't a soft "style" difference, they're opposite operating systems with different starting points, foci, means, and ends.

AxisSelling ConceptMarketing Concept
Starting pointThe factory (we already made it)The market (what does the customer want?)
FocusExisting productsCustomer needs
MeansSelling and promotion (push)Integrated marketing (pull)
EndsProfit through sales volumeProfit through customer satisfaction & loyalty

Plain-English version: Selling tries to convert what's already in the warehouse into cash. Marketing decides what goes into the warehouse in the first place by listening to customers. Same end-goal (profit), opposite route.

1.3 Relationship marketing and one-to-one marketing

Relationship marketing means treating every customer interaction as one point in a long-term relationship, not a standalone transaction. One-to-one marketing is the extreme version: technology lets you tailor the product and the message to a single individual. [Slide: Kotler Ch.1 s.12]

The old Kodak slogan, "Press the button, we do the rest" — that's transactional. The customer gives money, gets a photo, goes home. Contrast it with Netflix: every show you watch teaches the system what to recommend next. Each interaction deepens the relationship and makes leaving the platform more painful. That is relationship marketing.

One-to-one marketing is when your product line has effectively one unit per customer. A bride gets her wedding car painted with her name; a private-banking client has an investment portfolio no one else in the world owns; a custom music playlist is generated for you alone. It's only possible because technology made it cheap to customise at scale (mass customisation).

Dr. Alaa's verbatim Arabic:

الهدف المبدأي للمركب... هو تشتغل طريقة طويلة ومتعاونة مفيدة للطرفين. الطرفين اللي هم مين؟ الشركة والشركة... أي مجتمع يحتاج شركات

"The primary purpose of marketing is to build a long, cooperative, mutually beneficial relationship for both sides — the company and the customer. Society needs companies — they produce goods, employ people, pay taxes, advance society." [Lec 1 @ 2:08:29]

انا ممكن افصل برنامج كمبيوتر شركة معينة، موسيقى معينة، ده tailor — ده اسمه one-to-one marketing or customized marketing

"I can tailor a software programme to a specific company, a specific music playlist — that's called one-to-one marketing, or customised marketing." [Lec 3-2 @ 34:21]

عروسة عايزة تعمل كوستان بمواصفات معينة... الاتليال اللي عيبصر الكوستان بمواصفات مطبعة العروسة دي بس

"A bride wants a wedding car customised to specific specs — the atelier builds a car matching only that bride's requirements." [Lec 3-2 @ 20:22] This is pure micromarketing / individual marketing / one-to-one.

Dr. Alaa's in-class examples for Ch.1a

  • Bakery thought experiment (Selling vs Marketing mindset) [Lec 1 @ 1:01:30]
  • Kodak film vs Netflix — transactional vs relationship marketing [Slide: Kotler Ch.1 s.12]
  • Ideal / Atlas / Harbeya / Fajr — Egyptian Production-concept icons [Lec 2 @ 50:00]
  • Post-war Japan (Toyota / Sony / Honda) — Product (quality) concept [Lec 2 @ 14:24]
  • Bride's custom wedding car — one-to-one / mass customisation [Lec 3-2 @ 20:22]
  • The interview question Dr. Alaa asks job candidates — "What is the difference between Selling and Marketing?" [Lec 1 @ 2:10:57]

Common confusions for Ch.1a

Concept A Concept B Key difference
Selling Marketing Selling pushes an existing product (inside-out). Marketing starts from the customer (outside-in).
Need Want A need is a basic requirement (food). A want is a culturally-shaped specific form (shawarma vs. pizza).
Production concept Product concept Production = cheap + available (Ford Model T, Ideal). Product = highest quality + features (post-war Japan, Apple).
Product concept Marketing concept Product concept pushes the best product (inside-out). Marketing concept starts from customer needs (outside-in).
Relationship marketing Transactional marketing Relationship = many touchpoints, long lifetime value (Netflix). Transactional = one-off exchange (Kodak film).
Mass customisation / one-to-one / individual marketing / markets-of-one (these are the same thing — synonyms) All four labels describe tailoring to a single customer using technology.

Why this matters for the final

  • Per CALIBRATION.md, Ch.1a was ~22% of the actual midterm (definitions, orientations, value proposition, market offering). Expect a similar 15-20% weight on the final's MCQ + T/F sections.
  • High-confidence retest items (all hit verbatim on midterm — Dr. Alaa recycles them):
  • MCQ: "___ is managing profitable customer relationships" (Marketing)
  • MCQ: "Marketing is more than ___ and advertising" (selling)
  • MCQ: "set of benefits the company promises to consumers" (value proposition)
  • MCQ: "Societal marketing balances consumer wants and society's ___" (long-run welfare)
  • MCQ: "Which concept favours quality / performance / features?" (Product concept)
  • T/F: "Production concept means consumers favor products that are available and highly affordable" (TRUE)
  • T/F: "Relationship marketing means maintaining a good relationship with customers more than just saving them with products" (TRUE)
  • T/F: "Market offerings are not limited to physical goods" (TRUE)
  • Calibration miss to fix on final: MCQ on "Customer satisfaction depends on perceived value relative to buyer's ___" (expectations) — added at bottom of this file.
  • Essay-style retest: "Selling concept versus Marketing concept" — proven exam essay.

Chapter 1b — Strategic Marketing Management (Kerin Ch.1 + Strategic Pilot deck)

Source decks: Kerin Ch.1 slides + Strategic Marketing Pilot deck (Basic Concepts s.2-3, Strategic Pilot s.5-37). Source lectures: Lec 1 (mostly), Lec 2 (mostly). All claims below cite [Slide ...] or [Lec N @ mm:ss].

💡 In Plain English — Chapter 1b, told as one story

Same BrewHaus running example. In Ch.1a you learned the philosophy of marketing (customer first, the 5 orientations, the 4P toolkit). In Ch.1b you zoom out to strategy: the long-term thinking that decides which markets BrewHaus should even enter, which products to push there, how to plan over 5–10 years, and how to recover when things go wrong.

1. What's different about "strategic" marketing?

Normal day-to-day marketing = "what ad do we run on Instagram tomorrow?" Strategic marketing = "should BrewHaus be a single shop, a national chain, a B2B coffee-bean supplier, or all three? And in which cities?" Strategic = big questions, multi-year, top management decisions. Tactical = daily operations.

2. The 5-step strategic marketing CYCLE (memorize this)

Every company runs (and re-runs) the same 5-step cycle. BrewHaus is no exception:

  1. Define the business — "What business are we actually in?" Coffee retail? Hospitality? Snack distribution? The answer changes everything else. (Famous example: railroads thought they were in "trains" — they were really in "transportation," and missed the airline opportunity. Don't be Kodak.)
  2. Identify opportunities (SWOT) — scan internally for Strengths/Weaknesses and externally for Opportunities/Threats.
  3. Formulate strategy — pick WHICH products to send to WHICH markets (Ansoff matrix), and design the 4P mix to support it.
  4. Budget & resource the plan — assign money, people, time. A strategy without a budget is wishful thinking.
  5. Marketing audit / control — measure results, compare to plan, FIX what's off. Then re-run the whole cycle.

The cycle never stops — re-run every year (and emergency-rerun whenever the market shifts, like COVID).

3. SWOT — the diagnostic that feeds step 2

A 2×2 grid:

  • Strengths (internal, positive) — what BrewHaus does better than competitors. (Best Arabic-coffee recipe in Alexandria. Loyal regulars.)
  • Weaknesses (internal, negative) — what BrewHaus is bad at. (Slow service. No delivery infrastructure.)
  • Opportunities (external, positive) — market trends BrewHaus could ride. (Café culture growing in Alex; office workers want premium coffee delivered.)
  • Threats (external, negative) — dangers from outside. (Starbucks opening 2 blocks away; coffee bean prices rising.)

How to use it: match strengths to opportunities to find your winning move (S × O = "use our great Arabic recipe to capture the growing café market"). Use strengths to defend against threats (S × T). Fix weaknesses before they collide with threats (W × T).

4. The Ansoff Matrix — which products in which markets?

Another 2×2 grid that answers "where should BrewHaus grow?":

Existing productsNew products
Existing markets1. Market Penetration — sell more of what you already sell, to people who already buy. (Get current BrewHaus customers to buy MORE cappuccinos: loyalty cards, larger sizes.) Lowest risk.3. Product Development — sell NEW things to existing customers. (Add pastries, sandwiches, branded mugs to the BrewHaus menu.) Medium risk.
New markets2. Market Development — sell EXISTING product to NEW customer groups. (Take BrewHaus cappuccino to Cairo. Or target teenagers instead of professionals.) Medium risk.4. Diversification — new product + new market. (BrewHaus opens a clothing brand. Or a B2B coffee-machine rental business.) Highest risk.

Memory trick: the more "new" stuff you have (new product? new market?) the higher the risk. Always start with Penetration; only Diversify when you've mastered the others.

5. The 4 P's overview (preview of Ch.8, 10, 11, 14)

Once strategy picks the where/what, the 4P mix is the tactical toolkit to execute:

  • Product — what you offer. (Ch.8)
  • Price — what you charge. (Ch.10)
  • Promotion — how customers learn about you. (Ch.11)
  • Place — how the product reaches them. (Ch.14)

The 4P mix must be consistent with the strategy chosen in Ansoff. Example: if BrewHaus picked "Market Development → premium professionals in Cairo," then Product = premium signature drinks, Price = high-end, Promotion = LinkedIn ads, Place = upscale district shops. If those Ps don't match the strategy, the strategy fails.

6. Budgeting and Marketing Audit — closing the cycle

Budget = "how much money do we spend on each P, and over how long?" Without a budget, a strategy is just a wish. BrewHaus example: 30% Product (new beans), 20% Place (delivery setup), 25% Promotion (ads), 25% reserve.

Marketing audit = the 12-month check-up. Did sales hit plan? Did the new market work? Are weaknesses now smaller or worse? The audit feeds the NEXT year's strategy cycle.

How chapter 1b connects together:

Strategic marketing = the long-term layer ABOVE day-to-day marketing → runs as a 5-step cycle (define business → SWOT to find opportunities → formulate strategy via Ansoff → execute with 4P mix → audit and re-run) → SWOT is the diagnostic; Ansoff is the strategic choice; 4Ps are the execution levers; budgeting funds it; audit closes the loop. Ch.1a told us the marketing philosophy. Ch.1b shows us how to plan its long-term execution. Ch.7 will tell us how to pick the customer. Ch.8-14 will go deep on each P.

In one sentence

Strategic marketing management is the five-step cycle a company runs (and re-runs as the environment changes) to decide what business it is in, what opportunities to pursue, which products to send to which markets, how to fund the plan, and how to recover when things go wrong. [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot]

In plain language

The strategic-marketing layer sits above day-to-day marketing operations. While a campaign manager is choosing today's ad copy, the strategic marketer is asking: which business are we even in?, which opportunities are worth pursuing for the next 5–10 years?, which products go to which markets?, how is the plan funded?, and what do we do when the environment turns against us?

The 8 reasons listed on Basic Concepts slide 2 (slide title: "Why studying Strategic Marketing Management?" — see next section) explain why strategic marketing is a distinct discipline. The five processes (§2.1) are the cycle every firm runs. SWOT (§2.3) is the diagnostic that drives Process 2 (opportunity identification). The Ansoff matrix (§2.4) is the product-market grid for Process 3 (strategy formulation). The 4Ps overview (§2.5) is the tactical lever-set inside Process 3 — and the deep dives on each P live in the four new-chapter tabs (Ch.8 Product, Ch.10 Pricing, Ch.11 Promotion, Ch.14 Place). Budgeting and the marketing audit (§2.6) close the cycle.

Core concepts

2.0 The Primary Purpose of Marketing [Slide 4]

Deck-verbatim: "To create long-term and mutually beneficial exchange relationships." This single line — easy to skip as filler — is the anchor for every Process that follows: every framework (mission, SWOT, Ansoff, Mix, audit) ultimately serves this purpose.

2.1 Processes in Strategic Marketing Management

Before the processes, three slide-grounded preludes:

Why studying Strategic Marketing Management? — the 8 reasons on slide 2 [Slide: Basic Concepts s.2]:

  1. Changes in market structure — new entrants, consolidations, deregulation, e-commerce reshaping how a market is organised.
  2. Competition — globalisation means the threat is no longer just the shop next door; it's a Chinese or Turkish substitute landed at half your price.
  3. Cultural dynamics — values, family roles, religious adherence, gender norms shift over time. What sold in 2000 doesn't always sell now.
  4. Micro & Macro factors — micro: customers, suppliers, intermediaries, competitors. Macro: economy, demographics, technology, politics, law.
  5. Impact of technology — smartphones, AI, e-payments, social media advertising. Technology destroys old channels (Kodak film) and creates new ones (Netflix, ride-hailing).
  6. Marginal Propensity to Save / Consume — when income rises, do people save (luxury goods slow down) or consume (luxury goods boom)? Marginal propensity to consume applies most to basic goods; marginal propensity to save applies more to luxury goods.
  7. Changes in buyers' behaviour — younger generations buy online, read reviews, switch brands faster, ignore TV ads.
  8. Heterogeneity (تنوع المنتجات) — markets fragment into more sub-segments every year. There is no average customer any more.

The marketing manager's evolved role: from tactical executor (printing brochures, running campaigns, organising trade shows) to strategy maker [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.5-6]. Dr. Alaa's verbatim:

From being only an implementer ... to being a maker of market organization. هو بقى الميكر، هو اللي بيصنع الاستراتيجي للشركة

"They are now the maker, the one who builds the strategy for the company." [Lec 2 @ 42:17-42:31]

Expanded responsibilities: - Charting the direction of the organisation — deciding which markets and products the company should pursue 5–10 years out. - Contributing to decisions that create and sustain a competitive advantage — pricing, branding, positioning calls that affect long-term performance. - Making strategic decisions, not just operational ones — sitting at the executive table.

Sizing the market strategically — the four levels [Slide: Basic Concepts s.3]:

  1. Market Potential — the absolute ceiling. How many units could conceivably be sold under ideal conditions to every possible buyer? (Every adult in Egypt is a potential mobile-phone buyer.)
  2. Industry Sales Forecast — what the entire industry is expected to actually sell next period (less than market potential).
  3. Company Sales Forecast — what your firm expects to sell = market share × industry sales.
  4. Unrealized Potential — the gap between Market Potential and your Company Sales. This is the white space — the room to grow.

The strategic mindset is to constantly ask: where is my unrealized potential, and which marketing actions can convert it? A complacent firm forecasts company sales and stops there. A strategic firm forecasts industry sales, sees the gap, and plans how to close it.

The 5 processes — deck-verbatim wording [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.7] (memorise these forms — Dr. Alaa tests using this exact phrasing):

  1. Defining the organization's business, mission, and goals [Slide s.8 — Process One]
  2. Identifying and framing organizational growth opportunities [Slide s.15 — Process Two]
  3. Formulating product-market strategies [Slide s.23 — Process Three]
  4. Budgeting marketing, financial, and production resources [Slide s.33 — Process Four]
  5. Developing reformulation and recovery strategies [Slide s.35 — Process Five]

Plain-language gloss (what each process actually does — use this to understand, but quote the verbatim list above on the exam):

  1. Define the business, mission, and goals. What are we here for? Who do we serve? Where do we draw the line?
  2. Identify and frame growth opportunities. What's happening in the environment? Which opportunities fit our strengths? (SWOT lives here.)
  3. Formulate product-market strategies. Ansoff's grid: penetration, market development, product development, diversification. Plus the Marketing Mix (4Ps).
  4. Budget marketing, financial, and production resources. Translate the plan into money — operating budget (income statement) and financial budget (cash flow). Note the 3 resource categories — same Production / Financial / Marketing trio as the Business Goals slide (s.13-14).
  5. Develop reformulation and recovery strategies. When the environment turns — war, pandemic, currency shock — rework the plan.

Dr. Alaa's verbatim:

المعلومات اللي بتعملها... ثم بتعمل SWOT — نقاط القوة والضعف للشركة مع الفرص والتهديدات

"First you gather information, then you do SWOT — strengths, weaknesses, opportunities, threats." [Lec 2 @ 33:38]

Dr. Alaa spent real time on Process 5 (recovery). Example: during Gulf–Egypt currency pressures and during COVID, companies that couldn't reformulate their plan went bankrupt. BYD, a Chinese car company, pivoted its assembly lines to produce masks during COVID — that's a reformulation triggered by environmental change [Lec 2 @ 1:48:21].

2.2 Process One: Defining the Organization's Business, Mission, and Goals

[Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.8 — Process One anchor]

Process One asks: who are we, what are we here for, and what does success look like? Before any scanning or strategy can begin, the organisation must define its business from the market's perspective, crystallise that into a mission statement, and convert the mission into concrete, measurable goals across three categories.

Define your business from the market (what the customer's need is), not from the product (what you happen to produce). This one shift protects you from going obsolete when technology changes the product.

Kodak defined itself as "we make film." When digital photography arrived, Kodak died. If Kodak had defined itself as "we capture memories," it would have embraced digital cameras instead of fighting them.

Dr. Alaa's four canonical market-perspective redefinitions:

Company Product perspective (narrow) Market perspective (wide)
Toyota We make cars. We provide transportation solutions — land, sea, air; trucks, buses, vans, forklifts.
Microsoft (Bill Gates) We sell software. "We are not producing products, we are solving our customers' problems." This is literally where the word "solutions" entered the software industry.
Disney We run amusement parks / make films. We provide entertainment.
IKEA We sell furniture. "Home dress" — every single thing that goes into making a home a home: furniture, candles, bedsheets, even chlorine.

Why this matters: if you call yourself "a sweets shop" (El-Abd, El-Orouba), you'll lose when a café opens next door that also sells nuts, sweets, coffee. But if you define yourself as an entertainment venue, the café is a natural extension, not a threat.

Business definition answers three questions: (1) Who are our customers? (2) What needs do we satisfy? (3) With what means / technology?

The mission statement then formalises the business definition into a short public statement — capturing purpose, customers, products, markets, philosophy, technology. It is revisable as the business evolves.

Why a mission statement matters — 4 specific benefits [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.12]:

  1. Crystallizes management's vision of the organisation's long-term direction and character. Forces leadership to put a stake in the ground.
  2. Provides guidance in identifying, pursuing, and evaluating market and product opportunities. When a new opportunity arises, the mission tells you whether it fits.
  3. Inspires and challenges employees to do those things valued by the organisation and its customers. A good mission rallies the team; a generic mission inspires nobody.
  4. Provides direction for setting business goals or objectives. The mission tells you what to measure; the goals tell you whether you're hitting it.

Business goals — the three categories

Goals (or objectives) convert the organisation's mission into tangible actions and results that are to be achieved, often within a specified time frame. [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.13]

The deck names three major categories of goals: [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.13-14]

CategoryWhat the slide says
Production Goals Apply to the use of manufacturing and service capacity and to product and service quality.
Financial Goals Focus on return on investment, return on sales, profit, cash flow, and shareholder wealth.
Marketing Goals Market share · marketing productivity · sales volume · profit · customer satisfaction · customer value creation

Exam tip: The most tested distinction is Financial vs Marketing. A question asking "what kind of goal is 'increase market share'?" → Marketing goal. "Increase ROI?" → Financial goal. Production goals are rarely the answer choice but know the definition.

Dr. Alaa's Arabic quotes:

شركة تيوتا بتنتج ايه؟ تشتغل في صناعات ايه؟ مش السيارة. السوق بيبص التيوتا لانها تشتغل في مجال النقل... نقل بري، نقل جوي، نقل بحري

"What does Toyota produce? Not cars. The market sees Toyota as being in the transportation business — land, air, sea." [Lec 1 @ 46:29]

we are not producing products, we are solving our customers' problems. احنا بنحل مشاكل العوملة. من هنا طلعت كلمة solution

Microsoft's framing from Bill Gates — "We are not producing products, we are solving customers' problems. That's where the word 'solution' was born." [Lec 2 @ 48:25]

ايكايا فرنيتشر... لا كل حاجة — الحلول البيت. أي حاجة في البيت — منزل، هوم درس. حط شطارة، حط ورق، حط شمع

"IKEA furniture? No — everything for the home. Anything in the home: curtains, paper, candles. That's 'home dress'." [Lec 1 @ 54:01]

2.3 Process Two: Identifying and Framing Organizational Growth Opportunities

[Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.15 — Process Two anchor]

Process Two asks: what real growth opportunities exist for us, and which of them actually fit who we are? After the mission and goals are set (Process One), the organisation must scan its environment, map its own strengths, and frame only those opportunities where its capabilities genuinely match what the market needs. This is where the analytical work begins — before any strategy is formulated.

A real growth opportunity exists only where your internal strengths (distinctive competencies) match the external environment's needs (success requirements of the market).

Environmental opportunities have three typical sources: (1) unmet or changing customer needs, (2) unsatisfied buyer groups, (3) new means or technology for delivering value. [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.16]

Distinctive Competency — verbatim deck definition [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.17]:
"describes an organization's unique strengths or qualities including: Skills, Technologies, Resources …that distinguish it from other organizations."

(Use to understand: it's what you do best — the capabilities competitors can't easily replicate. But quote the verbatim definition above on the exam.) [Slide: Kerin Ch.1 s.18]

Success Requirements — verbatim deck definition [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.18]:
"are basic tasks that an organization must perform in a market or industry to compete successfully."

Critical conditional — verbatim [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.18]:
"If what must be done is inconsistent with what can be done to capitalize on an environmental opportunity, an organizational growth opportunity will fail to materialize."

(Use to understand: if your distinctive competency doesn't cover the market's success requirements, the opportunity is not yours.)

SWOT analysis — the diagnostic framework

SWOT — verbatim deck definition [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.19]:
"A formal framework for identifying and framing organizational growth opportunities."

An organizational growth opportunity results from [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.20]:
"a good fit between an organization's INTERNAL CAPABILITIES (Strengths & Weaknesses) and its EXTERNAL ENVIRONMENT (Opportunities & Threats)."

SWOT arranges the diagnostic as a 2×2 [Slide: Kerin Ch.1 s.20]:

Favourable Unfavourable
Internal (you) Strengths — brand, money, location, technology Weaknesses — outdated facilities, weak talent
External (world) Opportunities — new segments, tech shifts, crises creating demand Threats — currency shocks, war, substitute products

Verbatim slide definitions — quote these exactly on the exam [Slide: Kerin Ch.1 s.21]:

ElementDeck-verbatim definition
Strength What the organization is good at doing or a characteristic that gives it an important capability.
Weakness What an organization lacks or does poorly relative to competitors.
Opportunities Developments or conditions in the environment that have favorable implications for the organization.
Threats Pose dangers to the organization.

Dr. Alaa was emphatic:

ده framework اللي بيتعامل لازم أي تحطيط استراتيجي على المدى طويل

"This framework MUST be used in any long-term strategic planning." [Lec 2 @ 1:45:59]

Three diagnostic questions to ask once SWOT is on the page [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.22]:

  1. Which internal strengths represent distinctive competencies? Deck-verbatim: "Do these strengths compare favorably with what are believed to be market or industry success requirements?" [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.22] Not every "strength" is a real differentiator — if a strength is widely shared by competitors, it isn't distinctive.
  2. Which internal weaknesses disqualify the organisation from pursuing certain opportunities? Some weaknesses are deal-breakers — no money for capex, no licence, no distribution reach in the target geography. These rule whole opportunity classes out of the picture early.
  3. Does a pattern emerge from the SWOT? The most useful insight is rarely a single S, W, O, or T item — it's the cross-pairing. A Strength matched to an Opportunity = offensive move. A Weakness matched to a Threat = existential risk.

The compound real-estate opportunity [Lec 1 @ 41:30]: before 2000, Egypt had only traditional apartment buildings. Gulf-based developers (Emaar, SODIC, Dajla, Mountain View) saw a gap — Egyptians with rising income who wanted gated security, parking, and services. They built compounds. Result: Egyptians now pay double the per-m² price for amenities the old buildings never offered. That's an environmental opportunity converted into an organisational one — but only because the developers had the distinctive competency (capital, construction tech, imported compound-design know-how).

COVID pivot — BYD making masks [Lec 2 @ 1:48:21]: a Chinese car maker stopped car production and pivoted its factory to masks. The environmental opportunity (pandemic mask shortage) matched BYD's distinctive competency (automated assembly line, supply chain speed). Nokia, similarly, was originally a forestry / wood-products company before it pivoted to mobile.

Crocs vs Skechers [Lec 1 @ 43:43]: Crocs had a distinctive competency in comfort material. Skechers copied and surpassed them. This is why a company needs a sustainable competitive advantage — something competitors can't easily replicate. Apple's AirPods made wireless the default; no customer accepts wired headphones anymore [Lec 1 @ 2:23:00]. That's sustainable.

2.4 Process Three: Formulating Product-Market Strategies

[Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.23 — Process Three anchor]

Process Three asks: which markets should we enter, and with which products? Having identified a real opportunity (Process Two), the organisation now chooses a growth trajectory using the Ansoff Matrix, then designs the Marketing Mix (4Ps) to execute it. Both tools belong to this process — Ansoff sets the direction, the 4Ps provide the levers.

2.4.1 Product-Market Strategies (Ansoff Matrix) [Slide 24]

Naming note: Slide 24's title is "Product-Market Strategies" — the deck never uses the word "Ansoff." We label it that way here because that's how the framework is universally known in the textbook/exam world.

Ansoff's 2×2 grid (Existing vs New products, Existing vs New markets) gives four growth strategies: Market Penetration, Market Development, Product Development, and Diversification — in increasing order of risk.

Existing products New products
Existing markets Market Penetration — sell more of what you already have to customers you already reach. Product Development — create new offerings for your existing markets.
New markets Market Development — introduce existing offerings to markets you don't yet serve. Diversification — new product, new market. High-risk strategy.

Verbatim deck definitions — quote these exactly on the exam [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.25–29]:

StrategyDeck-verbatim definition
Market Penetration"Seeking a larger market share in a market in which organization already has an offering."
Market Development"Introducing its existing offerings to markets other than those that the organization is currently serving."
Product Development"Creating new offerings for existing markets."
Diversification"Development or acquisition of offerings new to the organization and introducing those offerings to publics not previously served by the organization."

Tactics per strategy — verbatim from deck:

  • Market Penetration [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.25]: (1) Attempts to increase present buyer's usage or consumption rates of the offering; (2) Attracting buyers of competing offerings; (3) Stimulating product trial among potential consumers.
  • Market Development [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.26] — reaching new markets requires: (1) Carefully considering competitor strengths and weaknesses and competitor retaliation potential; (2) Modification of the basic offering; (3) Different distribution outlets; (4) Change in sales effort and advertising. Internationally: the four modes [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.27] — Exporting, Licensing, Joint Venture or Strategic Alliance, Direct Investment.
  • Product Development [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.28] — three modes: Product Innovation – develop totally new offerings; Product Augmentation – enhance the value to customers of existing offerings; Product Line Extension – broaden the existing line of offerings by adding different sizes, forms, flavors, etc.
  • Diversification [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.29]: high-risk because both the offering and market served are new to the organization. Often done by acquisition.

Dr. Alaa's verbatim Arabic — the Downy story (Market Penetration):

أول ما نزل [Downy]... محدش يشتريه، محدش عارف. محطه مع اوكسي... حطوا قبوات مع ايرين، ناس تجربوا. الناس عرفيتوا دلوقت، دلوقت انفصل، وبقى لها سعار، وسعره بقى غالي

"When Downy first launched, nobody knew it, nobody bought it. They bundled it with Oxi and Ariel detergents. People tried it that way, learned about it, and now Downy sells separately at a premium with large stable packs." [Lec 2 @ 1:53:30]

منتج جديد خالص قدمته للسوق لنفس الأسواق الموجودة حالياً

"A brand new product introduced to the same markets I already serve." [Lec 2 @ 1:55:19] That's Product Development (innovation) — the iPad and iWatch were new products for the same Apple ecosystem.

augmentation معناها تمديد المنتج. مش بعمل innovation... هو نفس المنتج الموجود، بس انا بحسنه وبطوره شوية، بعمله اضافة، بعمله تعديل

"Augmentation means extending the product. It's not innovation — it's the same product, just improved or modified." [Lec 2 @ 1:55:44]

بيبسيكو بتشتغل في مجال ايه — مشروه... والجديد عليه المجال في كوستا هو الضيافة... بيبسيكو كمان استحوذت على Pizza Hut و KFC

"PepsiCo is in beverages. Costa Coffee is hospitality — new to them. PepsiCo also acquired Pizza Hut and KFC." [Lec 3-2 @ 31:28] That's Diversification by acquisition — also why you can't order Coca-Cola at KFC and you can't order Pepsi at McDonald's.

2.4.2 Market Development in the International Arena [Slide 27]

Slide 27 expands Market Development (existing product, new market) into the cross-border case with 4 entry modes, in increasing order of commitment + control + risk:

  1. Exporting — ship product from home country; lowest commitment, lowest control.
  2. Licensing — let a foreign partner produce/sell using your brand & IP for a fee/royalty.
  3. Joint Venture (JV) — local partner + your firm form a co-owned entity in the target country.
  4. Direct Investment — build/buy your own subsidiary abroad; highest commitment, full control, full risk.

2.4.3 Strategy Selection — Sample Decision Tree [Slide 30]

Slide 30 illustrates the Process-3 choice as a decision tree: Market Penetration vs Market Development branched against competitor reaction (aggressive vs passive), with payoff figures ($2M / $3M / $1M / $4M) on the leaves. The exam takeaway: strategy choice depends not just on your own move but on how competitors are expected to respond — payoff is conditional on the competitive scenario.

2.5 The Marketing Mix — 4Ps (Slide 31) + 7Ps & 4Rs extensions (textbook, not on deck) [Slide 31 — "The Marketing Mix (4 p's)"]

Scope note: Slide 31 itself shows only the classic 4Ps (Product, Price, Place, Promotion). The 7Ps extension (People, Process, Physical evidence) and the 4Rs reframe (Relevance, Response, Relationship, Results) are textbook material added here for completeness — neither is on the Ch.1b deck.

Once you've chosen a growth trajectory via Ansoff (§2.4), the 4Ps are the four levers you actually pull to execute that strategy. They are always designed around the customer, who sits at the centre [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.4]:

  • Product — what you sell (features, design, quality, branding, packaging, service). → Deep dive: Ch.8 Product tab.
  • Price — what the customer pays (list price, discounts, credit terms, allowances, skimming vs penetration). → Deep dive: Ch.10 Pricing tab.
  • Place — how you distribute (channels, coverage, inventory, transport, locations). → Deep dive: Ch.14 Place tab.
  • Promotion — how you communicate (advertising, personal selling, sales promotions, PR, direct marketing, digital). → Deep dive: Ch.11 Promotion tab.

Service businesses extended the 4Ps to 7Ps by adding People (trained staff), Process (the steps that deliver the service), and Physical evidence (tangible cues of quality — Apple store design, hotel lobby). A more recent reframe is the 4Rs: Relevance, Recognition, Rewards, Relationships — shifting from transactional push to loyalty and long-term value.

Dr. Alaa's verbatim:

شايفة احنا حطينا في النص مين؟ الكاسمور... لو انا مش تاهم الكاسمور بتاعي مقدرش اصمم برودكت كرايز وعمللو برايسين كرايز. لازم احط سعر يناسب هذا الكاسمور

"See who we put in the centre? The customer. If I don't understand my customer I can't design the right product or set the right price. The price has to fit that customer." [Lec 2 @ 1:49:35] The 4Ps orbit the customer — always.

الامريكان يقولك كاستمر هو رائد

"The Americans say: the Customer is king." [Lec 1 @ 2:23:36]

2.6 Processes Four & Five: Budgeting, Reformulation, the Marketing Audit — and the Marketing Plan [Slides 32-37]

Scope note: bundles three deck topics that the slide-3 agenda separates: Process 4 (Budgeting & resource allocation, s.32-34), Process 5 (Reformulation / Marketing Audit, s.35-36), and the standalone Marketing Plan (s.37 — agenda item 6, not a Process-5 sub-bullet).

Process Four: Budgeting Marketing, Financial, and Production Resources [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.33 — Process Four anchor]

Process Four translates the chosen strategy into money. The organisation allocates its three resource categories — Marketing, Financial, and Production — in a formal budget (note: same trio as the Business Goals categories in §2.2).

Budget (textbook verbatim, slide 33): "a formal, quantitative expression of an organisation's planning and strategy initiatives expressed in financial terms." A well-prepared budget meshes and balances the firm's Financial, Production, and Marketing resources so that overall organisational goals or objectives are attained [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.33]. Two components [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.34]:

  • Operating Budget (also referred to as a pro forma Income Statement) — focuses on an organization's income statement.
  • Financial Budget — focuses on the effect that the operating budget and other initiatives will have on the organization's cash position.

Process Five: Developing Reformulation and Recovery Strategies [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.35 — Process Five anchor]

Process Five asks: when the environment changes drastically, how do we rework the plan? Triggers include currency shocks, war, a pandemic, or a disruptive competitor. The output is either a reformulation (a reworked strategy for the new reality) or a recovery plan (getting back on track after a crisis). The Marketing Audit is the diagnostic tool that feeds Process Five — it tells you what needs reformulating.

The Marketing Audit — verbatim deck definition [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.36]: "Comprehensive, systematic, independent, and periodic examination of a company's marketing environment, objectives, strategies, and activities to recommend a plan of action to improve the company's marketing performance."

It answers two questions — memorise these exactly:

  1. Are we doing the right things? (Is our strategy aimed at the right markets, customers, value propositions?)
  2. Are we doing things right? (Are we executing the strategy efficiently — budgets, processes, channels?)

Both questions are necessary: a company can execute a flawed strategy brilliantly, or execute a brilliant strategy badly — either way the outcome suffers.

The Marketing Plan — slide 37's exact definition: "A formal written document that describes the context and scope of an organisation's marketing effort to achieve defined goals or objectives within a specified future time period." [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.37]

Two characteristics: - Focus — the plan can be written for a whole business, a single product, or a specific brand. Each level has its own document. - Time dimension — short-run plans typically cover one year (the annual marketing plan), long-run plans span multiple years (3–5 years for strategic plans).

How the plan relates to everything else in this course: the plan is the output of all five processes from §2.1. Process 1 (mission/goals) goes in the front. Process 2 (SWOT/opportunities) shapes the strategy section. Process 3 (Ansoff + 4Ps) produces the action plan. Process 4 (budget) gives it numbers. Process 5 (recovery) is the contingency section. The marketing audit then loops back to evaluate execution.

Dr. Alaa's in-class examples for Ch.1b

  • Marketing manager evolution — implementer → strategy maker [Lec 2 @ 42:17-42:31]
  • The compound real-estate opportunity in Egypt (Emaar, SODIC, Mountain View) [Lec 1 @ 41:30]
  • BYD pivot — Chinese car maker to masks during COVID [Lec 2 @ 1:48:21]
  • Crocs vs Skechers — sustainable advantage [Lec 1 @ 43:43]
  • Apple AirPods — making wireless the default [Lec 1 @ 2:23:00]
  • Toyota = transportation solutions (not cars) [Lec 1 @ 46:29]
  • Microsoft = solving problems (not selling software) — origin of "solutions" [Lec 2 @ 48:25]
  • IKEA = home dress (everything for the home) [Lec 1 @ 54:01]
  • The Downy launch story — Market Penetration via bundling [Lec 2 @ 1:53:30]
  • PepsiCo + KFC + Pizza Hut + Costa — Diversification by acquisition [Lec 3-2 @ 31:28]

Common confusions for Ch.1b

Concept A Concept B Key difference
Strength Distinctive Competency Every distinctive competency is a strength, but most strengths are not distinctive (they're shared by competitors). Distinctive = competitors can't easily replicate.
Distinctive Competency Success Requirement Competency = what we do best (internal). Success Requirement = what the market demands (external). Opportunity becomes real only when they match.
Strength Opportunity Strength is internal; Opportunity is external. Both are favourable.
Mission Business Definition Business Definition answers "what business are we in?" Mission is the formal public expression of that definition.
Marketing goals Financial goals Marketing goals: market share, sales volume, customer satisfaction, customer value. Financial goals: ROI, ROS, profit, cash flow, shareholder wealth.
Operating Budget Financial Budget Operating = pro-forma income statement (revenues / costs / profit). Financial = cash-position impact.
Market Penetration Market Development Penetration: same market, more share of existing product. Development: new market for existing product.
Product Development Diversification Product Development: new product for existing market. Diversification: both new product AND new market.
Marketing audit "right things" Marketing audit "things right" Right things = strategy question (markets, customers, value props). Things right = execution question (budgets, processes, efficiency).

Why this matters for the final

  • Per CALIBRATION.md, Ch.1b was ~30%+ of the actual midterm — SWOT, Distinctive Competency, Marketing goals, Ansoff Matrix all heavily tested. Expect 15-20% on the final.
  • High-confidence retest items (all hit verbatim on midterm — recycle for final):
  • T/F: "What the organization is good at doing or a characteristic giving it important capability called Strength" (TRUE) [Slide: Kerin Ch.1 s.20]
  • T/F: "Distinctive Competency describes an organization's unique strengths or qualities" (TRUE) [Slide: Kerin Ch.1 s.18]
  • T/F: "Marketing mix = production / price / promotion / packaging" (FALSE — it's Product / Price / Place / Promotion) [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.4]
  • Essay: "What are the marketing goals?" — Production / Financial / Marketing goals categories [Slide: Kerin Ch.1 s.13-14]
  • Essay: "What are the sources of environmental opportunities?" — unmet needs / unsatisfied buyer groups / new means or technology [Slide: Strategic Marketing Pilot s.16]
  • MCQ: "four steps, in order, to designing a customer-driven marketing strategy" (Segmentation → Targeting → Differentiation → Positioning) — this also surfaces in Ch.7 below.
  • High-yield short-answer prompts to memorise:
  • The 5 processes of strategic marketing management — deck-verbatim: (1) Defining the organization's business, mission, and goals · (2) Identifying and framing organizational growth opportunities · (3) Formulating product-market strategies · (4) Budgeting marketing, financial, and production resources · (5) Developing reformulation and recovery strategies
  • The 8 reasons on slide 2 "Why studying Strategic Marketing Management?" (Market structure / Competition / Cultural / Micro&Macro / Tech / Marginal Propensity / Buyer behaviour / Heterogeneity)
  • The 4 levels of strategic market sizing (Market Potential / Industry Sales / Company Sales / Unrealized Potential)
  • The 4 benefits of a mission statement (Crystallizes vision / Provides guidance / Inspires employees / Provides direction for goals)
  • The 3 diagnostic questions after SWOT (Which strengths are distinctive? Which weaknesses disqualify us? Does a pattern emerge?)
  • The 4 cells of Ansoff (Penetration / Market Development / Product Development / Diversification)
  • The 2 questions the Marketing Audit answers (Right things / Things right)
