Chapter 7 — Segmentation, Targeting, Positioning (Kotler Ch.7)

Source deck: Kotler Ch.7 slides 1-33. Source lectures: Lec 3-1, Lec 3-2. All claims below cite [Slide ...] or [Lec N @ mm:ss].

Dr. Alaa called this "the central most important topic" of the course [Lec 3-1 @ 15:26] — proven on midterm (35% of all questions) and likely the heaviest topic on the final's STP question.

💡 In Plain English — Chapter 7, told as one story

Same BrewHaus running example. Ch.1a gave us the marketing philosophy. Ch.1b gave us the strategic cycle. Now Ch.7 answers the most important question of all: which customer does BrewHaus serve, and how do we make them choose us? Dr. Alaa calls this the "central most important topic" of the course — it's also the bridge that connects strategy (Ch.1b) to the 4P tactics (Ch.8-14).

1. The big idea — you CAN'T sell to everyone

If BrewHaus tries to serve everyone (kids, retirees, students, businessmen, tourists, families) it will please nobody. Why? Each group wants different things — kids want sweet, businessmen want fast, retirees want quiet, students want cheap WiFi. A single shop can't be all of them. The winning move = pick a specific group, serve them perfectly, and let competitors fight over the rest.

2. The 4-step engine: S → T → D → P

STP-D (or STDP) is a 4-step pipeline. The ORDER matters — skip a step and the next breaks:

  1. Segmentation — slice the market into groups.
  2. Targeting — pick the group(s) worth pursuing.
  3. Differentiation — make your offer visibly DIFFERENT for that group.
  4. Positioning — plant that difference in the customer's MIND so they remember and prefer you.

3. Step 1 — Segmentation: how to slice the market

You can slice the same coffee market in 4 different ways (or combine them):

  • Geographic — by location. (Downtown Alexandria vs. Stanley vs. Borg El Arab.)
  • Demographic — by age, gender, income, occupation, education, family size. (Young professionals 25-35 with disposable income.) Most common slice because data is easy.
  • Psychographic — by lifestyle, personality, values. (Health-conscious; eco-conscious; status-seekers.)
  • Behavioral — by how they USE the product. Usage occasion (morning rush vs. weekend leisure), benefits sought (energy vs. socializing), loyalty status (new vs. regular), usage rate (light vs. heavy).

For a segment to be USEFUL, it must be: M-A-D-A-S:

  • Measurable — you can count them.
  • Accessible — you can REACH them with ads/distribution.
  • Differentiable — they respond differently from other groups.
  • Actionable — you can actually DESIGN a program for them.
  • Substantial — big enough to be profitable.

Slicing "left-handed coffee drinkers who like classical music" might be differentiable, but it fails Substantial — too small to bother.

4. Step 2 — Targeting: pick which segment(s) to serve

Now BrewHaus has 5 possible segments on the table. Which to chase? Four targeting STRATEGIES:

  1. Undifferentiated (mass marketing) — ignore segments, offer ONE thing to EVERYONE. (Old Coca-Cola: "one Coke fits all.") BrewHaus version: one menu, one price, one vibe for everybody. Rare today.
  2. Differentiated (segmented) — design different offers for different segments. BrewHaus version: "Quick-service counter for office workers" + "lounge area for students" + "kids' corner for families." More cost, more sales.
  3. Concentrated (niche) — pick ONE small segment and dominate it. BrewHaus version: specialty pour-over only for connoisseurs. Small market but no Starbucks competition.
  4. Micromarketing — tailor to individuals or local groups. Two flavors: Local marketing (different menu per neighborhood) and Individual marketing (the Starbucks barista who writes your name + remembers your "usual").

5. Step 3 — Differentiation: make your offer ACTUALLY different

It's not enough to say "we're different" — your offer must be physically different in ways the target customer values. Five places to differentiate:

  • Product — better beans, signature drinks. (BrewHaus uses single-origin Ethiopian beans.)
  • Services — free WiFi, faster service, loyalty perks. (BrewHaus delivers in 15 min city-wide.)
  • Channels — where customers can find you. (BrewHaus on Talabat AND in-shop AND at office vending machines.)
  • People — friendlier staff, better-trained baristas. (BrewHaus baristas are SCA-certified.)
  • Image / Brand — the personality customers feel. (BrewHaus = "premium but warm," not "corporate cold.")

6. Step 4 — Positioning: plant the difference in the customer's MIND

Differentiation is what you DO; positioning is what the customer THINKS. Same thing? No — you can do something differently but if customer doesn't perceive it, it doesn't count. Three concepts:

  • Positioning = the place your brand occupies in the customer's mind relative to competitors. ("BrewHaus = the premium-but-cozy Alex coffee shop. Starbucks = corporate. Costa = mid-range. Local kahwa = cheap.")
  • Positioning statement — a one-sentence formula:
    "To [target segment], BrewHaus is the [category] that [unique benefit] because [reason to believe]."
    Example: "To young Alex professionals, BrewHaus is the premium coffee shop that delivers in 15 minutes because we own our own delivery fleet."
  • Value proposition — the full bundle of benefits the brand promises. Picking among: more-for-more, more-for-the-same, the-same-for-less, less-for-much-less, more-for-less. Each is a viable positioning. "More-for-less" is the holy grail but rare; "more-for-more" (premium) is what most differentiated brands chase.

7. The "Perceptual map" — how to visualize positioning

Draw a 2D map with two axes that matter to customers (e.g., X = price, Y = quality). Plot every competitor as a dot. Empty spots on the map = opportunities. BrewHaus example: if all 5 competitors cluster at "low price + low quality," the open space is "premium quality at fair price" — go there.

How chapter 7 connects together:

You can't serve everyone → so you SEGMENT the market into groups (geographic/demographic/psychographic/behavioral) → check segments pass M-A-D-A-S filter → TARGET one or more with one of 4 strategies (undifferentiated / differentiated / concentrated / micromarketing) → DIFFERENTIATE your offer along Product/Service/Channel/People/Image → POSITION the difference in customer's mind via a positioning statement and value proposition → use the perceptual map to find empty space. STP is the bridge: it takes the strategic vision from Ch.1b and tells you exactly WHICH customer to design every P (Ch.8 product, Ch.10 price, Ch.11 promotion, Ch.14 place) around.

In one sentence

STP is the four-step customer-driven marketing-strategy engine: Segment the market into groups, Target the ones worth pursuing, Differentiate your offering, and Position it in the customer's mind. [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.2]

In plain language

You almost never sell to "everyone." Even Coca-Cola — the most universal product on Earth — runs different ads for different audiences and offers different sizes, flavours and packaging by segment. The STP engine forces you to (1) see the market as a collection of distinct groups rather than one undifferentiated mass (Segmentation), (2) choose which groups you can serve profitably (Targeting), (3) change your offer so it visibly fits the chosen group better than competitors (Differentiation), and (4) plant that difference in the customer's mind so they remember and prefer you (Positioning).

The order matters: S → T → D → P. Skip a step and the next one breaks. Skip Segmentation and you can't Target. Skip Differentiation and your Positioning is empty.

Core concepts

3.0 Four core definitions (deck-verbatim — quote these on the exam)

[Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.3–4]

  • Market segmentation — "is the process that companies use to divide large heterogeneous markets into small markets that can be reached more efficiently and effectively with products and services that match their unique needs."
  • Market targeting — "(targeting) is the process of evaluating each market segment's attractiveness and selecting one or more segments to enter."
  • Differentiation — "involves actually differentiating the market offering to create superior customer value."
  • Positioning — "consists of arranging for a market offering to occupy a clear, distinctive, and desirable place relative to competing products in the minds of target consumers."

Use to understand (not to quote on exam): Segmentation = carve the market into slices. Targeting = pick which slices to pursue. Differentiation = change your offer to fit the chosen slice better than rivals. Positioning = make that difference live in the customer's mind.

Figure 7.1 — Designing a Customer-Driven Marketing Strategy [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.5]: Two-stage flow — Select customers to serve (Segmentation → Targeting) feeds into a red centre box "Create value for targeted customers", which connects to Decide on a value proposition (Differentiation → Positioning). The four steps in order are: Segmentation → Targeting → Differentiation → Positioning.

3.1 Consumer-market segmentation — the 4 bases

Scope: this section covers consumer-market (B2C) segmentation only, matching the slide-deck banner "Segmenting Consumer Markets" (slides 7–12). B2B segmentation is §3.3, international is §3.4, inter-market is §3.5.

Base How you slice Classic example
Geographic Deck-verbatim [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.8]: "divides the market into different geographical units such as nations, regions, provinces, parishes, cities, or even neighborhoods." Toyota builds stronger air-conditioning into Gulf cars; right-hand-drive for the UK. [Lec 3-1 @ 56:44]
Demographic Deck-verbatim [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.9]: "divides the market into groups based on variables such as age, gender, family size, family life cycle, income, occupation, education, religion, race, generation, and nationality." Sub-types from s.10: Age and life-cycle = "offering different products or using different marketing approaches for different age and life-cycle groups." Gender = "divides the market based on sex (male or female)." Income = "divides the market into affluent or low-income consumers." Different clothes, cars, perfumes, foods, even medicines for different ages [Lec 3-1 @ 1:00:32]. Family life-cycle: single / married-no-kids / married-young-kids / empty-nest / retired — each has its own products.
Psychographic Deck-verbatim [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.11]: "divides buyers into different groups based on social class, lifestyle, or personality characteristics." A Rolex buyer signals a lifestyle, not just a time-reading need.
Behavioural Deck-verbatim [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.12]: "divides buyers into groups based on their knowledge, attitudes, uses, or responses to a product." Variables: occasions, benefits sought, user status, usage rate, loyalty status. Signal toothpaste split into 3 variants: sensitive-teeth segment, whitening-for-smokers segment, kids segment [Lec 3-1 @ 1:11:00].

3.2 Using multiple segmentation bases together

Slide 13's exact wording: "Multiple segmentation is used to identify smaller, better-defined target groups." [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.13]

Plain language: the more bases you combine, the smaller and sharper the segment becomes. Geographic + Demographic + Behavioural = "30–45-year-old women living in upper-Cairo, single-mum life-cycle stage, regular users of organic baby food, willing to pay premium prices." That's a far more actionable segment than just "women" or just "upper-Cairo residents." Marketers call this multivariate segmentation or geodemographic segmentation when geography + demographics combine. The trade-off: each extra base shrinks the segment size, so you have to balance precision against substantiality (one of the 5 effective-segmentation requirements below).

3.3 Segmenting business markets (B2B)

The four bases above (geographic / demographic / psychographic / behavioural) slice an ordinary consumer market. When the customer is another company — a refinery buying inhibitors, a hospital buying lab kits, a fleet manager buying tyres — you still segment, but with a different set of variables. Slide 14 lists ten B2B segmentation variables [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.14]:

Deck-verbatim list [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.14]:

  1. Geographic
  2. Demographic
  3. Benefits Sought
  4. User Status
  5. Usage Rate
  6. Loyalty Status
  7. Operating Characteristics
  8. Purchasing Approaches
  9. Situational Factors
  10. Personal Characteristics

Note: the first six mirror the consumer-market variables (geographic → loyalty status); the last four are unique to B2B buying situations. The table below explains the four B2B-specific ones in depth — use it to understand; quote the full list of ten on the exam.

Deep-dive on the B2B-specific four [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.14]:

Variable What it really means How it changes your sales motion
Operating characteristics The technology and equipment the customer already runs (cloud-native vs. legacy on-prem; modern PLCs vs. old relays; SAP-enabled vs. paper-based). You can't sell an SAP cloud rollout the same way to a customer still on paper-based ERP. Different pitch, different price, different integration plan.
Purchasing Approaches How procurement is organised. Centralised = one HQ purchasing department buys for all branches. Decentralised = each branch buys for itself. Centralised rewards a single key-account manager and big bundled deals. Decentralised rewards a wide field force selling branch-by-branch.
Situational factors The buying situation: urgency, order size, application. Routine planned vs urgent unplanned. Urgent buyers tolerate a price premium for fast delivery; planned buyers will run a tender for the best unit price.
Personal characteristics The personalities of the people on the buying centre — technical engineer, finance, end user, gatekeeper, final approver. Some are loyal and risk-averse, some open to switching, some buy on relationship and some on price. A long-tenured conservative engineer wants continuity; a new dynamic procurement officer wants innovation and savings. Same company, totally different sale.

3.4 Segmenting international markets

When you slice across countries rather than within one country, the variables shift again. Slide 15 names four factors [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.15]:

Deck-verbatim list [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.15]:

  1. Geographic location
  2. Economic factors
  3. Political-legal factors
  4. Cultural factors

Use to understand (not to quote on exam):

  1. Geographic location — which region, continent, or climate zone the country sits in. Proximity shapes logistics costs, trade blocs, and consumer behaviour. Middle East, North Africa, Sub-Saharan Africa, South-East Asia each have distinct market profiles.
  2. Economic factors — the strength and structure of each country's economy (developed vs. developing, GDP per capita, currency stability, household disposable income). Premium cars fit high-income countries; sachet detergent fits low-income markets.
  3. Political-legal factors — political stability, regulatory environment, tax regimes, import/export rules, IP enforcement. A country with unstable politics or sudden tariff swings is a riskier segment even if its economics look attractive.
  4. Cultural factors — language, religion, social norms, values. Dr. Alaa highlighted religion most — what's halal/haram, what may be advertised, dress codes, dietary restrictions, gender-mix in public spaces. Beef burgers don't sell in India; pork doesn't sell in the Gulf; alcohol advertising is illegal in Saudi Arabia.

3.5 Inter-market segmentation

Easy to confuse with international segmentation, but it's the opposite move. Slide 16's exact wording: "Inter-market segmentation divides consumers into groups with similar needs and buying behaviors even though they are located in different countries." [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.16]

Plain language: instead of treating "Egypt" and "Germany" as two separate markets and tailoring to each, you find the same kind of customer in both countries and treat them as one global segment. Classic example: gamers. A 20-year-old PlayStation gamer in Cairo wants the same console, the same games, the same headset as a 20-year-old gamer in Berlin or Tokyo. Sony can sell the same product to all of them with the same global marketing because the segment travels across borders.

Other classic inter-market segments: luxury-watch buyers (a Rolex buyer in Dubai and a Rolex buyer in New York behave the same way), Apple iPhone power-users, Marlboro smokers, business-class air travellers. Lexus deliberately targets wealthy consumers in many countries — that's an inter-market play.

Quick distinction to memorise: - International segmentation = country-by-country. Each country = one segment with its own economic/political/cultural profile. You tailor to each country. - Inter-market segmentation = customer-type across countries. One segment, many countries. You treat the cross-border tribe as one market.

3.5b Requirements for effective segmentation

Deck-verbatim intro [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.17]: "To be useful, market segments must be:"

  • Measurable
  • Accessible
  • Substantial
  • Differentiable
  • Actionable

Use to understand (not to quote on exam):

  1. Measurable — you can size the segment (how many buyers, how much they spend).
  2. Accessible — you can reach them via a channel (media, sales force, distributor).
  3. Substantial — the segment is large enough to be profitable.
  4. Differentiable — the segments respond differently to different marketing mixes; if two segments respond identically, they're really one segment.
  5. Actionable — you can design an effective programme to attract and serve the segment.

3.6 What is a "target market"?

Once you've sliced the market into segments, you don't serve every segment — you pick the ones you'll actually go after. Slide 18's exact wording: "A target market consists of a set of buyers who share common needs or characteristics that the company decides to serve." [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.18]

Three things to notice in that definition: - "A set of buyers" — not the whole market, just a chosen group. - "Share common needs or characteristics" — the segment must be cohesive; what unites them must be more important than what divides them. - "The company decides to serve" — targeting is an active choice. You're saying yes to one segment and no to others, and the no's are just as important.

3.7 Evaluating market segments — the 3 criteria

Before you pick which segment(s) to chase, you have to evaluate each one. Slide 19 lists three criteria — every segment must be judged against all three [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.19]:

  1. Segment size and growth — is the segment big enough today, and is it growing? A small but fast-growing segment can be more attractive than a large but stagnant one.
  2. Segment structural attractiveness — how brutal is competition inside the segment? Borrow Porter's logic: are there many strong rivals? Cheap substitutes? Easy entry? A segment that looks big and growing can still be unattractive if a price war is dragging margins to zero.
  3. Company objectives and resources — does this segment fit your strategy and your capability? Only pursue segments where your distinctive competency matches the segment's success requirements (the same logic from Ch.1b §2.3).

A segment is "go" only when the answer to all three criteria is favourable.

3.8 Targeting — the 4 strategies

Figure 7.2 — Marketing Targeting Strategies [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.20]: A left-to-right spectrum with Targeting broadly on the left and Targeting narrowly on the right. Order: Undifferentiated (mass) → Differentiated (segmented) → Concentrated (niche) → Micromarketing (local or individual).

Deck-verbatim definitions [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.21–24] — quote these on the exam:

  • Undifferentiated marketing — "targets the whole market with one offer. Mass marketing. Focuses on common needs rather than what's different."
  • Differentiated marketing — "targets several different market segments and designs separate offers for each. Goal is to achieve higher sales and a stronger position. More expensive than undifferentiated marketing."
  • Concentrated marketing — "targets a large share of one or a few smaller segments or niches. Limited company resources. Knowledge of the market. More effective and efficient."
  • Micromarketing — "is the practice of tailoring products and marketing programs to suit the tastes of specific individuals and locations. Local marketing. Individual marketing."

Sub-types of Micromarketing (s.25–26):

  • Local marketing [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.25] — "involves tailoring brands and promotion to the needs and wants of local customer groups. Cities. Neighborhoods. Stores."
  • Individual marketing [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.26] — "involves tailoring products and marketing programs to the needs and preferences of individual customers. Also known as: One-to-one marketing. Customized marketing or mass customization. Markets-of-one marketing."

Use to understand (not to quote on exam) — strategy table:

Strategy Logic Example Dr. Alaa used
Undifferentiated (mass) One offer for the whole market. Works when the product has low variability. Water, Pepsi, plain biscuits [Lec 3-2 @ 36:04].
Differentiated (segmented) Several segments, each with its own tailored offer. Higher sales but costlier (more product lines + more marketing). Kia Picanto / Cerato / Sportage / Carnival / Sorento; Samsung phones from 5k to 80k EGP [Lec 3-2 @ 12:30].
Concentrated (niche) Dominate one or a few small segments. Driven by limited resources, deep market knowledge, efficiency. Apple's original iPhone (one model, premium segment); diet sweets for dieters + diabetics; hijab-only clothing stores; disability equipment shops [Lec 3-2 @ 15:23-19:32].
Micromarketing (local or individual) Tailor to a specific city / neighbourhood / store (local) or even to one customer (individual = one-to-one = mass customisation = markets-of-one). Custom-painted bride's wedding car; custom software for one client [Lec 3-2 @ 20:22].

3.9 Choosing your targeting strategy — the 5 factors

Deck-verbatim [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.27]: "Depends on:" followed by five factors:

  • Company resources
  • Product variability
  • Product life-cycle stage
  • Market variability
  • Competitor's marketing strategies

Use to understand (not to quote on exam): [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.27 ; Lec 3-2 @ 35:06]

  1. Company resources — small / cash-constrained → concentrated (niche). Large firms with deep pockets can afford differentiated or undifferentiated. Dr. Alaa: "limited company resource" [Lec 3-2 @ 18:11].
  2. Product variability — commodity-like products (water, sugar, plain biscuits) are uniform → undifferentiated. Highly variable products (phones, cars, cosmetics, fashion) → differentiated.
  3. Product life-cycle stage — at introduction, undifferentiated single offer is best (one message, build category awareness). As the market matures, the firm shifts to differentiated.
  4. Market variability — if every buyer wants roughly the same thing → undifferentiated. If buyers have visibly different tastes → only differentiated or concentrated satisfies them. Dr. Alaa: "market variability يعني درجة الاختلافات في السوق" [Lec 3-2 @ 36:16].
  5. Competitor marketing strategies — if every major competitor is running differentiated with multiple variants, you usually have to match or you'll look impoverished and lose share. [Lec 3-2 @ 37:34]

3.10 Socially responsible target marketing

Deck-verbatim [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.28]: "Concern for vulnerable segments (Children) with controversial or potentially harmful products. Cigarettes. Internet abuses."

Use to understand (not to quote on exam): The last sub-topic of targeting asks an ethical question: just because a firm can target a segment, does that mean it should? [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.28 ; Lec 3-2 @ 37:36]

Some segments are vulnerable — they need protection from aggressive or harmful targeting. The two examples on the slide:

  • Children with potentially harmful or controversial products — cigarettes targeted at minors, sugary cereals with cartoon mascots, junk food advertised during kids' programming. Dr. Alaa told the Nike-children-labour story [Lec 3-2 @ 38:08-38:30] — a brand celebrating its global success made a film that accidentally exposed children working in their supply chain, and the brand was hit hard for child-labour abuse.
  • Internet abuses — predatory online targeting, addictive games, dark-pattern paid loops, exploiting psychological weaknesses of teenagers, mining personal data without consent.

Other vulnerable segments often raised in the same discussion: addicts (alcohol, gambling, junk food), the elderly (financial scams, miracle-cure products), people in financial distress (predatory lending). The principle: a marketer is socially responsible not when they refuse to target these groups, but when they protect them from products that would damage their long-term welfare. This is the Societal Marketing orientation from Ch.1a §1.2 in action.

3.11 Differentiation & Positioning

Differentiation and positioning are two halves of the same job: differentiation is the real-world change you make to the offer; positioning is where that change lives in the customer's mind. Slides 29–33 unpack this in five linked pieces — covered in §3.11a → §3.11e below.

3.11a Product position — definition [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.29]

Deck-verbatim: "Product position is the way the product is defined by consumers on important attributes—the place the product occupies in consumers' minds relative to competing products."

3.11b Competitive advantage — definition [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.31]

Deck-verbatim: "Competitive advantage is an advantage over competitors gained by offering consumers greater value, either through lower prices or by providing more benefits that justify higher prices."

3.11c Choosing a D&P strategy — the 3-step build process [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.30]

Deck-verbatim — 3-step process heading: "Choosing a Differentiation and Positioning Strategy" consists of:

  1. Identifying a set of possible competitive advantages to build a position
  2. Choosing the right competitive advantages
  3. Selecting an overall positioning strategy

Use to understand (not to quote on exam):

  1. Identify a set of possible competitive advantages. List every single way your offer could be different and better — across product, services, channels, people, image. Cast a wide net first; you'll narrow later.
  2. Choose the right competitive advantages. From that long list, pick the one (or two) that actually matter to the customer, that you can deliver consistently, and that competitors cannot easily copy.
  3. Select an overall positioning strategy. Decide the value proposition (more benefit at higher price, or greater benefit at the same/lower price), then write the positioning statement.

3.11d Step 1 — the 5 bases of differentiation [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.32]

Deck-verbatim: "Identifying a set of possible competitive advantages to build a position by providing superior value from:"

  • Product differentiation
  • Service differentiation
  • Channel differentiation
  • People differentiation
  • Image differentiation

Use to understand (not to quote on exam):

  • Product differentiation — features, performance, style, durability (Sharp AC's air-purifying tech).
  • Service differentiation — installation, after-sales, warranty. Classic example: Apple's 2010 iPhone-migration-from-Nokia service — they provided the cables and a free in-store device that moved your Nokia contacts/photos/emails onto your new iPhone. [Lec 3-2 @ 50:30]
  • Channel differentiation — store experience, digital integration, distribution reach.
  • People differentiation — staff quality. The attentive waiter vs. the distracted waiter — same restaurant, very different experience [Lec 3-2 @ 51:41].
  • Image differentiation — brand perception, symbols, logos, heritage. Rolex sells image as much as it sells timekeeping.

Step 2 — what makes a "right" advantage: - Important — the customer actually values it. - Distinctive — competitors don't already deliver it. - Communicable — you can explain it in one sentence. - Affordable — the customer can pay for the value it adds. - Profitable — you can deliver it at a margin. - Sustainable — competitors can't easily copy it. Today's advantage can be copied (Skechers copied Crocs; Huawei copied Apple).

Step 3 — value proposition phrasing Dr. Alaa emphasised verbatim: - "Greater value at the same or lower price." — more benefit, same cost. - "Providing more benefit that justifies a higher price." — premium positioning.

Positioning is the mental place. Positioning is the place your brand occupies in the customer's mind relative to competitors. It is not about your product; it is about their perception. Dr. Alaa's phrasing: "ضع نفسك في مكانة ذهنية مميزة" — "put yourself in a distinctive mental place" [Lec 3-1 @ 31:44].

Slide 29 unpacks "the customer's mind" into three specific elements [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.29]: - Perceptions — what the customer thinks about the brand on important attributes (quality, price, reliability). E.g., "BMW = engineered to drive." - Impressions — what the customer remembers from past exposure (ads, packaging, store visits, word of mouth). E.g., an Apple Store as bright, minimal, premium. - Feelings — the emotional response the brand triggers (pride, comfort, excitement, trust, fear). E.g., wearing a Rolex = pride; flying first class = status; using Amazon = convenience.

A purely rational positioning (perceptions only — "we have the lowest price") is fragile; the strongest positioning combines all three (perceptions + impressions + feelings).

Positioning slogans Dr. Alaa cited: - OPPO = "Camera Phone" (camera as the hook) [Lec 3-2 @ 53:45] - Nokia = "Connecting People" [Lec 3-2 @ 53:55] - Toyota in the Gulf = best air-conditioning + durability + fast spare parts [Lec 3-1 @ 56:44] - Ford and Mazda in Egypt — failed to establish a distinctive positioning, which is why they lost share.

3.11e The positioning statement template [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.33]

Memorise this template verbatim — it's exam-quotable:

"To (target segment and need), our (brand) is (concept) that (point of difference)."

Worked example using OPPO: "To young photographers who want a phone that is also their main camera, our OPPO is the camera-phone that captures DSLR-quality images in a pocket-sized device."

Dr. Alaa's in-class examples for Ch.7

  • Toyota in the Gulf — geographic segmentation by climate [Lec 3-1 @ 56:44]
  • Family life-cycle products — different products by stage [Lec 3-1 @ 1:00:32]
  • Signal toothpaste 3 variants — behavioural segmentation [Lec 3-1 @ 1:11:00]
  • LADA — one car for everyone = undifferentiated (NOT concentrated!) [Lec 3-2 @ 36:04]
  • LC Waikiki — middle-class Egyptian targeting [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.18]
  • Nido Nestle — different age groups = demographic (NOT behavioural!) — exam trap
  • Kia / Samsung phones — differentiated targeting [Lec 3-2 @ 12:30]
  • Apple's original iPhone — concentrated (premium segment only)
  • Diet sweets / hijab-only / disability shops — concentrated [Lec 3-2 @ 15:23-19:32]
  • Bride's wedding car — individual marketing / one-to-one [Lec 3-2 @ 20:22]
  • Nike children-labour story — socially responsible targeting cautionary tale [Lec 3-2 @ 38:08-38:30]
  • Apple iPhone-migration service from Nokia — service differentiation [Lec 3-2 @ 50:30]
  • Attentive vs distracted waiter — people differentiation [Lec 3-2 @ 51:41]
  • OPPO Camera Phone / Nokia Connecting People — positioning slogans [Lec 3-2 @ 53:45, 53:55]
  • Zamalek fans — loyal but dissatisfied (satisfaction ≠ loyalty) [Lec 3-1 @ 1:13:51]

Common confusions for Ch.7

Concept A Concept B Key difference
Segment Target market Segment = a group with similar needs identified by the marketer. Target market = the segment the company chose to serve.
Segment Market Segment = subset of the market with similar buying behaviour. Market = all potential buyers. (See MCQ #8 partial-miss correction at bottom — answer was "A and C": both segment AND market.)
Undifferentiated Concentrated Undifferentiated = same offer for the whole market (LADA). Concentrated = focus on one narrow segment (hijab-only). Exam trap — "LADA one car for everyone" was incorrectly labelled "concentrated" on midterm.
Concentrated Micromarketing Concentrated targets a few small segments. Micromarketing targets individual customers or specific local cells.
Differentiation Positioning Differentiation = what you actually change in the offer. Positioning = where that change lives in the customer's mind.
Satisfaction Loyalty Satisfaction = happy with this transaction. Loyalty = sticking around even when unsatisfied (Zamalek fans).
International segmentation Inter-market segmentation International = country-by-country. Inter-market = customer-type across countries (global gamers, Rolex buyers worldwide).
B2B segmentation variables B2C segmentation bases B2C: 4 bases (geographic / demographic / psychographic / behavioural). B2B: 10 variables total [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.14]: Geographic, Demographic, Benefits Sought, User Status, Usage Rate, Loyalty Status, Operating Characteristics, Purchasing Approaches, Situational Factors, Personal Characteristics.
Demographic segmentation Behavioural segmentation Demographic = age / gender / income (who they are). Behavioural = occasions / usage / loyalty (what they do). Nido kids by age = demographic, not behavioural.
Targeting Differentiation Targeting = choosing which segment. Differentiation = changing what you offer.
Positioning (perceptions) Positioning (impressions) Perceptions = current thinking. Impressions = remembered past exposure. Both feed positioning.

  • High-confidence retest items (all hit verbatim on midterm — recycle for final):

  • T/F: "LC Waikiki middle-class Egyptian clothing — example of targeting" (TRUE)
  • T/F: "Nido Nestle products for children at different age groups — example of behavioral segmentation" (FALSE — it's demographic)
  • T/F: "LADA offered one car for everyone — example of concentrated marketing" (FALSE — it's undifferentiated)
  • T/F: "Business and consumer marketers use the same set of variables to segment" (FALSE)
  • Calibration partial-miss to fix on final: MCQ on "buyers sharing a need...exchange relationships" = "A and C" (segment AND market) — added at bottom of this file.
  • Likely short-answer prompts:

  • List the 5 requirements for effective segmentation (Measurable / Accessible / Substantial / Differentiable / Actionable)
  • List the 4 B2B segmentation variables
  • List the 4 international segmentation factors (Geographic location / Economic / Political-legal / Cultural) [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 s.15]

Chapter 8 — Product (Kotler Ch.8)

Source decks: 33 slides (Kotler Ch.8 "Products, Services, and Brands: Building Customer Value"). Source lectures: Lec 4 (parts 1 + 2 from source A; Lec_4_B from source B — Lec 4 covers Ch.8 Product first, then transitions to Ch.11 Promotion later in the same session). All claims below cite either [Slide N] or [Lec 4 @ mm:ss].

💡 In Plain English — Chapter 8 in one running story

Running example for the whole chapter: imagine you're opening a small coffee shop called BrewHaus. Every concept in Chapter 8 is a decision you'll have to make for this shop.

1. What is a "product"?

A product is anything you offer to customers that satisfies a need or want — it doesn't have to be physical. Your cappuccino is a product. So is the WiFi. So is the cozy atmosphere. Even an experience ("a quiet morning with great coffee") is a product.

Now look at your cappuccino in 3 layers:

  • Core value = the real benefit. Customers don't buy "coffee" — they buy energy + a 5-minute break + a small daily reward. That's what they're really paying for.
  • Actual product = the physical thing — the cup, the foam art, the temperature, the BrewHaus logo on the sleeve.
  • Augmented product = everything around the cup — the free WiFi, loyalty card, comfy chairs, friendly barista, clean bathroom.

Why this matters: cheap competitors fight on the actual product (cheaper coffee). Premium shops like Starbucks win on the augmented layer (the "third place" experience). You decide which layer you compete on.

2. Product classifications — who buys it and how?

Two big buckets:

  • Consumer products (sold to people for personal use). These split into 4 sub-types based on how the customer shops for them:
    1. Convenience — bought often, almost no thinking. (A bottle of water at your shop's counter.)
    2. Shopping — bought after comparing. (A new laptop. Customers compare 3 shops before buying.)
    3. Specialty — strong brand loyalty, willing to travel for it. (A Rolex. Or a die-hard fan who only drinks BrewHaus.)
    4. Unsought — customer doesn't even know they want it. (Life insurance. Funeral plans.)
  • Industrial products (sold to businesses, not end consumers). Example: the espresso machine you buy for BrewHaus is industrial; the espresso you sell to a customer is consumer.

3. Individual product decisions — the 5 knobs for ONE item

For each single product (say, your cappuccino), you turn 5 knobs:

  1. Attributes — quality (premium beans?), features (latte art? oat milk option?), style/design (how it looks).
  2. Branding — what name goes on it? "BrewHaus Signature Cappuccino" sells differently than "Cappuccino #3".
  3. Packaging — the cup design, the sleeve, the carry tray. Protects AND markets.
  4. Labeling — info printed on it: ingredients, calories, "Fair Trade," barcode.
  5. Support services — what comes WITH the cappuccino? Free refill? Free pastry on 10th visit? "Wrong order? Free remake."

4. Product line decisions — managing a GROUP of similar items

A product line = a family of related products. BrewHaus's "coffee line" might be: Espresso, Americano, Cappuccino, Latte, Mocha. Decisions:

  • Length — how many drinks in the coffee line? Too few = lose customers. Too many = staff confusion + slow service.
  • Stretch DOWN — add a cheaper option (basic drip coffee at 20 EGP for budget customers).
  • Stretch UP — add a premium option (single-origin pour-over at 80 EGP for connoisseurs).
  • Line filling — plug gaps inside the existing range (add a "Flat White" between Cappuccino and Latte).

5. Product mix decisions — managing ALL your lines together

Your product mix = every line you sell. BrewHaus might have: Coffee line + Tea line + Pastry line + Sandwich line + Branded mugs line. Four dimensions:

  • Width = how many different lines? (Currently 5.)
  • Length = total items across all lines. (Maybe 35 items total.)
  • Depth = how many versions of each item? (Cappuccino comes in small/medium/large × regular/oat/almond milk = 9 versions.)
  • Consistency = how related are the lines? (Coffee + tea + pastries = high consistency. If you added a clothing line, consistency drops.)

Quick climb: one cappuccino is a product. All your coffee drinks together = a line. All your lines together (coffee + tea + pastries + …) = the mix. You zoom out one level each time.

6. Branding — turning a product into a brand

A brand is a name/logo/promise that makes customers pick yours over an identical competitor's. Same coffee in a "BrewHaus" cup vs. a "no-name café" cup feels different — that difference is brand equity (the extra value the name adds).

Four ways to grow your brand:

  • Line extension — new flavor in the SAME line (BrewHaus adds "Pumpkin Spice Latte").
  • Brand extension — use the BrewHaus name on a NEW category (BrewHaus-branded coffee beans for home use).
  • Multibrands — launch a 2nd brand alongside the first (you open "QuickBean" — a budget sub-brand — so BrewHaus stays premium).
  • New brands — totally new name for a totally new category (you start "LeafHaus" tea bar with no BrewHaus connection).

7. Services marketing — why services are different from products

Your coffee is a product. But your barista's service is different in 4 ways (memorize: I-I-V-P):

  • Intangibility — customer can't touch "good service" before buying. They have to trust your reviews/atmosphere.
  • Inseparability — the service is produced and consumed at the same moment. The barista's mood when she made the latte IS part of the latte.
  • Variability — every cup tastes slightly different depending on who made it, when, and how busy the shop was.
  • Perishability — an empty seat at 3pm can't be "saved" for tomorrow's rush. Unused service capacity vanishes.

How chapter 8 connects together: Start with one cappuccino → decide its 3 levels and its 5 individual knobs → group it with other drinks into a coffee LINE → group all your lines into the full MIX → wrap the whole thing in a BRAND → and remember services need extra care because of I-I-V-P.

In one sentence

A product is anything offered to a market for attention, acquisition, use, or consumption that might satisfy a need or want — and modern marketers think of it in three layers (core value → actual product → augmented product), classify it by who buys it and how, and dress it up with branding, packaging, labeling, and after-sale support. [Slide 3]

In plain language

Kotler's chapter 8 walks you through four interlocking topic blocks [Slide 2]:

  1. What is a Product? — the formal definition, the three product levels (Figure 8.1), and the consumer- vs industrial-product classifications.
  2. Product and Service Decisions — the five individual decisions every marketer must make: product attributes (quality, features, style & design), branding, packaging, labeling, product support services; plus the line-level decisions (length, filling, stretching) and the mix-level decisions (width, length, depth, consistency).
  3. Branding Strategy: Building Strong Brands — what brand equity is, how to pick a brand name, the four brand-sponsorship options (manufacturer / private / licensed / co-brand), and the four brand-development paths (line extension / brand extension / multibrands / new brands).
  4. Services Marketing — the four characteristics that make services different from goods (Intangibility / Inseparability / Variability / Perishability) and the service-profit chain.

Dr. Alaa kept reminding the class that the "product decision" looks simple but is actually the hardest of the 4Ps. As he put it: "يعني موضوع المنتج بيبدو أنه سهل، لكنه هو قرار صعب، ومش سهل، فيه تفاصيل كتيرة" (the product topic looks easy but it's a hard decision with many details) [Lec 4 @ 1:14:47]. Every one of the items below is one of those details.

Whenever you can, anchor the abstract definitions to a regional brand you actually buy — Chipsy crisps, Nido baby milk, LC Waikiki, Coca-Cola, Pampers, Vodafone — because Dr. Alaa's exam questions repeatedly hang concepts on familiar Egyptian/MENA brand names (see CALIBRATION.md for evidence from the midterm).

Core concepts

1. What is a Product? — Products / Services / Experiences + Three Levels [Slides 3-4]

The book's exact definition: "Product is anything that can be offered in a market for attention, acquisition, use, or consumption that might satisfy a need or want." [Slide 3] A service is the intangible sibling: "any activity or benefit that one party can offer to another that is essentially intangible and does not result in the ownership of anything." [Slide 3] An experience — deck-verbatim from s.3 — "represents what buying the product or service will do for the customer" (e.g., a Disney holiday, an AppleCare visit) [Slide 3].

Figure 8.1 — Three Levels of Product [Slide 4]:

Level What it really is Example (BlackBerry, from slide)
Core customer value The fundamental benefit that solves the customer's problem. Buyers don't buy products — they buy benefits. "Freedom" and "on-the-go connectivity" — not the device itself.
Actual product The tangible thing the customer takes home. The slide lists: brand name, features, quality level, design, packaging. The physical BlackBerry handset (keyboard, screen, OS, branding). Textbook-canonical mapping — Slide 4 callout only specifies the core; Actual / Augmented illustrations below are not on the slide.
Augmented product All the extras around the actual product that add real value: delivery and credit, after-sale service, product support, warranty. Carrier plans, software updates, BlackBerry Enterprise Server, warranty. Textbook-canonical, not on Slide 4.

Dr. Alaa's framing: "الproduct or service... إيه كان نوع الproduct" — every marketing decision starts by asking what level you compete on. Cheap commodity producers compete on "actual product." Premium brands compete on "augmented product" [Lec 4 @ 1:07:35-1:07:37].

Two more anchor examples Dr. Alaa used in class (NotebookLM-captured) for the three levels:

  • MBA programme — "Business Language" as core value. For an MBA student, the core value isn't the certificate at all — it's a change in mindset and maturity; you're buying the business language that lets you understand how a corporation functions. The certificate is the actual product (the tangible thing you walk away with). The augmented product is the alumni network, career placement, and the lifelong upgrade of business frameworks you keep getting. ⚠ Source note: the MBA / career-change discussion IS in Whisper at [Lec_4_1 @ 22:17-23:44] (Arabic verbatim: "و في ناس يقولك انا بدرس MBA عشان اغير الكرير… ممكن تاخد كورسة و تنقل لكتب بس") and again at [Lec_4_B @ 1:56:34-1:57:45], but the specific "Business Language as Core Value" framing has no Whisper hit — it is a NotebookLM-only paraphrase, not Dr. Alaa-verbatim. Treat the MBA topic as lecture-anchored color; do not quote "Business Language as Core Value" as deck or lecture content on the exam.
  • Netflix — Value Innovation by redefining the product. Netflix didn't sell "content" — it redefined the product from a physical outing (cinema trip) to a convenience-based service. They increased the benefit of convenience while slashing the temporal cost (no travel, no parking, no waiting). This is the Value = Benefit / Cost equation applied to product design — a textbook Value Innovation that disrupted an entire industry by attacking the customer's hidden time cost. [Lec_4_2 @ 10:33, 10:43 — verbatim "Netflix"]

2. Product and Service Classifications

2-pre. Bridge from Ch.1 — Needs, Wants, Demand & market fragmentation [CROSS-REFERENCE — NOT ON Ch.8 DECK]

Scope note: This subsection is not on the Ch.8 product deck — it pulls the Needs / Wants / Demand framework from Ch.1 to set up the consumer-vs-industrial split that follows. Kept here purely as a pedagogical bridge. Do not cite this material as Ch.8 content on the exam; cite Ch.1 if needed. ⚠ Source note: the specific Needs / Wants / Demand framework is NotebookLM-captured; no verbatim Whisper anchor with these three labels was found in Lec_4_1/_2/_B. Treat as lecture-color background, not as Dr. Alaa-verbatim deck content.

  • Need — a biological imperative. Hunger. Thirst. Shelter. You can't market these into existence; they're hard-wired.
  • Want — a culturally or market-shaped form the need takes. "I'm hungry" is a need; "I want a crunchy chicken nugget" is a want.
  • Demand — a want backed by purchasing power. Wanting a Mercedes is a want; being able to pay for it turns it into demand.

The marketer's job is to create new wants out of old needs — and the tool for doing that is market fragmentation (slicing one generic product into many specialised niches). Dr. Alaa's three illustrations:

Generic need Old single product Modern fragmented market (each item = a new "want" the market created)
Hunger (chicken) A whole chicken Strips, wings, nuggets, crunchy boneless air-fryer pieces, breaded, marinated, kids-portion
Hunger (dairy) Basic Roumy / Turki cheese (رومي / تركي) White, light, medium, flavoured (pepper, olive, herbs), varying fat levels, single-serve, kids-shaped
Thirst (tea) Basic black tea Earl Grey, green, white, fruit-infusion, calming/energising blends, ice-tea, RTD bottled

Every cell on the right side of those rows is a new SKU (Stock Keeping Unit — the smallest, individually sellable variant of a product, with its own barcode and inventory count; e.g. Coke Zero 330 ml can and Coke Zero 500 ml PET bottle are two different SKUs) that a marketer convinced a consumer to buy. That's market fragmentation in action — and it's why the "consumer products" classification you're about to learn matters: each fragment can sit on a different point of the convenience↔specialty spectrum.

The first split [Slide 5] is by who buys it and why:

Deck-verbatim definitions — quote these on the exam:

  • Consumer products [Slide 6] — "are products and services for personal consumption. Classified by how consumers go about buying them."
  • Industrial products [Slide 11] — "are products purchased for further processing or for use in conducting a business. Classified by the purpose for which the product is purchased."

Dr. Alaa stressed the test: "اي حد بيشتري الgoods لغاية البيع، ده بيطلق عليه industrial" (anyone who buys goods in order to resell, that's called industrial) [Lec 4 @ 27:36-27:42]. So the same physical thing can be a consumer product or an industrial product depending on why the buyer bought it: a tire bought by you for your car = consumer; the same tire bought by a fleet operator = industrial.

2a. Four types of Consumer Products [Slide 6]

Consumer products are classified by how consumers go about buying them — i.e., by the buying effort.

Type Definition (textbook wording) Buying behaviour Dr. Alaa's Egyptian examples
Convenience [Slide 7] Deck-verbatim: "Convenience products are consumer products and services that the customer usually buys frequently, immediately, and with a minimum comparison and buying effort." Tiny effort. Nearest shop. Auto-pilot. "زي اللي احنا بتشتريها في السوبر ماركت: بسكويت شيبسي، مية، آيس كريم" — Chipsy biscuits, water, ice cream [Lec 4 @ 28:50-29:02]. Plus newspapers, candy, fast food [Slide 7].
Shopping [Slide 8] Deck-verbatim: "Shopping products are consumer products and services that the customer compares carefully on suitability, quality, price, and style." Multiple stores, online research, family input. "حضرتك هتبدأ تبزل مجهود أكبر شوية... هتنزل تلف، هتروح المولات" — you'll start to exert more effort, go down and walk around, go to the malls [Lec 4 @ 29:48-30:00]. Examples: التلاجة (fridge), الجهاز الكهربائي (appliances), furniture, cars [Slide 8].
Specialty [Slide 9] Deck-verbatim: "Specialty products are consumer products and services with unique characteristics or brand identification for which a significant group of buyers is willing to make a special purchase effort." Brand-loyal. Will travel. Won't substitute. Designer clothes, high-end electronics, medical services [Slide 9]. A Rolex buyer, an Apple iPhone purist. Dr. Alaa: "specialty product منتجات خاصة" [Lec 4 @ 28:16].
Unsought [Slide 10] Deck-verbatim: "Unsought products are consumer products that the consumer does not know about or knows about but does not normally think of buying." Pushed by salesperson, not pulled by consumer. Life insurance, funeral services, blood donations [Slide 10]. Dr. Alaa: "unsought product يعني منتجات لا يبحث عنها المستخدم" [Lec 4 @ 28:18].

Memorise the buying-effort spectrum: Convenience → Shopping → Specialty (increasing effort + brand attachment). Unsought sits outside this spectrum entirely.

2b. Three types of Industrial Products [Slide 11]

Industrial products are classified by the purpose for which they are purchased [Slide 11]:

Verbatim deck definitions — quote these on the exam [Slide 12 — verbatim]:

  1. Materials and parts — "include raw materials and manufactured materials and parts usually sold directly to industrial users."
  2. Capital items — "are industrial products that aid in the buyer's production or operations."
  3. Supplies and services — "include operating supplies, repair and maintenance items, and business services."

Textbook-canonical illustrations (not on Slide 12, included as memory aids): Materials and parts — steel coils to a car maker, flour to a bakery, microchips to Apple. Capital items — machinery, factory buildings, forklifts. Supplies and services — operating supplies like lubricants and stationery; business services like auditing, legal, and consulting.

2c. Organizations, Persons, Places, Ideas [Slides 13-14]

Kotler stretches "product" beyond physical goods and services to four softer categories that marketing still applies to [Slides 13-14].

Deck-verbatim definitions — quote these on the exam:

  • Organization marketing [Slide 13] — "consists of activities undertaken to create, maintain, or change attitudes and behavior of target consumers toward an organization."
  • Person marketing [Slide 13] — "consists of activities undertaken to create, maintain, or change attitudes and behavior of target consumers toward particular people."
  • Place marketing [Slide 14] — "consists of activities undertaken to create, maintain, or change attitudes and behavior of target consumers toward particular places."
  • Social marketing [Slide 14] — "is the use of commercial marketing concepts and tools in programs designed to influence individuals' behavior to improve their well-being and that of society."

Use to understand (not to quote on exam) — Egyptian/MENA examples:

  • Organization marketing — Red Crescent campaigns, AUC's brand image.
  • Person marketing — a politician's campaign, Mohamed Salah's personal brand, a CEO's media presence.
  • Place marketing — Egypt Tourism Authority's "Pyramids" campaign, Dubai's tourism push.
  • Social marketing — anti-smoking campaigns, "Khaleek bel Beit" COVID campaigns, breast-cancer awareness.

3. Individual Product Decisions — Figure 8.2 [Slide 15]

Every product manager works through this five-step chain [Slide 15]:

Product attributes → Branding → Packaging → Labeling → Product support services

3a. Product attributes [Slides 16-19]

Scope: "Product attributes" on slide 16 is the umbrella; slides 17 (Quality — level + conformance), 18 (Features) and 19 (Style & Design) are the four sub-slides that make it up. All four sub-attributes are unpacked below.

Deck-verbatim [Slide 16]: "Product attributes are the benefits of the product or service." Three sub-categories:

  • Quality [Slide 17] — Slide-17 umbrella verbatim: "Product quality includes level and consistency." Two dimensions:
  • Quality level — Slide 17 verbatim: "is the level of quality that supports the product's positioning." (You can't sell a Rolls-Royce on Lada-quality and you don't need Rolls quality to sell budget biscuits.)
  • Conformance quality — Slide 17 verbatim: "is the product's freedom from defects and consistency in delivering a targeted level of performance." (Whatever level you promised, you must hit every time. Note: slide says "a targeted level," not "the targeted level" — Dr. Alaa could test the article.)
  • Dr. Alaa's example: a single brand can be sold in three quality levels at three price points. The European importer asks for grade #1; the Egyptian importer asks for grade #4. Same factory, same brand mark, different specs. [Lec 4 @ 1:15:21-1:15:41].
  • Features [Slide 18] — Deck-verbatim: "Product features are a competitive tool for differentiating a product from competitors' products. Product features are assessed based on the value to the customer versus the cost to the company." (Don't add a feature whose cost exceeds the value the customer would pay for it.)
  • Style and design [Slide 19] — style describes appearance; design contributes to a product's usefulness as well as to its looks. Style = how it looks. Design = how it works and looks. (A car can have flashy style but bad design if doors won't open in winter.)

3b. Branding [Slide 20]

  • Brand — Slide 20 verbatim: "is the name, term, sign, or design—or a combination of these—that identifies the maker or seller of a product or service." [Slide 20]
  • Brand equity — appears on TWO slides with a one-word difference Dr. Alaa could test:
    • Slide 20 verbatim: "is the differential effect that the brand name has on customer response to the product and its marketing." [Slide 20 — verbatim]
    • Slide 25 verbatim: "is the differential effect that knowing the brand name has on customer response to the product and its marketing." [Slide 25 — verbatim]
    Slide 25 also adds: "It's a measure of the brand's ability to capture consumer preference and loyalty." [Slide 25 — verbatim] Two identical bottles of water with different labels — if customers reach for the Nestle Pure Life one and pay 50% more, that price-and-preference gap is the brand equity.

3c. Packaging [Slide 21]

Slide 21 verbatim: "Packaging involves designing and producing the container or wrapper for a product." [Slide 21 — verbatim] Packaging is a silent salesperson — it has to attract, describe, and protect.

Dr. Alaa's strong emphasis: "الpackaging ممكن يخليك تشتري الجودة" (packaging can make you buy the quality) [Lec 4 @ 1:12:45]. He named four categories where packaging dominates the purchase decision [Lec 4 @ 1:12:54-1:13:25]:

  • منتجات الأطفال (children's products — colourful, character-led)
  • Perfume / العطور (bottle shape and design carry most of the brand value)
  • Cosmetics — الروج والمسكرة والاستحضارات (lipstick, mascara, etc.)
  • Premium food / cosmetics

His takeaway: "شكل الغلاف والألوان اللي عليه بتعمل إغراءات شديدة جداً" (the wrapper shape and colours create strong temptations) [Lec 4 @ 1:13:01].

3d. Labeling [Slide 21]

"Labels identify the product or brand, describe attributes, and provide promotion." [Slide 21] Dr. Alaa: "الـliboling هو الكلام المكتوب. لازم الماركتير يقرر إيه الكلام اللي هيكتبه. فيه حاجات قانونية معينة لازم تتحط، زي المكونات، زي تاريخ الصلاحية" (labeling is the written text — the marketer decides what to write; legal mandatories include ingredients and expiry date) [Lec 4 @ 1:13:37-1:13:55]. Legal vs commercial content split is important — promotional claims (e.g., "no preservatives", "low calorie") are commercial; ingredients, weight, expiry date, country of origin are legal.

3e. Product support services [Slide 15]

After-sale support, warranty, installation, repair, training. Dr. Alaa's framing: "product support service — خدمات دعم المنتج بعد البيع" (after-sale product support services) [Lec 4 @ 1:14:33-1:14:39].

4. Product Line Decisions [Slides 22-23]

  • Product line = "a group of products that are closely related because they function in a similar manner, are sold to the same customer groups, are marketed through the same types of outlets, or fall within given price ranges." [Slide 22]
  • Product line length = the number of items in the line. [Slide 23]

Two ways to change line length [Slide 23]. Slide 23 verbatim definitions: Product line length = "the number of items in the product line." Product line filling = "occurs when companies add more items within the present range of the line." Product line stretching lists three sub-modes by name only on Slide 23 — Downward stretching / Upward stretching / Both directions. The sub-mode definitions below are textbook-canonical (not on Slide 23):

Move Definition Direction
Line filling Deck-verbatim [Slide 23]: "occurs when companies add more items within the present range of the line." Inward — fill gaps between existing items.
Line stretching — downward Extending the line toward the lower end (cheaper / lower quality) than its current range. Down-market.
Line stretching — upward Extending the line toward the higher end (more expensive / premium) than its current range. Up-market.
Line stretching — both directions Both ends simultaneously. Both.

Dr. Alaa introduced this concept right at the start of Lec 4: "عايز أعرف إزاي بعمل product line filling — يعني إيه filling — لما الشركة تقرر إن هي to add more items within the present range" [Lec 4 @ 00:26-00:46]. Then: "ممكن أعمل حاجة اسمها stretching — يعني تمديد الخط أو مد الخط. Downward stretching... upward stretching... both direction" [Lec 4 @ 01:02-01:42].

Classic Egyptian-market examples to anchor in your head: - Toyota filled its line by adding Camry Hybrid alongside regular Camry — same range, more items = line filling. - Mercedes launched the A-Class to reach younger / lower-income buyers — downward stretching. - Kia added the Sorento and Carnival above the Sportage to reach premium buyers — upward stretching. - A toothpaste brand adding Sensitive + Whitening + Kids variants at similar price points = line filling.

5. Product Mix Decisions [Slide 24]

  • Product mix — Slide 24 verbatim: "consists of all the products and items that a particular seller offers for sale." [Slide 24 — verbatim]
  • It's evaluated on four dimensions, listed by name only on Slide 24: Width / Length / Depth / Consistency [Slide 24]. The definitions in the table below are Kotler textbook standard — Slide 24 lists the four dimensions by name only; the descriptions are not on the slide:
Dimension What it measures Example: Nestle Egypt
Width How many different product lines the company runs. Nestle has dairy (Nido), water (Pure Life), confectionery (Kit Kat), coffee (Nescafé), infant nutrition (Cerelac). Width ≈ 5.
Length The total number of items across all lines. Sum of all Nido SKUs + all Nescafé SKUs + …
Depth The number of versions of each item in the line. Nescafé Classic / Gold / 3-in-1 / decaf / sachet vs jar — depth per line.
Consistency How closely the various lines are related in end-use, production, or distribution. Nestle = food/drink: high consistency. A conglomerate like Mansour Group (cars + groceries + machinery) has low consistency.

6. Branding Strategy — Building Strong Brands [Slides 25-31]

Figure 8.3 — Major Brand Strategy Decisions [Slide 26], a four-step flow:

Brand positioning → Brand name selection → Brand sponsorship → Brand development

6.0 Brand equity — definition [Slide 25]

(Standalone definition slide before the brand-building figure.) Brand equity is the differential effect knowing the brand name has on customer response to the product. High-equity brands command higher loyalty, price premium, and resilience to competitive moves.

6a. Brand positioning [Slide 26 — Figure 8.3 first cell; not a standalone slide]

Three levels a brand can be positioned on:

  • Attributes — features and specifications (e.g., Tide gets your clothes white).
  • Benefits — what the attribute does for you (e.g., Volvo = safety).
  • Beliefs and values — what the brand stands for in the world (e.g., Patagonia = sustainability, Apple = think different).

Beliefs-and-values is the strongest position because it's hardest for competitors to copy.

6b. Brand name selection — "Desirable qualities" [Slide 27]

Slide 27 verbatim — quote these on the exam (note item 5 is verbatim ungrammatical "Translate easy" — do not silently correct):

  1. Suggest benefits and qualities
  2. Easy to pronounce, recognize, and remember
  3. Distinctive
  4. Extendable
  5. Translate easy into foreign languages
  6. Capable of registration and legal protection

Textbook-canonical illustrations (not on Slide 27, useful for memory): Suggest benefits — Beautyrest mattresses, Dove soap. Extendable — Amazon (started with books, works for everything). Translate easily — Chevrolet's "Nova" famously means "doesn't go" in Spanish.

And once selected, the brand must be defended legally — Dr. Alaa raised the counterfeit issue: "العلامات التجارية المقلدة... 70% من المنتجات المقلدة في العالم — الصين" (counterfeit trademarks... 70% of the world's counterfeits — China) [Lec 4 @ 1:31:28-1:31:46]. Companies like Tommy Hilfiger lose serious revenue to fakes, which is exactly why the textbook lists "capable of registration and legal protection" as a brand-name requirement.

6c. Brand sponsorship — four options [Slide 28]

Option What it means Example
Manufacturer's brand The producer's own name on the product. "Nestlé Nido", "Apple iPhone", "Toyota Corolla".
Private brand (store brand) The retailer's own name on a product the retailer doesn't manufacture. Carrefour-branded biscuits, Spinneys-branded juice, Costco's Kirkland line.
Licensed brand A brand name licensed from another company (entertainment, sports, celebrity). Disney-branded children's clothing, Ferrari-branded sunglasses, Mickey Mouse on a bag of pasta.
Co-brand Two established brand names on the same product. Intel-Inside on a Dell laptop, Visa-Mastercard with banks, Apple-Nike Sports Watch.

6d. Brand development — Figure 8.4 (Brand Development Strategies) [Slide 29]

The matrix is Brand name (Existing / New) × Product category (Existing / New):

Existing product category New product category
Existing brand name Line extension — new flavour, size, ingredient of the same product. (Coca-Cola Vanilla, Diet Coke, Coke Zero.) Brand extension — push the brand into a new category. (Honda from cars into motorbikes and generators.)
New brand name Multibrands — different brand names within the same category. (P&G has Tide, Ariel, Bonux — all detergents.) New brands — fresh brand in fresh category. (Toyota → Lexus for luxury; Toyota → Scion for youth.)

Slide-29 callout (verbatim, questions only — slide does not supply answers): "This is a very handy framework for analyzing brand development opportunities. For example, what strategy did Toyota use when it introduced the Toyota Camry Hybrid? When it introduced the Toyota Prius? The Scion?" [Slide 29 — verbatim callout]

Common textbook readings (not on Slide 29 — Slide 29 leaves them as an exercise): Camry Hybrid → line extension; Prius → brand extension (new product category, same Toyota brand); Scion → new brand (new brand name + new product category).

7. Managing brands [Slide 30]

Companies must manage their brands carefully — putting great care into managing the touch points through which customers know the brand [Slide 30]:

  • Advertising
  • Personal experience with the brand
  • Word of mouth
  • Company web pages

Any one of these can build or destroy brand equity.

8. International and Regional Branding [Slide 31]

"Many local brands in the Arab world have succeeded in dominating their markets by benefiting from their local knowledge of consumer needs and preferences. Many of them have even extended their operations internationally as well as regionally." [Slide 31]

Strong MENA examples worth memorising (Dr. Alaa's territory): Juhayna (dairy, Egypt → Gulf), Edita (snacks, Egypt → MENA), Almarai (dairy, Saudi → regional), Aramex (logistics), LC Waikiki (Turkish → big in Egypt), MoneyGram-style local money services.

9. Services Marketing [Slides 32-33]

9a. Types of service industries [Slide 32]

Three big buckets, listed by name only on the slide [Slide 32]: Government, Private not-for-profit organizations, Business services.

Textbook-standard illustrations (not on Slide 32): Government — postal, defence, public health. Private not-for-profit — universities, hospitals, NGOs. Business services — banking, insurance, consulting, telecom, airlines, restaurants.

9b. Four Service Characteristics — Figure 8.5 [Slide 33]

The Big Four. Memorise these — they are textbook exam material.

Characteristic Definition (verbatim from slide) What it means in practice
Intangibility "Services cannot be seen, tasted, felt, heard, or smelled before purchase." You can't try a haircut before paying. Marketers compensate with tangible cues — clean uniforms, professional offices, before/after photos.
Inseparability "Services cannot be separated from their providers." Your barber is part of the haircut. If she's having a bad day, your service is bad. Production and consumption happen at the same time.
Variability "Quality of services depends on who provides them and when, where, and how." Two flights on the same airline have different service quality depending on cabin crew, weather, time of day. Standardisation training tries to minimise this.
Perishability "Services cannot be stored for later sale or use." An empty seat on tonight's EgyptAir flight is gone forever. Hotels overbook for the same reason.

Slide 33 — Figure 8.5 side callout, full verbatim: "Although services are 'products' in a general sense, they have special characteristics and marketing needs. The biggest differences come from the fact that services are essentially intangible and that they are created through direct interactions with customers. Think about your experiences with an airline versus Nike or Apple." [Slide 33 — Figure 8.5 callout, verbatim]

9c. Two stories Dr. Alaa used to lock down the service mindset (NotebookLM-captured)

The "Genetic Inheritance" hotel-shampoo joke — anchor for intangibility / no-ownership-transfer:

"When you stay in a hotel, you leave and don't take the room with you. Well, some people try! [laughs]. People always take the small shampoos — I don't know, maybe it's a 'Genetic Inheritance' — Egyptians always take them! We consider those paid for, but some take the bathrobes or slippers too. Usually, after the service — like a taxi ride — you leave with nothing tangible except the experience of being moved." [Lec_4_1 @ 08:54-09:36 — verbatim Arabic: "يعني أنت لما بتروح تنزل في فندق… بياخد الشامبو… الشامبوهات صغيرة… كل المصريين لازم ياخدوا الشامبو"]

The point under the humour: a service is consumed in the moment of delivery and leaves no tangible asset behind. The tiny shampoo bottle exists precisely so the hotel can hand you something to take away — a small souvenir of an otherwise intangible product.

Price-Quality Inference in expertise — the Abu Shaka / Mortada lawyer fees example:

A junior lawyer in Egypt charges a small fee. A high-profile expert — Abu Shaka, Mortada — can charge millions for the same kind of legal representation. Both technically deliver "court appearance + argument," but the client pays the premium for expertise and the higher perceived probability of success. Same logic for medical consulting: a newly-graduated doctor's fee is low; a 30-year consultant's fee is a multiple of that — for the same diagnosis. [Lec_4_B @ 1:50:50 — verbatim Arabic: "أبو شقة ده… ألا أسمه أبو شقة ده"]

This is the Price-Quality Inference mechanism (it returns in Ch.10 as one of the psychological-pricing tactics): when the consumer cannot test the result beforehand (because services are intangible), they read the high price as a signal of expertise and quality. The "product" being sold is the provider's accumulated experience — the ultimate differentiator in any market where you can't try-before-you-buy.

Dr. Alaa's in-class examples (Ch.8 specific)

  • Chipsy biscuits, water, ice cream as the archetypal convenience products [Lec 4 @ 28:50-29:02]
  • Fridge / electrical appliances / cars as the archetypal shopping products [Lec 4 @ 30:00-30:08]
  • Toothpaste with sensitive / whitening / kids variants as line filling [references Slide 23]
  • Quality grading 1-4 — same brand mark, three price/quality levels: European importers buy grade 1, Egyptian importers buy grade 4 [Lec 4 @ 1:15:23-1:15:41]
  • Packaging of children's products, perfumes, lipstick, mascara = where packaging dominates buying decisions [Lec 4 @ 1:12:54-1:13:25]
  • Counterfeit brands ("brand counterfeit / علامات تجارية مقلدة"); 70% of world counterfeits come from China [Lec 4 @ 1:31:22-1:31:50] — used to motivate brand-name legal protection
  • Toyota Camry Hybrid / Prius / Scion as the brand-development examples [Slide 29]
  • BlackBerry as the three-levels-of-product example: device (actual) vs. freedom & on-the-go connectivity (core value) vs. BlackBerry Enterprise Server (augmented) [Slide 4 callout]
  • MENA local brands extending regionally / internationally — Dr. Alaa's repeated example slot [Slide 31]

Common confusions & key distinctions

If you see… …think about which side it's on
Product vs Service Service = intangible, no ownership transfer [Slide 3]. Product = tangible (or branded experience).
Consumer product vs Industrial product Personal consumption = consumer. Resale / further processing / business use = industrial [Slides 6, 11].
Convenience vs Shopping Convenience = bought frequently, immediately, minimum comparison. Shopping = customer compares carefully on suitability, quality, price, style. [Slides 7-8]
Shopping vs Specialty Shopping = comparison-shopper, will substitute. Specialty = brand-loyal, will travel for the specific brand, won't substitute [Slide 9].
Specialty vs Unsought Specialty = customer actively seeks it out (brand loyalty). Unsought = customer doesn't normally think of buying it (insurance, blood donation) [Slides 9-10].
Quality level vs Conformance quality Quality level = the target (low / medium / high) that fits positioning. Conformance = how consistently you hit that target without defects [Slide 17].
Style vs Design Style = appearance only. Design = appearance + usefulness [Slide 19].
Brand vs Brand equity Brand = the name/sign that identifies the maker. Brand equity = the differential customer response caused by knowing the brand [Slide 20].
Packaging vs Labeling Packaging = the physical wrapper/container. Labeling = the written information on the package (legal + commercial) [Slide 21].
Line filling vs Line stretching Filling = adding items within the current price/quality range. Stretching = extending the range outward (up, down, or both) [Slide 23].
Line stretching down vs up Downward = extending toward cheaper/lower-quality end. Upward = extending toward premium end [Slide 23].
Product mix Width vs Length Width = number of different lines. Length = total number of items across all lines [Slide 24].
Product mix Length vs Depth Length = items across the whole mix. Depth = versions of each item (sizes, formulations) [Slide 24].
Line extension vs Brand extension Line extension = existing brand + existing category (Diet Coke). Brand extension = existing brand + new category (Honda motorbikes) [Slide 29].
Multibrands vs New brands Multibrands = new brand name but existing category (P&G's Tide, Ariel, Bonux). New brands = new brand name AND new category (Toyota → Scion) [Slide 29].
Manufacturer's brand vs Private brand Manufacturer = producer's own name. Private = retailer's own name (Carrefour, Spinneys) [Slide 28].
Licensed brand vs Co-brand Licensed = you rent someone else's name (Disney on kids' clothes). Co-brand = two equal brands on one product (Intel Inside on Dell) [Slide 28].
Service characteristics — 4 of them Intangibility (cannot be sensed before purchase), Inseparability (cannot be separated from provider), Variability (quality depends on who/when/where/how), Perishability (cannot be stored). Memorise IIVP. [Slide 33]

Why this matters for your exam

Based on the calibration evidence from the midterm (Dr. Alaa hit 23/23 actual exam questions verbatim from the Kotler test bank — see CALIBRATION.md), expect:

  1. A definition-style T/F or MCQ on the four consumer-product types (convenience / shopping / specialty / unsought) — most likely a swap trap: "convenience products are bought with maximum effort" — FALSE; or "shopping products are bought frequently with minimum comparison" — FALSE. The exact textbook wording [Slides 7-10] is the answer key.
  2. An MCQ on the three product levels (Core / Actual / Augmented) — answer-key wording: "what the customer is really buying" = core value [Slide 4]. Watch for "augmented product" being defined as the physical product (FALSE — that's actual).
  3. A T/F on the four service characteristics (Intangibility / Inseparability / Variability / Perishability) — likely swapping definitions, e.g., "Perishability means services cannot be separated from their providers" (FALSE — that's Inseparability). [Slide 33]
  4. A short-answer or mini-case asking you to identify which brand-development strategy (line / brand / multibrands / new brands) a given launch represents — for example, "Toyota launches the Lexus" → new brand; "Coca-Cola launches Coke Zero" → line extension. [Slide 29]
  5. A T/F on packaging vs labeling — likely swapping the definitions: "Packaging involves the written information on the wrapper" (FALSE — that's labeling). [Slide 21]
  6. An MCQ on line filling vs line stretching — most likely: "Adding items within the present range is called…" → line filling. Or: "Extending the line toward lower-priced segments is called…" → downward stretching. [Slide 23]
  7. A brand-equity definition MCQ — almost guaranteed because Dr. Alaa hit the same definition format on the midterm. Stem: "_____ is the differential effect that knowing the brand name has on customer response to the product and its marketing." Answer: Brand equity. [Slide 25]
  8. A mini-case anchoring 4Ps decisions to a specific Egyptian/MENA brand (e.g., Juhayna, Nido, LC Waikiki) — expect to be asked to identify which P each action sits under and to justify packaging / branding / line-stretching choices.
