Chapter 10 — Pricing (Kotler Ch.10)

💡 In Plain English — Chapter 10, told as one story

Same running example as Ch.8: you own BrewHaus coffee shop. In Ch.8 you decided WHAT to sell. Now in Ch.10 you decide how much to charge for it.

1. What is "price" really?

Price = the money customer gives up to get your product. But here's the killer line to memorize:

Price is the ONLY P in the 4Ps that brings money IN. The other 3 (Product, Place, Promotion) only cost money.

So if you mess up pricing at BrewHaus, none of the rest matters — your great coffee, your cute shop, your Instagram ads all become losses.

2. The price has a FLOOR and a CEILING

Imagine pricing as a window with a top and a bottom:

  • Ceiling (TOP) = the maximum customers perceive as worth it. Charge 200 EGP for a cappuccino and nobody buys. Customer's perceived value sets the top.
  • Floor (BOTTOM) = your cost to make and serve the cappuccino (beans + milk + cup + barista's time + rent share). Charge below that and you lose money on every cup.
  • Sweet zone = anywhere between floor and ceiling. The exact number inside depends on your strategy, competitors, and market conditions.

If your floor ever rises ABOVE the ceiling (your costs exceed what customers will pay), exit that market — no profitable price exists.

3. Three ways to choose a number inside the window

Three main pricing strategies — most shops use a mix:

  1. Customer-value-based pricing (the modern textbook favorite). Ask: "What's this worth in the customer's mind?" Then price from there. Steps: understand customer → set price by value → design product to fit cost ≤ price. BrewHaus example: customers value a calm Sunday-morning cappuccino at 60 EGP — so we price at 55 and build the cost structure to fit. Two flavors:
    • Good-value pricing — fair quality at a fair price (BrewHaus standard cappuccino).
    • Value-added pricing — add features, charge more (BrewHaus "Signature" cappuccino with imported beans + foam art at 85 EGP).
  2. Cost-based pricing (old school). Calculate cost, add a fixed markup. BrewHaus example: cup costs 15 EGP, add 40% markup = 21 EGP price. Simple but ignores what customer will pay.
  3. Competition-based pricing. Look at Costa next door and price accordingly — same, slightly cheaper, or slightly higher with better service.

4. Internal + external factors squeezing the number

Even after you pick a strategy, real-world factors push the price around:

  • Internal: your marketing objective (are you trying to grab market share or maximize profit?), the overall marketing mix, your costs, your organization.
  • External: the market type (monopoly? lots of competitors?), demand sensitivity (will a 10% price hike lose you 50% of customers?), competitors' prices, the economy, government rules.

5. New product pricing — only TWO strategies to remember

Launching something brand new at BrewHaus (say, a new signature drink)? Two extreme options:

  • Market-skimming = start HIGH, then drop. Milk the early enthusiasts who'll pay anything. Works when product is unique and copy-cats are slow. Like Apple at iPhone launches.
  • Market-penetration = start LOW to grab huge market share fast. Works when market is price-sensitive and you can scale. Like Xiaomi entering Egypt.

6. Product MIX pricing — pricing products that live together

Once you have many products at BrewHaus, you can't price each one in isolation. Five tactics:

  1. Product line pricing — set price steps across a line. Small 25 EGP / Medium 35 EGP / Large 45 EGP. The gaps feel logical.
  2. Optional product pricing — base item + paid extras. Cappuccino 35 EGP, oat milk +5, extra shot +7.
  3. Captive product pricing — cheap base, expensive refills. Cheap printer, expensive ink. BrewHaus version: sell coffee machines at home cost, charge premium for branded coffee pods.
  4. By-product pricing — sell the leftovers. Used coffee grounds to gardeners as fertilizer.
  5. Product bundle pricing — combo deal cheaper than sum of parts. "Cappuccino + croissant = 50 EGP" (vs. 35 + 25 separately).

7. Price ADJUSTMENT strategies — changing the price after launch

Seven ways you can tweak BrewHaus prices in real life:

  1. Discount & allowance — early bird coffee 20% off before 8am.
  2. Segmented pricing — student discount, senior discount (same coffee, different price for different groups).
  3. Psychological pricing — 29.99 EGP feels cheaper than 30.
  4. Promotional pricing — short-term sale ("buy 1 get 1 free this weekend").
  5. Geographical pricing — Cairo branch prices differ from Alexandria branch.
  6. Dynamic pricing — price changes by demand (Uber surge; airline tickets).
  7. International pricing — same product, different country, different price (Big Mac costs different in every country).

How chapter 10 connects together:

Price is the only money-maker → set a floor (cost) and ceiling (perceived value) → pick a strategy (value / cost / competition) → consider internal + external factors → for new products choose skim or penetrate → for existing portfolios use mix-pricing tactics → over time fine-tune with adjustments. Same BrewHaus, just zooming through layers of the pricing decision.

In one sentence

Price is the amount of money charged for a product or service — and crucially, it is the only element in the marketing mix that produces revenue; every other P (product, place, promotion) represents a cost. [Slide 3]

Verbatim deck definition — quote this on the exam

"Price is the amount of money charged for a product or service. It is the sum of all the values that consumers give up in order to gain the benefits of having or using a product or service." [Slide 3 — both sentences, verbatim]

"Price is the only element in the marketing mix that produces revenue; all other elements represent costs." [Slide 3 — verbatim]

The second sentence of the definition ("sum of all the values that consumers give up…") is the textbook-classic phrasing — Dr. Alaa is likely to recycle it verbatim.

In plain language

The other three Ps cost the company money. The factory costs money. The trucks that move the product to the shelf cost money. The TV ads cost money. Price is the only lever that turns those costs into cash coming back into the firm. Get it wrong and you either leave money on the table (too low) or scare customers away (too high). Get it right and the rest of the marketing plan funds itself.

Where does the "right" price come from? Three different angles, all valid, used in combination: - Customer perceptions of value set the ceiling. Above this price, no one will buy because they don't think the product is worth it. [Slide 4] - Product costs set the floor. Below this price, the company loses money on every unit. [Slide 4] - Other internal and external considerations live in the band between the floor and the ceiling. [Slide 4]

This is the Price Ceiling / Price Floor framework [Figure 10.1]. If your costs (floor) ever rise above what customers will pay (ceiling), you have no viable pricing zone — exit that market.

Figure 10.1 — Considerations in Setting Price (verbatim labels)

The slide's labelled diagram shows three blocks bridged by green double-arrows:

Left boxMiddle box (3 verbatim items)Right box
Customer perceptions of value — Price ceiling: No demand above this price Other internal and external considerations:
1. Marketing strategy, objectives, and mix
2. Nature of the market and demand
3. Competitors' strategies and prices		 Product costs — Price floor: No profits below this price

[Slide 4 / Figure 10.1 — verbatim middle-box list]. The middle-box items are the deck's specific framing of "other considerations" — broader factors like the economy and government regulations live separately on Slide 20 and should not be conflated with this figure.

Price is a multi-dimensional value sacrifice — not just money

Dr. Alaa's foundational reframe: the "price" the customer pays is the total exchange of value, not just the monetary number on the tag. The customer surrenders four distinct kinds of cost to acquire your product [Lec 5 @ ~00:15-02:10, source: NotebookLM transcript]:

Cost type What the customer gives up Example
Monetary (explicit) Cash, tuition, broker fees, running costs Apartment purchase price, MBA tuition
Time Cost Hours/days/months of life spent searching, comparing, coordinating Months of apartment-hunting visits and broker calls
Effort Cost Physical and logistical energy Driving to multiple sites, carrying things, managing paperwork
Psychological Cost Stress, anxiety, mental burden MBA exam stress; coordinating a major life purchase
Opportunity Cost Foregone alternatives — family time, leisure, other purchases MBA = time NOT spent with kids, NOT on another course, NOT building a business

Strategic "So What?" — For the manager, the imperative is to minimize the customer's implicit costs (Time, Stress, Effort) to justify a higher monetary price. If a brand reduces the searching cost (good shopping experience, clear information) or the stress cost (reliable delivery, easy returns) or the time cost (fast service, single visit), it captures more of the value surplus and can charge more for the same physical good. Netflix's whole proposition is "we cut your temporal cost of going to a cinema" — and the monthly subscription price reflects that captured surplus.

Dr. Alaa's MBA + Zamalek anchor [Lec 5 @ ~02:10, source: NotebookLM transcript]:

"Take your MBA as another example. Is the price just the tuition? No. You are paying with your nerves, your stress during exams, and your time away from family. These are all values you 'pay' to receive the degree. This is a painful realization, isn't it? Like the stress of being a Zamalek fan — it stays with you! [Laughter]. If it were just money, you would be buying a certificate, not an education."

Apartment hunting example [Lec 5 @ ~01:00, source: NotebookLM transcript]: buying an apartment is not just the millions of pounds you pay the seller — it's the broker's commission, the location, the months spent calling brokers, visiting sites, meeting agents (Time Cost), the physical and mental energy expended (Effort Cost). All of those are "prices" the customer pays in addition to the monetary number.

Dr. Alaa's class anchor: starting point and success in pricing both come from understanding the customer — what they want, what quality level, what brand image, what price they can absorb. "نقطة البداية والنجاح كله بيجي من فهم العميل — عميل عايز إيه، إيه الجوة اللي عايزها، إيه السعر اللي ممكن يتضعف" [Lec 5 @ 40:41].

Core concepts

1. The pricing strategies (and how to pick between them)

The deck explicitly names two pricing strategies and contrasts them on Slide 6 — value-based vs cost-based. A third category, competition-based pricing, is a textbook-derived label (Kotler) commonly used in MBA teaching but is not named on this deck's slides; the deck instead treats competitive dynamics under §10.4 Pricing in Different Types of Markets (Slide 17 — pure competition, monopolistic, oligopoly, pure monopoly). Quote the two deck-named strategies on the exam; use "competition-based pricing" as a textbook-derived umbrella only if asked.

Strategy Anchor used to set price When it works Source
Value-based pricing Buyers' perceptions of value — what they think the product is worth Differentiated products with strong brand or features the customer truly values [Slide 6 — deck-named]
Cost-based pricing The cost to make + a standard markup Stable industries, commodity products where competitors have similar costs [Slides 6, 7 — deck-named]
Competition-based pricing (textbook-derived label, not deck-named) Competitors' prices for the same/similar offering Pure competition or oligopoly markets where customers see products as substitutes Kotler textbook umbrella; deck handles competitive dynamics via market types on [Slide 17]

The key contrast on Slide 6: "Value-based pricing is customer driven. Cost-based pricing is product driven." Cost-based asks "what did this cost me to make?" first, then bolts on a margin. Value-based asks "what is this worth to my customer?" first, then engineers cost down to fit.

Dr. Alaa hammered this with the iPhone case [Lec 5 @ 27:18 source B]: a phone selling at $1000 may cost Apple only $100 to manufacture — about 10% of the selling price. The other 90% margin exists because customers attach value to the brand, the OS, the camera, the lifestyle. "المستهلك بيشتري بناء على ال value — customer value — ده ال value-based pricing" [Lec 5 @ 28:42 source B]. Compare a Toyota Corolla at 1.5 million EGP — three times the production cost — still selling, because customers value reliability, resale value, and the Toyota brand [Lec 5 @ 1:12:00 source B].

2. Customer perceptions of value (Slide 5–6)

Customer-oriented pricing means understanding how much value consumers place on the benefits they receive and setting a price that captures that value [Slide 5]. The value is not in the metal or the plastic — it's in what the product does for the customer's life.

Verbatim deck definition — Value-Based Pricing

"Value-based pricing uses the buyers' perceptions of value, not the sellers cost, as the key to pricing. Price is considered before the marketing program is set." [Slide 6 — verbatim]

"Value-based pricing is customer driven."
"Cost-based pricing is product driven." [Slide 6 — verbatim contrast]

Situational utility — the bottle of water case (Temporary Monopoly)

Dr. Alaa's signature case for how context moves the price ceiling [Lec 5 @ ~06:45-07:30, source: NotebookLM transcript]:

"How much is a bottle of water? Five, seven, maybe ten pounds? But would you pay 150 pounds for it? [Student: Only in the desert!] Or if you are at an Amr Diab concert in the North Coast — you've been there for seven hours, you are thirsty, and you aren't allowed to bring your own water. If they tell you the bottle is 150 or 200 pounds, you will buy it. In that context, the value equals 200 pounds because the situational utility is at its peak."

Strategic principle: situational utility creates a "Temporary Monopoly." For the manager this means pricing should be dynamic. By controlling the environment (an exclusive venue where competitors aren't allowed in) or by identifying urgent / inelastic needs (water in the desert, antibiotics in a hospital, a phone charger at an airport), a firm can legally push the price toward a ceiling that is exponentially higher than the cost-based floor. Same bottle, same cost — but the context changes the perceived value 30× over.

Brand equity decouples price from cost — the 4 luxury value drivers

For premium brands, the customer is not paying for the physical product — they are paying for a bundle of intangibles. Dr. Alaa's Mercedes GLC case [Lec 5 @ ~11:15-12:00, source: NotebookLM transcript]:

"Look at a Mercedes GLC. It might cost five or six million pounds. Does Mercedes price based on cost or value? A Chinese car might have more features and gadgets for half the price. Why does the customer still want the Mercedes? [Student: For the star! For the prestige.] Exactly. They want the 'star,' the luxury image, the social distinction, and the resale value. The customer knows they can sell it easily later."

The four luxury value drivers for premium-brand pricing:

Driver What the customer is buying Why it justifies the premium
The Star / Logo Symbolism of social status, elite achievement Visible signal to the customer's social circle
Resale Value Security of a liquid asset retaining worth The premium is partly recovered when sold
Luxury Image Psychological satisfaction of prestige Self-perception, emotional return
Lifestyle Profiling Admission to a specific community/identity Membership in a tribe (see Harley case below)

Harley-Davidson — Psychographic profiling and community-driven loyalty

Harley-Davidson doesn't sell motorcycles — it sells a "Bikers" lifestyle [Lec 5 @ ~13:30, source: NotebookLM transcript]:

"Harley-Davidson doesn't just sell motorcycles; they sell a 'Bikers' lifestyle. They study their customers' age, income, and profession to understand why they form clubs and ride together on Friday mornings. This community-driven loyalty makes the customer price-insensitive, as they are paying for admission into a specific social hierarchy."

The company has invested decades in profiling exactly who its customer is (the demographics: age, income, occupation; the psychographics: weekend-club affiliation, brotherhood identity). When you buy a Harley, you're buying entry to that tribe. Price sensitivity collapses because the customer can't get the same identity from any cheaper alternative — a Chinese sportbike at one-fifth the price doesn't grant Bikers Club membership.

Starbucks vs Nescafé — paying for atmosphere and trust

The same drink (instant coffee, espresso, latte) can sell for 5 LE at a local café (powdered "ceramic" Nescafé, plastic cups, no ambience) or 90+ LE at Starbucks (branded paper cup, sofa, Wi-Fi, music). The customer is paying for atmosphere and trust, not for the raw ingredient [Lec 5 @ ~15:00, source: NotebookLM transcript]:

"Whether it's a Mercedes or a Starbucks coffee vs. a 'ceramic powder' Nescafé at a local cafe, the customer pays for the atmosphere and trust."

For a manager, building a brand is the ultimate defense against "cost-plus" competition. Brand equity lets the firm dictate the ceiling rather than be trapped by the floor.

Asian-themed compound — re-engineering perceived value

The Asian-themed compound case Dr. Alaa told in class is the perfect illustration [Lec 5 @ 22:00 source B]: a tourist village that couldn't sell its units hired a marketing firm, themed everything around Asia (elephant statue at the entrance, Asian building designs), ran a big advertising campaign, built a brand identity (Flavor / Identity), and the next phase was bought sight unseen by the government's legal authority. "شوف هنا التسويق فرقت ايه — فرقت انه هو عمل فليفر وعمل هوية وعمل ايدنتي بالقرية" [Lec 5 @ 23:14 source B]. Marketing changed the perceived value, and the customer paid more for what was essentially the same brick.

3. Company and product costs (Slides 7–16) — Cost-Based, Cost-Plus & Break-Even Pricing

Scope: this section covers cost-based pricing (s.7–13), Cost-Plus Pricing (s.14), and Break-Even & Target-Profit Pricing (s.15–16). The earlier "(Slides 7–13)" range was wrong.

Cost-based pricing adds a standard markup to the cost of the product. To use it you must understand the structure of your costs.

Verbatim deck definition — Cost-Based Pricing

"Cost-based pricing involves setting prices based on the costs for producing, distributing, and selling the product plus a fair rate of return for its effort and risk." [Slide 7 — verbatim]

"Cost-based pricing adds a standard markup to the cost of the product." [Slide 7 — verbatim]

Types of costs [Slide 8]: - Fixed costs — do not vary with production or sales level. Examples on slide: rent, heat, interest, executive salaries [Slide 9]. Dr. Alaa: "زي ايجار المكان — سواء انتجت أو ما انتجتش هتدفع الإيجار، هتدفع مرتبات الإدارة" [Lec 5 @ 58:18 source B]. - Variable costs — vary with the level of production. Examples: packaging, raw materials [Slide 10]. - Total costs are the sum of the fixed and variable costs for any given level of production. [Slide 10 — verbatim] - Average cost is the cost associated with a given level of output. [Slide 10 — verbatim] (i.e., total cost ÷ units produced.)

Cost behaviour at scale [Slide 11 / Figure 10.3]: inside a fixed-size plant, the short-run average cost (SRAC) curve is U-shaped — costs drop as volume rises (you spread fixed costs over more units), then eventually rise again as the plant hits capacity. Across plants of different sizes, the long-run average cost (LRAC) curve is the envelope of all the SRACs — showing the best cost a firm could achieve if it could redesign its plant for any volume. The bottom of LRAC is the minimum efficient scale.

The experience / learning curve [Slide 12 / Figure 10.4]: as cumulative production rises, average cost falls — not just because fixed costs spread, but because the firm learns (better suppliers, better methods, less waste, better quality). Dr. Alaa's Egyptian example: the tahina-and-halawa market has only ~10–15 producers (Rashidi El-Mizan, El Halawany, and a few others) because incumbents have a learning curve that new entrants cannot easily climb — they know the suppliers, the secret recipes, the quality tricks [Lec 5 @ 51:00 source B]. Halawany's halawa simply tastes different from Rashidi's, because of decades of accumulated process knowledge.

Cost-plus pricing [Slide 14]: take the cost, add a standard markup (the percentage typical for your industry — could be 10%, 15%, 20%, 30%, or 100% depending on the sector). Banks use a "markup fees" rate that has moved between 5 and 10 and back to 5 in recent years in Egypt [Lec 5 @ 56:30 source B]. Advantages: sellers are certain about their costs; prices look similar across the industry so price wars are minimised; consumers see it as fair. Disadvantage: it ignores demand and competitor prices — you might be charging far below what customers would actually pay [Slide 14].

Break-even and target-profit pricing [Slide 15 / Figure 10.5]:

"Break-even pricing is the price at which total costs are equal to total revenue and there is no profit." [Slide 15 — verbatim]

"Target profit pricing is the price at which the firm will break even or make the profit it's seeking." [Slide 15 — verbatim]

Note the deck wording: both definitions frame break-even and target-profit as a price (the unit price that, given an expected volume, satisfies the equation), not as a volume. In practice the same equation can be solved for either price or break-even quantity — but on the exam quote the deck's "price at which…" phrasing.

Dr. Alaa's Felafel-shop example [Lec 5 @ 59:30 source B]: "the foul/falafel shop knows it must sell 1,000 sandwiches a day to cover its costs. Every sandwich after the thousandth is pure profit. Below 1,000, it's losing money." That's break-even thinking applied to a corner shop.

Figure 10.5 — Break-Even Chart for Determining Target Price [Slide 16]

The textbook chart on Slide 16 plots three lines on a Cost-in-dollars (y-axis, thousands) vs Sales-volume-in-units (x-axis, thousands) graph:

  • Fixed cost — flat horizontal line at ~$300,000 (independent of volume).
  • Total cost (red) — upward-sloping line starting at the fixed-cost intercept and rising with variable cost per unit.
  • Total revenue (green) — upward-sloping line through the origin (price × units).

The two sloped lines cross at the break-even point, marked on the chart at 30,000 units, where the callout reads: "At the break-even point, here 30,000 units, total revenue equals total cost." Above 30,000 units, the wedge between the green and red lines is profit. The chart highlights a target profit of $200,000 at ~50,000 units (the dashed bracket between the Total revenue and Total cost lines on the right side). This is the visual proof of why volume × price-margin must clear the fixed-cost block before any profit appears.

Unconscious losses — why SMEs misprice

Dr. Alaa: small-shop owners chronically misprice by only counting the variable costs they can see (raw materials, immediate inputs) and forgetting the running fixed costs that consume the firm whether it produces or not [Lec 5 @ ~17:15, source: NotebookLM transcript]:

"How does a small falafel shop price a sandwich? He looks at the bread, the beans, the tahini. He says it costs 5 or 6 pounds, so he sells it for 8. Is this correct? No! He forgot the Running Costs — the rent, the electricity, the labor, the taxes, even the fines from the local municipality!"

When the manager only sees variable costs, the firm operates at an unconscious loss: the books look fine on a per-sandwich basis, but cash silently drains because fixed costs are not being recovered. The break-even framework forces the discipline of counting all costs before setting price.

Egyptian SME classification (sales-volume tiers)

For exam purposes, Dr. Alaa explicitly classified SMEs by annual sales volume in the Egyptian market [Lec 5 @ ~18:30, source: NotebookLM transcript]:

SME tier Annual sales (EGP)
Micro-Enterprise Up to 2 million EGP
Small Enterprise 2 million – 100 million EGP
Medium Enterprise 100 million – 400 million EGP

These tiers matter for pricing because the strategic options differ by tier: a micro-enterprise has almost no pricing power (cost-plus only); a medium-enterprise can invest in branding, R&D, and channel control to migrate toward value-based pricing.

3b. The Loyalty Ladder — 4 stages from Satisfaction to Partnership [Lecture-only — NOT on Ch.10 deck; relates to §2 Customer perceptions of value]

Scope note: Loyalty Ladder is from lecture, not the Ch.10 pricing deck. It belongs conceptually with §2 "Customer perceptions of value" (loyalty is what unlocks price premium). Kept here for now but cite as lecture-only, not pricing-slide.

Marketing is an investment in customer psychology. High marketing efficacy transforms a one-time purchaser into a Partner, which grants the company immense pricing power and protection against market volatility [Lec 5 @ ~22:00-25:00, source: NotebookLM transcript].

Dr. Alaa's Four Stages of the Loyalty Ladder:

Stage What the customer does Pricing power for the firm
1. Satisfaction Baseline — the product met the stated requirements. Customer is content but not yet attached. Low — customer will switch if a competitor offers slightly better value.
2. Loyalty Repeated purchases and consistent brand preference. Customer is now a habit. Medium — switching cost is now psychological; some price premium tolerable.
3. Advocacy Customer actively defends the brand against critics and recommends it to friends and colleagues. High — customer is now doing marketing work for the brand for free.
4. Partnership Highest level — customer feels a sense of "ownership" and deep connection with the brand. Maximum — customer is price-insensitive.

Strategic "So What?" — A "Partner" is no longer a transactional buyer. They become an unpaid sales force: defending the brand on social media, bringing in new customers by word-of-mouth, tolerating price increases without complaint, and forgiving the occasional service slip. Price sensitivity collapses once the customer reaches Partnership, which is the entire point of investing in identity-driven branding.

Skipping stages — the Blue Blue Asia / Sokhna case in this lens [Lec 5 @ ~25:00, source: NotebookLM transcript]: when a developer delivers a project six months ahead of schedule (as in the Sokhna example), they skip the "Satisfaction" stage entirely and move the customer directly to Partner. The buyer never goes through the doubt phase — they receive immediate evidence that the firm exceeded its promise, and they bring the next batch of buyers themselves (which is exactly what happened: judicial-authority bulk purchase of Phase 1).

This is also why Dr. Alaa's Asian-themed compound (above) didn't just sell apartments — the identity turned residents into Partners who advertised the place for free. Partner-stage customers reduce the firm's promotion costs and lift its pricing ceiling simultaneously.

4. Other Internal and External Considerations Affecting Price Decisions [Slides 17-20]

Scope note: matches the slide-deck banner verbatim. Slide 17 = Pricing in Different Types of Markets; slide 18 = Analyzing the Price-Demand Relationship; slide 19 = Price Elasticity of Demand; slide 20 = Other External Factors. Each is covered as an h4 below.

Type of market you're competing in [Slide 17]: - Pure competition — many sellers, identical product (commodities). Price is set by the market; you can't deviate. Dr. Alaa's banana-seller example [Lec 5 @ 57:14 source B]: every grocer sells the same kilo of bananas — "كيلو الموز معروف سعره — مقدرش هرفع الرينج ده بشكل كبير. هنا الأسعار بيحكمها المنافسة في السوق". Try to price above the going rate and no one buys. - Monopolistic competition — many sellers, differentiated products. Some pricing flexibility because customers see brands as different (restaurants, clothing, branded foods). - Oligopoly — few sellers, watching each other closely (telecom, airlines, automakers). - Pure monopoly — one seller, no substitutes (a regulated utility, a government-controlled service).

Price–demand relationship [Slide 18]: normally price up → demand down (inverse / negative relationship). Exception: for prestige (luxury) goods, higher price can equal higher demand because customers read the price as a quality signal — "if it costs more, it must be better." Sony TVs, Mercedes cars, Rolex watches — when price drops, buyers get suspicious.

Price elasticity of demand [Slide 19 / Figure 10.6]: - Inelastic demand — a big change in price produces only a small change in quantity (steep curve). Necessities, monopolies, prestige goods. - Elastic demand — a small change in price produces a big change in quantity (shallow curve). Luxuries with substitutes, undifferentiated commodities.

Other external factors [Slide 20]: Economic conditions (inflation, recession), Reseller's response to price (will distributors push it?), Government (regulations, price ceilings, taxes), Social concerns (price gouging, ethical pricing).

5. New-product pricing strategies (Slides 21–23)

Two opposite strategies for the very first price of a new product:

Market-skimming pricing [Slide 22]: launch at a high initial price to "skim" maximum revenue from the layer of customers who will pay the most. Conditions that must hold: - Product quality and image must support the high price. - Buyers must want the product at that price. - Producing in small volume must not destroy the high-price advantage. - Competitors must not be able to enter easily.

Classic example: Apple iPhone launches — premium price first year, gradually lowered as later iPhones launch. Dr. Alaa: "الناس بيشتروا منتج فاخر — السيارات الفخمة، النظرات، الساعات الفخمة، الملابس الفخمة... ده quality — quality تاع المرسيدس عالية، حتى النهاردة بتلاقي عربية مرسيدس موديل 80 أو 90 ماشية في الشارع" [Lec 5 @ 1:10:30 source B]. Customers tolerate the high price because durability, image, and resale value protect their investment.

Market-penetration pricing [Slide 23]: launch at a low initial price to penetrate the market quickly and deeply — grab market share fast. Conditions: - Price-sensitive market. - Production and distribution costs fall as sales grow (so the low price becomes sustainable). - Low prices must keep competitors out.

Dr. Alaa's Chinese-car case [Lec 5 @ 1:13:23 source B]: Chinese auto brands entering Egypt priced their flagships at 3–5 million EGP — much lower than Mercedes — to penetrate fast. But the brand is not yet known, so customers hesitate. "براندات الصينية تاع العربيات الغالية دي لسه مش مشهورة — لما يكبرش يرفع سعر البراند" — once the brand grows, they can raise prices and migrate toward skimming.

6. Product-mix pricing strategies (Slides 24–27)

Five strategies for pricing a family of related products [Slide 24]:

Product-line pricing [Slide 25]: set price steps between products in the line based on cost differences, customer perception of features, and competitor prices. Example: Toyota's line — Yaris (entry), Corolla (mid), Camry, Land Cruiser (top). The customer sees one ladder of prices that helps them self-select [Lec 5 @ 1:22:34 source B]. Cross-subsidy logic — sometimes the firm deliberately loses margin on one line item to gain sales on another: a juice producer may price Orange juice at a loss (high-volume staple, attracts buyers to the shelf) to drive higher-margin Mango sales within the same line [Lec 5 @ ~38:00, source: NotebookLM transcript]. The line is priced as a portfolio, not as isolated SKUs.

Optional-product pricing [Slide 25]: price the main product and price optional/accessory products separately. TV with optional wall mount — the TV alone is one price, the mount adds to it [Lec 5 @ 1:23:02 source B]. Car with optional GPS (built-in navigation upgrade) [Lec 5 @ ~38:00, source: NotebookLM transcript]. Mobile phone with optional extended warranty year [Lec 5 @ 1:23:14 source B] — the warranty is an optional add-on the customer can take or leave; the base phone is priced without it. Sunroof, leather seats, sound system on a car all follow the same logic.

Captive-product pricing [Slide 26]: products that must be used along with the main product. [Slide 26 — verbatim wording, including "along"]. The main product can be cheap; the captive consumables make the profit. Classic examples: razor handles cheap, blades expensive; printer cheap, ink cartridges expensive; Nespresso machine cheap, pods locked-in. For services this is called two-part pricing — a fixed fee + a variable usage fee (mobile subscription monthly fee + per-call charges; gym membership + per-class fee).

By-product pricing [Slide 27]: by-products are produced as a side-effect of the main product and have little or no value on their own. Producers will accept any price that covers storage and delivery to avoid disposal costs. Examples: - Tar / asphalt from petroleum refining — pricing it just above storage cost lets refineries clear inventory. - Sawdust from furniture shops [Lec 5 @ ~37:00, source: NotebookLM transcript] — sold to chicken farms (bedding) and bakeries (combustion); turns disposal cost into modest revenue. - Fabric scraps from clothing factories [Lec 5 @ ~37:00, source: NotebookLM transcript] — sold to mattress stuffers and rag merchants; same logic at micro-scale.

The strategic principle: any price above the net storage + removal cost is better than disposal, because the by-product would otherwise consume warehouse space and disposal fees. Egyptian SME workshops survive partly on this by-product revenue stream.

Product-bundle pricing [Slide 27]: combine several products at a reduced price relative to buying each individually. Classic examples: - McDonald's combo meal — burger + fries + drink cheaper than the three separately. - Shampoo + Conditioner combo packs [Lec 5 @ ~38:00, source: NotebookLM transcript] — the supermarket twin-pack costs less than buying both bottles individually; the manufacturer trades unit margin for bigger basket size and category capture. - Cable TV bundles — TV + internet + phone packaged at a discount.

The goal is to push customers toward higher total spend by making the bundle look like a deal — and to make it harder for a competitor to peel off any one item, because the customer has already committed to the bundle.

7. Topics on Slide 2 outline that are NOT covered in the slide deck

The chapter's official learning outcomes on Slide 2 list two more sections that the slide deck stops short of: - 10.7 Price-Adjustment Strategies (discounts/allowances, segmented pricing, psychological pricing, promotional pricing, geographical pricing, dynamic pricing, international pricing) - 10.8 Price Changes (initiating cuts/increases, buyer/competitor reactions, responding to competitor moves)

Dr. Alaa nonetheless discussed several adjustment topics verbally, and the textbook (Kotler Ch.10) is the source for any exam item that drills them. Coverage from his lecture:

Segmented pricing — Felfela Hareedy example [Lec 5 @ 46:35 source B]: a quick-service restaurant near a university campus targets ~120,000 students plus employees. Because volume is high it can charge lower prices per unit and still hit profit targets. Compare to a similar restaurant in an upscale neighbourhood (Roxy, Heliopolis) charging higher prices to fewer customers. Same product, different prices by location segment.

Psychological / quality-signal pricing — Dr. Alaa stressed that the consumer interprets price as quality [Lec 5 @ 28:28 source B] — "المستهلك دايماً بيجهل أي تكلفة للتكنولوجيا — المكونات اللي جوة دي تكلف كام مش هيشعر". They cannot see manufacturing cost, so they read price as a proxy for value. This is why skimming works for premium brands and dropping the price can paradoxically hurt a luxury brand.

Reseller's response to price — Dr. Alaa flagged this implicitly: a manufacturer who under-prices loses distributor goodwill (the distributor can't make margin); over-prices loses shelf space (the distributor finds substitutes).

For the final exam, author T/F + MCQ items on these missing topics from the Kotler Ch.10 textbook test bank, because the slide deck's omission is no protection against them.

Dr. Alaa's in-class examples for Ch.10

  1. iPhone — value-based pricing: $1000 phone, $100 production cost, 90% margin captured because customers value brand and features. [Lec 5 @ 27:18 source B]
  2. Asian-themed compound — marketing turned unsellable units into sold-out phases by building Asian identity, exotic ad campaign, and an elephant statue at the entrance. [Lec 5 @ 22:00 source B]
  3. Compound that delivered units 6 months early built credibility → satisfied customer → loyal customer → bought again in the next phase. "الشركة عندما سلمت قبل المعاد عملت لنفسها مصداقية، خلت العميل الساتسفاي تحول إلى الـ loyal" [Lec 5 @ 20:00].
  4. Samsung's 10-segment pricing strategy — releases ~10 mobile models at different price tiers, one for each segment. [Lec 5 @ 29:42 source B]
  5. Mercedes vs Chinese cars — quality + after-sales + spare-parts availability justify Mercedes' higher price even when Chinese cars are physically newer. [Lec 5 @ 42:37 source B]
  6. Harley-Davidson — millions for a motorcycle, sold to a psychographic segment (biker lifestyle, Friday-morning club rides, distinctive engine sound). [Lec 5 @ 43:31 source B]
  7. Felfela Hareedy near campus — segmented pricing by location/volume; serves 120,000 students at low per-unit price. [Lec 5 @ 46:35 source B]
  8. Business cards — minimum 100 — print shop refuses 30 cards because cost-per-card below MEQ is too high. "اقل حاجة مية — لو عايز خمسين هعملهم لك بس بتدفع سعر المية" [Lec 5 @ 47:48 source B]
  9. Tahina/Halawa market (Rashidi El-Mizan, El Halawany) — only 10–15 producers in Egypt because the learning curve is a barrier. "عشان الشركات القديمة في السوق عندها Learning curve — دول خبرة في السوق، يعرفوا ينتجوا المنتج ده بجودة عالية وتكاليف مناسبة" [Lec 5 @ 51:00 source B]
  10. Steel and rebar — additional barrier beyond learning curve: government license + huge capital required. [Lec 5 @ 51:27 source B]
  11. Banks markup fees — moved 5 → 10 → 5 in recent years; standard cost-plus markup in banking. [Lec 5 @ 56:30 source B]
  12. Bananas — pure competition pricing, no room to deviate. [Lec 5 @ 57:14 source B]
  13. Felafel shop break-even of 1,000 sandwiches/day — every sandwich beyond breakeven is pure profit. [Lec 5 @ 59:30 source B]
  14. Sony TV lasts 20 years — durability + brand quality justify skimming. [Lec 5 @ 1:11:30 source B]
  15. Toyota Corolla at 1.5 million EGP, 3× production cost — skimming sustained by brand and resale value. [Lec 5 @ 1:12:00 source B]
  16. Chinese cars at 3–5 million EGP — penetration pricing for new brand entry. [Lec 5 @ 1:13:23 source B]
  17. TV without stand, mobile extended warranty — optional product pricing. [Lec 5 @ 1:23:02 + 1:23:14 source B]
  18. Captive consumables/refills — locking customers into branded refills. [Lec 5 @ 1:24:20 source B]

Common confusions & key distinctions

Pair Skimming Penetration
Initial price High Low
Goal Maximum profit per unit, peel customers willing to pay most Maximum market share fast
Best when Differentiated, premium product; few substitutes; quality signal matters Price-sensitive market; production scales well; want to lock out competitors
Risk Slow uptake; competitors enter under your umbrella Hard to raise price later; brand may seem "cheap"
Pair Cost-based pricing Value-based pricing
Starting point Production cost Customer perception of value
Direction Cost → markup → price (inside-out) Customer value → price → design cost (outside-in)
Risk Ignores demand; may price too low Requires deep customer research
Pair Captive-product Optional-product
Relation Must be used with the main product Can be added to the main product
Example Razor handle + blades; printer + ink TV + wall mount; car + sunroof
Pricing Main product low, consumable high Main product priced separately from each option
Pair By-product Product bundle
Definition Side-effect of main production, low/no value alone Several products combined at a discount
Goal Recover any value beyond storage cost Increase total spend per customer
Example Asphalt from petroleum refining McDonald's combo meal
Pair Fixed cost Variable cost
Behaviour Constant regardless of output Scales with output
Examples Rent, executive salaries, depreciation Raw materials, packaging, hourly wages
Pair Demand curve Price elasticity
Definition Quantity demanded at each price Sensitivity of demand to a 1% price change
Inverse direction Normal: price up → demand down Inelastic = small response; Elastic = big response
Exception Prestige goods (higher price → higher demand) Necessities are inelastic; luxuries with substitutes are elastic

  1. T/F-justify (Question 1 of the final per the template image) likely to test:
  2. "Price is the only element in the marketing mix that produces revenue" → TRUE (Slide 3 verbatim).
  3. "Cost-based pricing is customer driven; value-based pricing is product driven" → FALSE (it's the reverse — Slide 6).
  4. "Market-skimming pricing sets a low initial price to penetrate the market quickly" → FALSE (that's penetration).
  5. "Variable costs do not vary with production level" → FALSE (that's fixed costs — definition trap, Slide 9–10).
  6. "Captive-product pricing involves products that can be used along with the main product" → FALSE (must be used, not can — Slide 26).
  7. "For prestige goods, higher price can equal higher demand" → TRUE (Slide 18).
  8. Short-answer / concept question (Question 2 of the final) likely to test:
  9. List the three pricing strategies (value-based, cost-based, competition-based) and explain when each is used.
  10. Draw or label the Price Ceiling / Price Floor diagram [Figure 10.1].
  11. List the 5 product-mix pricing strategies (product line, optional, captive, by-product, bundle) with one example each.
  12. Explain break-even pricing with the diagram.
  13. Compare skimming vs penetration — definitions + 3 conditions each.
  14. Mini-case (Question 3 of the final) application predictions:
  15. A new Egyptian smartphone brand launches — should it use skimming or penetration? Justify.
  16. A coffee-shop chain is choosing between three product-mix pricing strategies for its food + drink combo — analyse.
  17. The Starbucks case (adapted from Some case studies/Starbucks Case.docx) for Ch.10 will likely combine pricing perception, value-based pricing, and price elasticity of premium-coffee demand.

Chapter 11 — Promotion / Integrated Marketing Communications (Kotler Ch.11)

💡 In Plain English — Chapter 11, told as one story

Same running example: your coffee shop BrewHaus. Ch.8 = what to sell. Ch.10 = how much to charge. Now Ch.11 = how do customers even hear about BrewHaus? Promotion is the noise you make so people show up.

1. What is "promotion"?

Promotion = all the ways a company communicates customer value. It's the 4th P. If Product, Price, and Place are silent — Promotion is the voice. No matter how great your cappuccino is, if nobody knows about it, you sell zero cups.

2. The 5 promotion tools (memorize: A-S-P-P-D)

Five tools in your toolbox — most companies use a mix:

  1. Advertising — paid, non-personal communication through media. BrewHaus example: Facebook ad, billboard, radio spot, Instagram sponsored post. One-way: brand talks, you listen.
  2. Sales Promotion — short-term incentives to push immediate action. BrewHaus: "20% off this weekend," "Buy 1 get 1 free Monday," loyalty stamps. Sales promotion = urgency.
  3. Personal Selling — face-to-face sales conversations with a real human. BrewHaus: your barista upselling "Want to add a pastry?" — that's personal selling. The hallmark = a real conversation that aims to close a sale.
  4. Public Relations (PR) — building good image / earned coverage (not paid ads). BrewHaus: a food blogger writes a free article praising you; you sponsor a local marathon; you donate coffee to a charity. PR builds reputation, doesn't ask for a sale directly.
  5. Direct & Digital Marketing — one-to-one targeted messages. BrewHaus: sending an SMS "Hi Ahmed, your favorite latte is 30% off today," personalized email, WhatsApp message to loyal customers.

Memory trick: Advertising shouts to crowds. Sales promotion bribes them now. Personal selling talks one-on-one. PR earns their respect. Direct/Digital whispers in their ear personally.

3. IMC — Integrated Marketing Communications

Imagine your billboard says "Premium Coffee Experience" but your Facebook ad says "Cheapest Coffee in Town" and your barista calls you a "fast-food coffee chain." Customer gets confused → trust collapses.

IMC = make all 5 tools deliver ONE consistent message. Same voice, same promise, same brand feel everywhere. Slide 12 shows all 5 tools as arrows pointing to ONE center box that says "consistent, clear, compelling message." That's IMC.

4. Push vs. Pull strategy

Two opposite ways to use these tools:

  • Push — you PUSH the product through the channel. Manufacturer convinces wholesalers, wholesalers convince retailers, retailers convince customers. Heavy on personal selling and trade promotions. Example: Procter & Gamble paying supermarkets to display Tide prominently.
  • Pull — you create demand among end customers; they PULL the product through the channel by asking for it. Heavy on advertising + consumer promotions. Example: Coca-Cola TV ads make consumers ask shops for Coke, so shops are forced to stock it.

BrewHaus version: Push = paying delivery apps to feature you. Pull = Instagram ads that make customers ask "where can I find BrewHaus?"

5. How to actually DEVELOP a communication — the 6-step recipe

Don't just "do an ad." Follow this path:

  1. Identify the target audience — who exactly are we talking to? (Young professionals 25-35 in Alexandria.)
  2. Set the communication objective — what do we want them to do? (Awareness → Knowledge → Liking → Preference → Conviction → Purchase — the "buyer-readiness stages.")
  3. Design the message — what to say (content), how to say it (structure), and how to present it (format).
  4. Choose the media — personal (face-to-face, phone, email) vs. non-personal (TV, billboards, social).
  5. Select the message source — who delivers it? (A famous footballer endorsing BrewHaus? A trusted local chef? A regular customer testimonial?)
  6. Collect feedback — did it work? Are sales up? Did awareness increase? Adjust and repeat.

6. Why IMC matters MORE today

Old days: companies controlled the message. Today, customers compare on Google, read TikTok reviews, watch YouTube unboxings. If your IMC isn't tight, social media will catch your contradiction in 5 minutes.

How chapter 11 connects together:

Promotion is the 4th P → use a blend of 5 tools (A-S-P-P-D) → unify them under IMC for ONE consistent message → choose push or pull strategy → develop each communication through 6 steps (audience → objective → message → media → source → feedback). Same BrewHaus, learning to talk to its customers without contradicting itself.

Lecture mapping: Lec 4 covers Ch8 Product (first ~12 minutes) → Ch11 Promotion (from ~12:00 to ~50:00 in source B, ~21:00 to ~50:00 in Lec_4_2 source A) → returns to Ch8 B2B Product topics from ~1:00:00 onward in source B. So the cleanest stretch of Promotion teaching is Lec 4 @ 12:00 – 50:00 in source B (Lec_4_B.md) and Lec 4 @ 21:00 – 32:00 in source A (Lec_4_2.md). Both sources contain Whisper hallucination loops in places (notably Lec_4_B @ 45–58:30 and Lec_4_2 @ 30:00 – 31:00) — those gaps are filled from the cleaner of the two sources.

In one sentence

Promotion (a.k.a. Marketing Communications) is the fourth P of the marketing mix. Slide 3 defines it as four tools verbatim: "the specific blend of advertising, public relations, personal selling, and direct-marketing tools that the company uses to persuasively communicate customer value and build customer relationships" [Slide 3]. Sales promotion is added as a fifth tool on Slide 5, and Slide 12's IMC diagram (Figure 11.1) shows all five tools arrowed into one "consistent, clear, and compelling" centre — that is the IMC definition Slide 11 makes verbatim [Slides 5, 11, 12].

⚠ Exam trap: the verbatim Slide-3 definition lists only four tools (no sales promotion). If a T/F or MCQ quotes the Slide-3 sentence with "sales promotion" inserted, the correct answer is FALSE. Sales promotion is the 5th tool added on Slide 5 and shown in the Slide-12 diagram, not in the Slide-3 verbatim definition.

In plain language

Think of the marketing mix as 4 things you decide about your offer: the Product (what you sell), the Price (what you charge), the Place (how it reaches the buyer), and the Promotion (how you tell the world about it). This chapter is about that last one. Dr. Alaa says the modern word for Promotion is Communication — because today it isn't just "we shout, you listen"; it's a two-way conversation between brand and customer [Lec 4 @ 11:43–12:11, Lec_4_B.md].

The chapter sits on five big ideas:

  1. The Promotion Mix has five tools across Slides 3-8. Slide 3's verbatim definition lists four (advertising, public relations, personal selling, direct-marketing). Slide 5 then introduces sales promotion as a fifth tool, and Slide 12's IMC diagram (Figure 11.1) shows all five arrowed into one centre. Each tool has different strengths — TV ads reach millions but feel impersonal; a sales rep can answer questions but is expensive [Slides 3-8, 12].
  2. The communications landscape has shifted. Consumers are better informed, mass-media is dying, more communication is targeted and digital, and customers are now content-creators themselves [Slide 9-10].
  3. IMC = the company coordinates all its communication channels — TV, social, sales reps, PR releases, in-store displays — to deliver one clear, consistent, compelling message about the brand [Slide 11, 12]. If your TV ad says "premium luxury" but your salesperson talks like a discount peddler, the customer is confused and the message is lost.
  4. Communication itself is a process. A sender encodes a message; the message travels through media with some noise; a receiver decodes it; feedback travels back. Marketers must design every element so the customer's decoded version matches what was encoded [Slide 13].
  5. Developing effective marketing communications — Slide 2's outline calls this section "11.4 Steps in Developing Effective Marketing Communications". The deck's slides cover 4 of the textbook's standard 6 steps: Determine Communication Objectives [Slide 14], Design the Message [Slides 15-16], Choose the Media [Slides 17-21], Select the Message Source [Slide 22]. The other two textbook steps — Identify the target audience (Step 1) and Collect feedback (Step 6) — are not on dedicated slides in this deck. Then comes Budget + Mix tactics — push vs pull [Slides 23-28].

Core concepts

1. The Promotion Mix — Slide 3 defines 4 tools; Slide 5 adds the 5th; Slide 12 frames all 5 as IMC [Slides 3-8 + s.12]

Scope note (exam trap): Slide 3's deck-verbatim definition lists only four tools (Advertising, Public Relations, Personal Selling, Direct Marketing). Sales Promotion is added on Slide 5. The 5-tool framing comes from Slide 12's IMC diagram. If a question quotes Slide 3, the answer is 4; if it asks about the IMC mix, it's 5.

The Slide-3 verbatim definition (memorize exactly as-is): "The promotion mix or marketing communication mix is the specific blend of advertising, public relations, personal selling, and direct-marketing tools that the company uses to persuasively communicate customer value and build customer relationships." [Slide 3 verbatim]

⚠ Source-strict note: Slide 3 names only four tools in its definition. Sales promotion arrives separately on Slide 5 and joins the other four on the Slide-12 IMC diagram (Figure 11.1), making five total promotion-mix tools. A T/F that inserts "sales promotion" into the Slide-3 verbatim sentence is FALSE.

Tool Definition Examples When best
Advertising "Any paid form of non-personal presentation and promotion of ideas, goods, or services by an identified sponsor" [Slide 4] Broadcast (TV/radio), Print, Internet display, Outdoor (billboards) Building awareness in mass markets, brand image, reminding
Sales Promotion "Short-term incentives to encourage the purchase or sale of a product or service" [Slide 5] Discounts, coupons, in-store displays, demonstrations, contests Triggering immediate purchase, clearing inventory, switching customers from competitors
Public Relations (PR) "Building good relations with the company's various publics by obtaining favorable publicity, building up a good corporate image, and handling/heading off unfavorable rumors, stories, and events" [Slide 6] Slide 6 examples: Press releases, Sponsorships, Special events, Web pages [Slide 6]. Dr. Alaa lecture addition: CSR / المسؤولية المجتمعية (Corporate Social Responsibility) as the modern PR umbrella [Lec 4 @ 19:44, Lec_4_B.md] Building credibility, managing crises, reaching skeptical audiences who tune out paid ads
Personal Selling "The personal presentation by the firm's sales force for the purpose of making sales and building customer relationships" [Slide 7] Field sales reps, sales presentations, trade shows, incentive programs High-value / high-complexity B2B sales, custom products, relationship-driven services
Direct Marketing (modern reframing: "Direct & Digital Marketing") "Direct marketing involves making direct connections with carefully targeted individual consumers to both obtain an immediate response and cultivate lasting customer relationships—through the use of direct mail, telephone, direct-response television, e-mail, and the Internet to communicate directly with specific consumers" [Slide 8 verbatim] Slide 8 examples: Catalog, Telemarketing, Kiosks [Slide 8]. Modern lecture additions: email, WhatsApp, SMS, direct-response TV "call now! 299 pounds!" [Lec 4 @ 25:50, 27:21, Lec_4_B.md] Targeted offers, measurable response, building one-to-one data, modern small-budget brands

Dr. Alaa's classroom riffs on the five tools [Lec 4 @ 21:28 – 27:30, Lec_4_B.md]: - PR via sponsorships — Pepsi's "Pepsi Football League" (دوري بيبسي) for school children. "Pepsi is building its name in young kids forever — they grow up memorizing Pepsi, drinking Pepsi" (Arabic: «بيبسي عامل دوري بيبسي للطلاب الملايين... فالطلبة كلها من الشباب الصغير لأن هو بيبني اسمه دايماً عند الشباب الصغير جداً») [Lec 4 @ 21:28, Lec_4_B.md]. This is PR turning a soft-drink brand into a generational identity. - Personal Selling — when it makes sense: banks selling credit cards/Visa, pharmaceutical companies sending medical reps to doctors, insurance ("life insurance sells one-to-one"), real-estate developers ("Sheikh Zayed sales") [Lec 4 @ 22:13–24:45, Lec_4_B.md]. - Pharma split — Dr. Alaa distinguishes two kinds of pharma rep [Lec 4 @ 22:33–23:07, Lec_4_B.md]: (a) the rep who only detail-promotes the drug to the doctor (asking the doctor to prescribe it) — this is promotion / PR, not Personal Selling; (b) the rep who actually moves and sells the boxes to pharmacies — that is Personal Selling. The distinction matters: "selling" requires an actual transaction or close attempt. - Personal selling at retail — Carrefour brand-promoters from L'Oréal / Heinz / etc. standing inside Carrefour to convince shoppers to pick their brand off the shelf [Lec 4 @ 23:52, Lec_4_B.md]. Same role at trade shows (المعارض التجارية). - Sales promotion incentives — the timeshare-pitch trick: "buy today and get a discount; if you leave the showroom we won't honor the discount" [Lec 4 @ 25:15, Lec_4_B.md]. This is sales promotion engineered to compress the buyer's decision time. - Direct marketing in 2026 Egypt — real-estate developers ringing customers daily ("we're building in Sheikh Zayed, are you interested?"); banks emailing credit-card offers; brands DMing on WhatsApp; clubs (Wadi Degla, etc.) employing call-center teams to push memberships [Lec 4 @ 26:18 – 27:46, Lec_4_B.md]. Plus direct-response TV ("call now! only 299 pounds!") [Lec 4 @ 27:04, Lec_4_B.md]. - The new umbrella term — Dr. Alaa says Promotion is the old word; the modern name is Marketing Communication because today the customer talks back [Lec 4 @ 11:43, Lec_4_B.md].

2. The New Marketing Communications Landscape [Slide 9]

Why the textbook re-frames Promotion as Communication:

  • Consumers are better informed — they Google before they buy, read reviews, watch YouTube unboxings.
  • More communication overall — every brand, every retailer, every influencer is in the customer's feed.
  • Less mass marketing — TV's share of attention is collapsing in favor of niche channels.
  • Changing communications technology — social media, streaming, mobile, AI-driven personalization.

[Slide 10] makes the implication explicit: "Many large advertisers are shifting their advertising budgets away from network television in favor of more targeted, cost-effective, interactive, and engaging media." The shift is sometimes called the shifting marketing communications model.

3. Integrated Marketing Communications (IMC) [Slides 11-12]

Textbook definition (memorize verbatim): "Integrated marketing communications is the integration by the company of its communication channels to deliver a clear, consistent, and compelling message about the organization and its brands." [Slide 11]

The diagram on Slide 12 shows the five promotion tools (Advertising / Sales Promotion / Public Relations / Personal Selling / Direct Marketing) all arrowed toward a single red box in the centre that reads "Consistent, clear, and compelling company and brand messages". The teaching point: if any one tool sends a different message, the whole communications system loses credibility [Slide 12].

Dr. Alaa's gloss [Lec 4 @ 40:00, Lec_4_B.md]: «يجب أن يكون الرسائل التي أرسلها متسقة مع بعضها البعض، سواء كانت تأتي من الـ personal selling، أو من الـ sales promotion، أو من الأدوات، كلهم يجب أن يكونوا يمشون في نفس الاتجاه.» — All messages, regardless of which tool they come from, must move in the same direction.

4. The Communication Process Model (9 elements) [Slide 13]

A marketer cannot design good communication without understanding how communication works. The Kotler diagram has 9 elements grouped into two overlapping circles ("Sender's field of experience" overlapping "Receiver's field of experience"):

  1. Sender — the party communicating (the company/brand).
  2. Encoding — the process of putting thought into symbolic form (words, pictures, jingles).
  3. Message — the actual set of symbols transmitted.
  4. Media — the communication channel through which the message travels (TV, magazine, Instagram, billboard, sales rep).
  5. Decoding — the receiver assigning meaning to the encoded symbols.
  6. Receiver — the consumer who reads/sees the message.
  7. Response — the reactions of the receiver after exposure.
  8. Feedback — the part of the response the sender can observe.
  9. Noise — unplanned static / distortion during the process that causes the decoded message to differ from what was encoded.

Why the overlap matters: effective communication only happens within the shared field of experience between sender and receiver. A McDonald's "I'm lovin' it" jingle works in markets where English emotional ads resonate; it fails when culture/language don't share that field [Slide 13 — annotation about applying model to McDonald's].

⚠ Source-strict note: the Marlboro-Man-rebrand and Dasani-bottle-color encoding/decoding cases that earlier circulated for this section came from a NotebookLM-synthesized transcript, not from the Whisper-captured Dr. Alaa lectures (grep -i "marlboro\|dasani" transcripts/Lec_4*.md → zero hits). Per the source-strict rule they have been removed. If Dr. Alaa is heard repeating either in a re-listen, re-add with the real Whisper timestamp.

Safe exam framing of the encoding/decoding lesson (from Slide-13 itself): effective communication only happens within the shared field of experience between sender and receiver. If a customer "wrongly" decodes the brand's intended message, the brand must redesign the encoding — different symbols, different visual cues, different cultural framing — until the decoded version matches intent. (Slide 13 illustrates this with McDonald's "I'm lovin' it" annotation.)

5. Steps in Developing Effective Marketing Communications [Slide 2 outline §11.4 — deck slides 14-22 cover Steps 2-5]

Slide 2's outline names this section "11.4 Steps in Developing Effective Marketing Communications". The Kotler textbook recipe has 6 steps, but this deck only has dedicated slides for Steps 2-5 (Objectives, Message, Media, Source). Steps 1 and 6 are textbook-standard but not on the slides — keep them in mind for short-answer questions, but the verbatim slide-anchored exam traps will come from Steps 2-5.

Step 1 — Identify the target audience (textbook-standard, not on a dedicated slide in this deck). Decide who you're talking to (current users? prospects? decision-makers? influencers within the household?). The audience determines everything downstream.

Step 2 — Determine the communication objectives [Slides 14, the Buyer-Readiness Stages diagram on its companion slide]. The Kotler Buyer-Readiness Stages are the canonical 6-stage funnel a customer travels:

Awareness → Knowledge → Liking → Preference → Conviction → Purchase

The communicator's job is to figure out which stage the target audience is in and design messaging to push them to the next stage. A new brand might focus on Awareness ads; a niche brand that customers like but don't trust enough to buy might focus on Conviction (testimonials, guarantees). [Slide 14 — Figure 11.3 Buyer-Readiness Stages]

A simpler classical model that Slide 15 highlights is the AIDA model: "Get Attention, Hold Interest, Arouse Desire, Obtain Action" [Slide 15].

Key distinction (exam trap): Buyer-Readiness Stages has 6 steps; AIDA has 4. Both describe the same customer journey from "knows nothing" to "buys". Don't confuse them.

Step 3 — Design the message [Slides 15-16]. Two design decisions:

  • What to say (message content) [Slide 16] — the appeal or theme. Three types:
  • Rational appeal — relates to the audience's self-interest (this product saves you money / works better / lasts longer). Used in B2B, durables, financial services.
  • Emotional appeal — stirs positive emotions (love, joy, pride, humor) or negative ones (fear, guilt, shame) to motivate a purchase. Used in cosmetics, soft drinks, luxury, life insurance.
  • Moral appeal — directed at the audience's sense of right and proper. Used in cause campaigns, CSR, environmental messaging.
  • How to say it (message structure and format) [Slide 15] — verbal phrasing, visual layout, music, length, sequence.

Step 4 — Choose the media [Slides 17-21]. Two big categories:

  • Personal communication channels [Slides 17-19] — two or more people communicate directly (face-to-face, phone, mail, email, internet chat). Three sub-types based on who controls the message [Slide 18]:
  • Company-controlled — company salespeople contacting buyers.
  • Independent experts — consultants, reviewers, doctors making recommendations.
  • Word-of-mouth (WOM) — neighbors, friends, family talking about the brand.
  • Personal communication is effective because it allows personal addressing + immediate feedback [Slide 18].
  • Opinion leaders [Slide 19] = people within a reference group who (because of special skills, knowledge, personality, or charisma) exert social influence on others. Today's social-media influencers are the new opinion leaders.
  • Buzz marketing [Slide 19] = cultivating opinion leaders and getting them to spread your message to their community (modern: influencer marketing, brand-ambassador programs).
  • Non-personal communication channels [Slides 20-21] — media that carry messages without personal contact or feedback. Three sub-types:
  • Major media [Slide 21] — print (newspapers, magazines), broadcast (TV, radio), display (billboards, signs, posters), and online media.
  • Atmospheres [Slide 21] — designed environments that create or reinforce buying leanings (the smell of bread in a supermarket, the lighting of a luxury watch boutique, the soundtrack at Apple Store).
  • Events [Slide 21] — staged occurrences that communicate messages to target audiences: press conferences, grand openings, exhibits, public tours, sport sponsorships.

Step 5 — Select the message source [Slide 22]. Slide-22 verbatim: "The message's impact on the target audience is affected by how the audience views the communicator." The slide then lists two categories of source: Celebrities (Athletes, Entertainers) and Professionals (Health-care providers) [Slide 22 verbatim]. A toothpaste ad delivered by a dentist (a Professional) hits differently than the same script delivered by an Entertainer.

Source-strict note: Slide 22 does NOT list "credibility / expertise / trustworthiness / attractiveness / similarity" as named attributes — those are general Kotler-textbook source-effectiveness criteria. For exam answers tied to Slide 22, stick to the two slide categories (Celebrities + Professionals) and the verbatim "how the audience views the communicator" phrase.

Step 6 — Collect feedback (textbook-standard closing step, not on a dedicated slide in this deck — but conceptually maps to the Feedback element of the 9-element Communication Process diagram on [Slide 13]). Measure recall, attitude, behavior, and sales response; feed the data back into the next campaign cycle.

6. Setting the Total Promotion Budget and Mix — budgeting methods [Slides 23-27]

Scope note: the slide-deck banner is "Setting the Total Promotion Budget and Mix" (Topic Outline §11.5). The portal splits this into §6 (budgeting methods) and §7 (mix shaping) for clarity.

Four classical methods, each with a definition and a weakness to memorize:

Method Definition Weakness
Affordable [Slide 24] Sets the budget at whatever the company can afford after other expenses "Ignores the effects of promotion on sales" [Slide 24 verbatim] — promotion is treated as cost, not as investment driving growth
Percentage-of-sales [Slide 25] Sets the budget at a certain percentage of current or forecasted sales (or unit sales price) "Wrongly views sales as the cause rather than the result of promotion" [Slide 25 verbatim] — counter-cyclical (budget shrinks when sales fall, exactly when more promotion is needed)
Competitive-parity [Slide 26] Sets the budget to match competitor outlays Represents industry standards and avoids promotion wars [Slide 26], but assumes competitors know what you should spend — they don't
Objective-and-task [Slide 27] Sets the budget based on what the firm wants to accomplish with promotion. Three sub-steps: (a) define promotion objectives, (b) determine tasks to achieve them, (c) estimate costs [Slide 27] Hardest to apply (requires careful objective-setting and accurate cost estimation), but logically the best because it ties spending to outcomes

Exam-style mnemonic: think of the methods on a "logic ladder" — Affordable (worst, no logic) → Percentage-of-sales (mechanical) → Competitive-parity (copycat) → Objective-and-task (best, goal-driven). The exam will likely ask which is most defensible strategically → answer: objective-and-task.

7. Shaping the Overall Promotion Mix — Push vs Pull [Slide 28 (Figure 11.4) + Kotler textbook for product-type and PLC factors]

Scope note: Slide 28 itself only contains the Push/Pull diagram. The discussion of "type of product/market" (consumer vs B2B) and "product life-cycle stage" below is Kotler-textbook material added for exam completeness — NOT on Slide 28.

Once the budget is set, the company decides how to split it across the 5 tools (Advertising / Sales Promotion / PR / Personal Selling / Direct). Two textbook framings:

Push vs Pull strategy [Slide 28 — Figure 11.4]:

  • Push strategy — the producer "pushes" the product through the channel. Producer → Retailers/Wholesalers → Consumers. Producer focuses on trade promotion (incentives for retailers) and personal selling to channel members; retailers then promote to consumers. Used heavily in B2B and complex industrial goods.
  • Pull strategy — the producer promotes directly to final consumers, creating demand that "pulls" the product through the channel. Producer → Consumers (advertising, consumer promotion) → demand pressures Retailers → Retailers order from Producer. Used by consumer-brand giants (Pepsi, Coca-Cola, P&G) where consumer demand forces retailers to stock the brand.

Most companies use a combination of push and pull [Slide 28 caption]. Pepsi runs a Pepsi Football League (pull-via-PR, building consumer pull) and offers retailers cooler racks + volume discounts (push-via-trade-promotion).

Choosing the mix also depends on the type of product/market: - Consumer goods → heavy advertising + sales promotion (pull-heavy). - B2B / industrial goods → heavy personal selling + sales promotion (push-heavy, because buyers are fewer and more rational). - Stage of the product life cycle — introduction phase wants Awareness (advertising + PR); maturity wants Reminder ads + sales promotion to defend share; decline wants minimal communication.

8. Dr. Alaa's modern direct/digital context (lecture-only color, no slide anchor)

This section captures Dr. Alaa's in-class commentary that extends the Slide-8 Direct Marketing tool into today's algorithm-driven environment. None of it is on a slide — cite Whisper, not the deck, when reusing this in exam answers.

The Carpet/Curtain example (Dr. Alaa's class story) [Lec 4 @ 33:19 + 38:21, Lec_4_B.md / Lec_4_2.md]:

An individual never types "curtains." But Facebook sees their friends discussing interior design, the phone's location tracking shows a recent visit to a furniture / carpet (السجاد) outlet, and the platform infers the consumer is "finishing an apartment" — and pushes carpet / curtain (الستار) ads. The need was inferred from community + location signals, not from a search query.

The lesson: in the modern direct-marketing environment, marketers no longer wait for the customer to search. The platform predicts the customer's stage in the buyer-readiness funnel and pushes the right communication at the right moment.

Platform Logic — Facebook vs TikTok [Lec 4 @ 36:24, Lec_4_B.md]:

Dimension Facebook TikTok
Targeting basis Community-based — the platform looks at who your friends are, what they like, what they buy, then projects those signals onto you Individualized — the platform looks only at your watch history, dwell time, swipes, and pauses, then builds a unique digital identity per user
Implication for marketers Reach groups via the social graph (target a community, the message diffuses to its members through likes/shares) Reach individuals via the algorithmic feed (the message must match the individual's behavior signature)
Best for Brand awareness within identifiable interest communities; viral PR; family/group purchases Hyper-personalized direct response; impulse purchases; products with strong visual hook

Exam-friendly framing: this is the modern version of Personal vs Non-personal communication channels. Facebook re-creates word-of-mouth at scale through social-graph cues; TikTok re-creates one-to-one marketing through behavioral data. Both extend Direct Marketing (Slide 8's deck term) into the algorithmic age, but they target very different units (group vs individual). For exam wording, stick to the slide term "Direct marketing"; bring in the Facebook/TikTok contrast only with a Lec-4 citation.

9. Socially Responsible Marketing Communications [Topic Outline §11.6]

This appears on Slide 2's outline (Topic 11.6) but does not get a dedicated content slide in our deck — Dr. Alaa is expected to discuss it verbally if at all. Standard textbook content includes: - Truth in advertising (no false or deceptive claims). - Avoiding "bait-and-switch" tactics. - Children-and-vulnerable-audience protections. - B2B ethics — bribery laws, anti-kickback rules. - Direct-marketing data privacy and "do-not-call" / opt-out rights.

Light treatment — if it appears on the final it will be a one-line definition T/F ("Marketing communications must be truthful and not deceive consumers — TRUE") rather than a deep essay.

Dr. Alaa's in-class examples for Ch.11

These are the brand vignettes you can reuse verbatim in T/F or short-essay answers:

  • Pepsi Football League (دوري بيبسي) — PR / sponsorship building generational brand loyalty among Egyptian schoolchildren. "بيبسي عامل دوري بيبسي للطلاب الملايين... فالطلبة كلها من الشباب الصغير لأن هو بيبني اسمه دايماً" [Lec 4 @ 21:28, Lec_4_B.md].
  • Pepsi vs Coca-Cola loyalty — "اللي بيشرب بيبسي بيشرب بيبسي، اللي بيشرب كوكا بيشرب كوكا" — illustrating brand-conviction stage of the buyer-readiness funnel [Lec 4 @ 50:48, Lec_4_2.md].
  • Skechers vs Crocs / "Sir Charles" trick (سيراشورس / حسن عبدالله) — the same ad campaign reused for a new launch by a competitor [Lec 4 @ 45:29, Lec_4_B.md] — illustrating message-copying and importance of distinctive messaging.
  • Pharma sales rep split — "detailing" reps vs distribution reps; only distribution counts as Personal Selling [Lec 4 @ 22:33 – 23:07, Lec_4_B.md].
  • Carrefour brand-promoters / trade-show personnel — Personal Selling in retail context [Lec 4 @ 23:52, Lec_4_B.md].
  • Real-estate developer cold-calling — "We're building in Sheikh Zayed, are you interested?" — modern Egyptian direct marketing [Lec 4 @ 26:18, Lec_4_B.md].
  • Wadi Degla / sports clubs phone outreach — call centers selling memberships, direct-marketing in services [Lec 4 @ 27:46, Lec_4_B.md].
  • Direct-response TV — "call now on this number, only 299 pounds" mid-2000s Egyptian TV ads [Lec 4 @ 27:04, Lec_4_B.md].
  • Timeshare sales-promotion trick — discount valid only inside the showroom, expires the moment you walk out [Lec 4 @ 25:15, Lec_4_B.md].
  • Public Relations / SAR (Corporate Social Responsibility) framing — Dr. Alaa describes the modern term CSR (سي إس آر — المسؤولية المجتمعية) as the umbrella for community-engagement PR [Lec 4 @ 22:08, Lec_4_2.md].
  • Skechers "Sale!" stampede — short-term incentive driving immediate purchase, classic sales promotion [Lec 4 @ 21:10 – 21:25, Lec_4_2.md].

In-class case anchors — group-project companies assigned by Dr. Alaa

For the 25-mark group project, Dr. Alaa assigned five companies that double as in-class study anchors for the promotion / IMC discussion. Netflix is named at [Lec 4 @ 10:33 + 10:43, Lec_4_2.md] and Defacto at [Lec 4 @ 13:36, Lec_4_2.md]:

  • Group 4 — Netflix (Digital Services / Value Innovation): textbook case of pull marketing — Netflix builds consumer demand directly through algorithmic recommendations + binge-worthy content + word-of-mouth advocacy, then channel partners (Smart TV makers, ISPs, hotel chains) are forced to make Netflix easily accessible because consumers expect it. Also a digital-identity case study — Netflix's recommendation engine builds a per-user digital persona, the same logic as the Carpet/Curtain example.
  • Group 5 — Defacto (Retail Fashion / Sales Promotion): textbook case of sales-promotion-heavy IMC — perpetual "30% off", "buy one get one", "weekend flash sale" pricing as a promotion-mix lever to trigger immediate purchase. Defacto trades long-term brand-building for short-term volume, the trade-off Slide 5 warns against.

These two are the most relevant Ch.11 anchors among the five (the others — IKEA, Sharp/El Araby, Nvidia — are better Ch.8 Product / Augmented Product anchors). Expect a possible mini-case in the final's Q3 framed around one of these brands.

Common confusions & key distinctions

A B The distinction
Advertising PR Advertising = paid + non-personal, brand controls the message. PR = earned, third-party credibility, brand controls but cannot guarantee the message (the journalist or influencer decides).
Advertising Sales Promotion Advertising = long-term brand-building, builds preference over time. Sales Promotion = short-term incentives, triggers immediate behavior.
Personal Selling Direct Marketing Personal Selling = interpersonal (face-to-face by a sales force). Direct Marketing = direct connection via tech (mail / phone / email / internet) — can be one-to-many automated. Personal Selling builds relationships through human-to-human contact; Direct Marketing scales by tech.
PR / "detailing" Personal Selling Pharma reps detailing drugs to doctors = PR/promotion (no transaction). Distribution reps moving boxes to pharmacies = Personal Selling (actual sales role). Dr. Alaa's specific distinction [Lec 4 @ 22:33, Lec_4_B.md].
AIDA (4 steps) Buyer-Readiness Stages (6 steps) Same journey, different granularity. AIDA: Attention → Interest → Desire → Action. Buyer-Readiness: Awareness → Knowledge → Liking → Preference → Conviction → Purchase. The 6-stage model adds "Knowledge" (cognitive), "Preference" (vs competitors), and "Conviction" (trust to actually buy).
Rational appeal Emotional appeal Rational = self-interest (saves money, performs better). Emotional = stirs feelings (joy, fear, love, pride). Moral = right-vs-wrong. Most modern ads blend the three.
Personal communication Non-personal communication Personal = two-way (face-to-face / phone / email chat) with feedback. Non-personal = one-way (mass media / atmosphere / event) without direct feedback.
Push Pull Push = producer pushes to channel; channel pushes to consumer (trade promotion + personal selling heavy). Pull = producer creates consumer demand that pulls product through channel (advertising + consumer promotion heavy).
Affordable method Objective-and-task method Affordable budgets backwards from leftover cash, ignoring sales effects. Objective-and-task budgets forwards from goals → tasks → costs, the most strategically defensible.
Encoding Decoding Encoding = sender turning idea into symbols (writing the ad, recording the jingle). Decoding = receiver interpreting those symbols. Mismatch = communication failure.
Noise Feedback Noise = unintended interference distorting the message in transit. Feedback = the portion of the receiver's response the sender can observe (sales lift, survey scores, click-throughs).
Opinion Leader Buzz Marketing Opinion Leader = the person with social influence. Buzz Marketing = the tactic of cultivating those people to amplify your message.
CSR / Sponsorship Advertising CSR / Sponsorship sits under PR (earned credibility, indirect benefit). Advertising = paid, direct, identified-sponsor message.

  4. The 4 budgeting methods + their weaknesses appear regularly in Kotler test banks — expect one MCQ on "which method ignores the effect of promotion on sales?" (answer: Affordable) or "which method wrongly views sales as the cause of promotion?" (answer: Percentage-of-sales).
