A company should consider it when: (1) current markets are saturated and existing-product growth is exhausted (Market Penetration ceiling reached); (2) the existing industry is in long-term decline and the firm needs to redeploy capital; (3) the firm has surplus cash and a credible expansion target — often the route is acquisition rather than organic build (PepsiCo acquired KFC, Pizza Hut, and Costa Coffee to diversify out of pure beverages). Risks include: brand dilution if the new business contradicts core positioning; managerial overstretch; capital tied up in unfamiliar competition. Mitigation: enter via acquisition rather than greenfield; preserve management of the acquired business; protect the core brand with a separate brand identity for the new venture. *

[Slide: Ansoff Matrix] [Lec 3-2 @ 31:28]

Ch.7 Essay Q92 [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — STP framework]
Apply the four-step STP framework to the launch of a new Egyptian-built smartphone targeting young urban professionals.
Why:

Model answer: Step 1 — Segmentation. Slice the market by psychographic (lifestyle: tech-savvy professionals) + demographic (age 22-35, middle/upper-middle income, Cairo/Alexandria), with a secondary behavioral cut for heavy data users / mobile-photography enthusiasts. Step 2 — Targeting. Use concentrated marketing rather than mass marketing: focus on the urban-professional segment with one carefully designed offer. The five targeting factors (resources, product variability, market variability, PLC stage, competitor strategy) all point to concentration since the firm is new and resources are limited. Step 3 — Differentiation. Engineer a meaningful, distinctive, communicable advantage — likely camera quality + a regional service network competitors lack. Step 4 — Positioning. Write the positioning statement: "To young Egyptian professionals who need a reliable productivity-and-photo phone, our brand is the locally-built camera-phone that captures DSLR-quality images at half the iPhone price, with same-day local service support." *

[Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — STP framework]

Ch.7 Essay Q93 [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — Differentiation & Positioning] · [Lec 5 @ 22:00 source B]
Compare Differentiation and Positioning. Give one example of a brand that has differentiated effectively but positioned poorly, and explain what corrective action you would recommend.
Why:

Model answer: Differentiation = the actual engineered differences (features, design, service, channel, image). Positioning = the mental place the brand occupies in the target customer's mind. The two are linked but distinct — differentiation is what the firm DOES; positioning is what the customer THINKS. A brand can be technically differentiated yet poorly positioned if it never successfully communicates the difference. Example: Many Egyptian compound developers have actually differentiated on amenities (security, parking, services), but some have positioned poorly by failing to build a distinctive identity — they look like commodities to buyers. Dr. Alaa's Asian-themed compound case showed the fix: the developer brought a marketing firm in, built an exotic Asian identity (elephant statue at the entrance, themed advertising, distinctive flavour), and the next phase sold sight-unseen. The corrective action is consistent IMC: pick one clear point-of-difference and broadcast it through every channel. *

[Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — Differentiation & Positioning] [Lec 5 @ 22:00 source B]

Ch.8 Essay Q94 [Slide 21] · [Lec 4 @ 1:12:54-1:13:25]
Give examples of effective packaging across different product categories, and discuss why packaging is "very important" for some categories more than others.
Why:

Model answer: Packaging is designing and producing the container or wrapper for a product — Dr. Alaa stressed it as "very important" because it can make customers buy on quality perception alone. Categories where packaging dominates: (1) Children's products — colourful character-led wrappers create pester-power purchase pressure (Kinder, Disney-licensed snacks). (2) Perfumes — the bottle shape and weight carry most of the brand equity (Chanel No. 5's bottle is the brand). (3) Cosmetics — lipstick cases, mascara wands, foundation jars; the look on the bathroom shelf is the value (MAC, L'Oréal). (4) Premium food and chocolates — gold wrappers, embossed boxes (Godiva, Lindt). Packaging matters less for convenience commodities (bottled water — generic) and industrial inputs (steel coils, raw materials). The marketer's job is to invest in packaging proportional to its purchase-decision weight in the target category — over-engineering generics wastes money, under-investing in cosmetics destroys brand value. *

[Slide 21] [Lec 4 @ 1:12:54-1:13:25]

Ch.10 Essay Q95 [Slide 22-23] · [Lec 5 @ 1:10:30 source B]
Give advice to an Egyptian company launching a premium product on whether to use skimming or penetration pricing, with examples and conditions.
Why:

Model answer: The choice depends on the conditions of each strategy. Advise skimming (high initial price) when: (1) the product's quality and image support the price; (2) enough early-adopter buyers value the product at that price; (3) small-volume production doesn't destroy the cost advantage; (4) competitors cannot enter easily. Egyptian example: Toyota Corolla holds its price at ~1.5 million EGP (3× production cost) because Egyptian customers value Toyota's reliability and resale value — a sustained-skimming model. Advise penetration (low initial price) when: (1) the market is price-sensitive; (2) production and distribution costs fall meaningfully with volume; (3) low prices will hold competitors out long enough to build share. Egyptian example: Chinese auto brands entering Egypt at 3-5 million EGP (well below comparable Mercedes) — the brand is new and unknown, so the low price is the lure. Plan to migrate from penetration to skimming once the brand grows. *

[Slide 22-23] [Lec 5 @ 1:10:30 source B] [Lec 5 @ 1:13:23 source B]

Ch.11 Essay Q96 [Slide 28] · [Lec 4 @ 21:28 source B]
Discuss the choice between Push and Pull promotion strategies and recommend which one fits an Egyptian consumer-goods brand vs an Egyptian industrial supplier.
Why:

Model answer: Push = the producer pushes the product through the channel to consumers, using trade promotion (discounts to retailers) + personal selling to channel members. Pull = the producer creates consumer demand directly through advertising and consumer promotion, so consumers force retailers to stock the brand. For an Egyptian consumer-goods brand (e.g., a new biscuit brand competing with Chipsy), recommend pull-heavy: heavy TV/Instagram/influencer advertising plus sales promotion at point-of-purchase will create consumer pull that forces wholesalers and supermarkets to carry the brand. For an Egyptian industrial supplier (e.g., steel rebar producer selling to construction contractors), recommend push-heavy: personal selling to procurement managers + trade promotions (volume discounts, credit terms) + technical sales support. Most companies use a combination of both — Pepsi runs the Pepsi Football League (pull-via-PR building consumer pull) AND offers retailers cooler racks + volume discounts (push-via-trade-promotion). *

[Slide 28] [Lec 4 @ 21:28 source B]

Ch.14 Essay Q97 [Slide 14 outline only — extracted from Ch.14 textbook section 12.4] · [Lec 6 @ 50:30-52:00]
Discuss when to use intensive, selective, and exclusive distribution. Give an Egyptian-context example for each.
Why:

Model answer: Intensive distribution — stock the product in as many outlets as possible. Use for low-value, high-volume convenience products with strong consumer pull. Egyptian example: Coca-Cola, Chipsy, cigarettes — available at every kiosk, supermarket, and corner shop in Cairo. Selective distribution — use more than one but fewer than all intermediaries. Use for mid-tier shopping products where customers compare brands and retailer reputation matters. Egyptian example: Samsung TVs at Carrefour, B-Tech, and CompuMe — selective coverage that screens for quality dealers without going exclusive. Exclusive distribution — only one intermediary per territory carries the brand. Use for specialty / luxury products where image control, after-sale service, and dealer training matter most. Egyptian example: Mercedes-Benz Egypt's exclusive dealership network (one main authorised dealer); Rolex boutiques. The trade-off is coverage vs control: intensive maximises coverage and minimises control; exclusive maximises control and minimises coverage.

[Slide 14 outline only — extracted from Ch.14 textbook section 12.4] [Lec 6 @ 50:30-52:00]

📄 Mock Final Exam Paper

E. Mock Final Exam Paper (template-faithful)

Sit it under exam conditions — 3 hours, closed book, paper or full-screen — and self-grade against the model answers. Hit-rate expectation: if the midterm portal's 23/23 calibration holds, ≥80% of structure and ~65% of content here will match what Dr. Alaa hands you.

Alexandria University — Faculty of Business — EMBA Program Course: Strategic Marketing — Final Exam — May 2026 Instructor: Dr. Alaa Elgharbawy Duration: 3 hours Total Marks: 80

Answer all questions. Same convention as the International Marketing final.

Question 1: (20 marks) — Determine whether each statement is True or False, and justify your answer in 1–2 sentences. (2 marks each)

1. The marketing mix includes Product, Price, Place, and Packaging — together these are known as the four Ps of marketing.

FALSE — 1 mark; justification — 1 mark. The fourth P is Promotion, not Packaging. The 4Ps are Product, Price, Place, Promotion. Packaging is a sub-decision within Product. [Slide: Kotler Ch.1 — Marketing Mix; Ch.8 packaging] [Lec 4 @ 1:12:57]

2. Skimming pricing is best used when a company wants to maximize market share quickly by setting a low introductory price.

FALSE. That describes penetration pricing. Skimming sets a high initial price to extract maximum revenue from early adopters first, then lowers price progressively (iPhone launch, Mercedes new-model launch). Penetration uses low initial price for fast market-share capture (Chinese cars, Xiaomi). [Slide: Kotler Ch.10 — New-Product Pricing] [Lec 5 — skimming-vs-penetration block]

3. A Vertical Marketing System (VMS) takes exactly three forms: corporate, contractual, and administered.

TRUE. Dr. Alaa enumerated this verbatim: "VMS ده بياخد ثلاثة اشكال — اما corporate marketing system … او contractual … او administered." Corporate = single ownership integrates production + distribution (Zara). Contractual = independent firms united by contract (McDonald's franchise, Khalil pharmacy chain). Administered = no ownership/contract, but one dominant party coordinates (P&G in supermarkets, Carrefour over suppliers). [Slide: Kotler Ch.14 — VMS] [Lec 6 @ 1:05:18]

4. Personal selling and direct marketing are essentially the same promotion-mix tool because both involve one-to-one communication with prospects.

FALSE. Personal selling requires face-to-face interpersonal interaction. Direct marketing reaches a specific customer without face-to-face contact — via email, mail, phone, SMS, or digital channels. Both are one-to-one in target, but the delivery channel is the discriminator. [Slide: Kotler Ch.11 — Promotion Mix] [Lec 4 @ 26:45]

5. A Distinctive Competency is a strength that competitors can easily replicate, which gives an organization a temporary advantage.

FALSE. Distinctive Competency = a strength competitors CANNOT easily replicate. It must MATCH the market's Success Requirements to convert an environmental opportunity into an organizational one. Crocs had a distinctive competency in comfort material until Skechers copied it — at that point it stopped being distinctive. Apple AirPods made wireless headphones the default — that's sustainable. [Slide: Kerin Ch.1 s.18] [Lec 1 @ 2:18:49 — midterm portal]

6. Captive product pricing means selling the main product at a high margin and selling the captive accessory at a low margin.

FALSE — the logic is reversed. Captive pricing sells the main product at a LOW margin (sometimes a loss leader) and the captive accessory or supply at a HIGH margin. Gillette razor cheap, blades expensive. Nespresso machine cheap, pods expensive. HP printer cheap, ink cartridges expensive. The captive accessory is mandatory for the main product to function, so the customer is locked into the high-margin supply. [Slide: Kotler Ch.10 — Product-Mix Pricing]

7. The societal marketing concept seeks to balance customer wants, company profits, and society's long-run welfare.

TRUE. The Societal Marketing Concept balances THREE interests: (1) customer wants (short-term), (2) company profits (medium-term), and (3) society's long-run welfare. Patagonia (environment), Tesla (sustainability), anti-smoking campaigns are textbook examples. [Slide: Kotler Ch.1 s.10 — Societal Marketing] [midterm portal §2 — recycled exactly on midterm]

8. Building up a good corporate image is the primary goal of Sales Promotion.

FALSE. Building good corporate image is the goal of Public Relations (PR), not Sales Promotion. Sales Promotion = short-term incentives to encourage purchase (coupons, discounts, contests). PR = building image and managing publicity — "صورة زينية للشركة" as Dr. Alaa put it verbatim. [Slide: Kotler Ch.11 — 5 Promotion Tools] [Lec 4 @ 20:26]

9. Intensive distribution means selling a product through only one outlet per geographic area, such as a single exclusive dealer.

FALSE. That describes Exclusive distribution (BMW, Rolex). Intensive distribution = sell EVERYWHERE possible (Coca-Cola in every kiosk, Pepsi in every store). Selective = sell at SOME pre-screened outlets (Samsung TVs at chosen electronics retailers). Exclusive = ONE outlet per area, often with non-compete clauses. [Slide: Kotler Ch.14 — Distribution Intensity]

10. Customer satisfaction depends on the product's perceived value relative to the buyer's expectations.

TRUE. Textbook definition. If perceived value ≥ expectations → satisfaction (or delight). If perceived value < expectations → dissatisfaction. The four customer-satisfaction drivers Dr. Alaa emphasized: (a) cost of obtaining, (b) expectations, (c) cost of competing products, (d) cost of the lost experience. The relativity to expectations is the key. [Slide: Kotler Ch.1 — Customer Satisfaction] (Was the partial miss on the midterm — see CALIBRATION.md.)

Question 2: (20 marks) — Applied STP on products.

For each of the following 5 products, identify in a clear 3-cell answer per product:

  1. The segmentation base the company used (or should use) to define its market — geographic, demographic, psychographic, behavioral, or a combination.
  2. The targeting strategy that was used (or should be used) — undifferentiated / differentiated / concentrated / micromarketing.
  3. Whether the differentiation is differentiated or undifferentiated.

Justify each answer in 1–2 sentences. (4 marks per product × 5 products = 20 marks)

Note: These products are anchored in Dr. Alaa's verbatim in-class examples and the calibration-proven midterm test-bank style. Expect 2–3 to recur on the actual exam.

Products to analyze:

(a) LC Waikiki — Turkish clothing chain offering affordable apparel for middle-class consumers in Egyptian cities.

(b) Nido Nestle — full milk-powder brand offering different products for different age groups (Nido 1+, Nido 3+, Nido 5+, Nido Junior, Nido Adult).

(c) LADA (the Soviet era) — offered one car model sold to everyone, every age, every income segment, with no variants.

(d) Apple iPhone Pro Max — the top tier of Apple's smartphone line, sold to early-adopter premium-segment consumers worldwide.

(e) Samsung Galaxy line (A-series + S-series + Z-Fold/Flip + tablets) — a full portfolio of devices for every price tier and use case in the Egyptian market.

Model answer — full table:

Product (a) Segmentation base (b) Targeting strategy (c) Differentiation Justification
LC Waikiki in Egypt Demographic (middle income / A/B/C class) + Geographic (Egyptian urban cities) Concentrated Differentiated LC Waikiki picked ONE segment (middle-class urban Egyptians) and tailored its product + price + store positioning to that segment. The brand is clearly differentiated within that segment vs Zara (higher price) and local mass-market shops (lower quality). It is NOT mass-market (it ignores rural and luxury segments). [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — Targeting strategies] [midterm T/F #1 — verbatim recycled]
Nido Nestle Demographic (age-based — 1+, 3+, 5+, Junior, Adult) Differentiated Differentiated Nido offers MULTIPLE products for MULTIPLE age segments, each with tailored formulation, packaging, and price. This is the textbook differentiated targeting pattern. Note: a common trap is to call this "behavioral" segmentation — but the discriminator is AGE, which is a demographic base. [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — Segmentation Bases] [midterm T/F #2 verbatim — FALSE because it's demographic, not behavioral]
LADA (Soviet era) None / mass-market — no segmentation base applied Undifferentiated (mass marketing) Undifferentiated LADA's strategy was "one product for everyone." This is the textbook example of undifferentiated (mass) marketing. Common trap: calling this "concentrated" because it's only one product — but concentrated means ONE NARROW segment with tailored offer, not whole market with one offer. [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — Targeting strategies] [midterm T/F #3 verbatim — FALSE because it's undifferentiated, not concentrated]
Apple iPhone Pro Max Psychographic (premium-aspirational, status-seeking) + Behavioral (early adopters, brand-loyal) + Demographic (high income A class) Concentrated within Apple's lineup Differentiated (highly) Pro Max is targeted at one narrow segment — premium early adopters — with a tailored, defensible price point. Differentiation rests on camera, materials, status, ecosystem lock-in. NOT undifferentiated (would lose to Samsung A-series at the low end). [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — STP] [Lec 4 — Apple example in product line discussion]
Samsung Galaxy line (all tiers) Demographic (income tiers A→D) + Psychographic (premium / mid / budget) + Behavioral (foldable users, gamers, basic users) Differentiated (multiple-segment) Differentiated Samsung sells to MULTIPLE segments simultaneously — A-series for mass-budget, S-series for premium, Fold/Flip for novelty-seekers, tablets for productivity users — with DIFFERENT product + price + positioning per segment. This is the textbook differentiated targeting pattern, and also a product-mix breadth play (Ch.8). [Slide: Kotler Ch.7] [Slide: Kotler Ch.8 — Product Mix dimensions]

Key trap to avoid in this question type: Don't confuse "concentrated" with "undifferentiated." Concentrated = ONE segment, TAILORED offer (LC Waikiki, Apple Pro Max). Undifferentiated = WHOLE market, ONE offer (LADA, Coca-Cola classic globally). Don't confuse "differentiated" the targeting strategy (multiple segments, multiple offers) with "differentiated" the positioning approach (distinct vs competitors) — Nido and Samsung are both differentiated in both senses, which is why they show up cleanly in column 3. [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — Targeting]

Question 3: (40 marks) — Two cases. (20 marks each)

For each case below: - (a) Define the main problem and its nature. (4 marks) - (b) Analyze the problem from a marketing point of view — which marketing concept(s) are at the root: STP failure? Brand erosion? Pricing mismatch? Channel conflict? Promotion misfire? Combination? Cite the framework. (4 marks) - (c) Suggest solutions across the marketing mix — Product / Price / Place / Promotion + STP adjustments where relevant. (12 marks)

Case A: Cilantro Egypt — Café Chain in Decline (20 marks)

Cilantro is an Egyptian premium-café chain founded in 2001, with ~50 branches across Cairo and Alexandria. Over the past three years, customer-satisfaction has dropped from a brand-perception index of 71 to 57 — a 20% drop. Three drivers are clear from the data: (1) wait times in flagship branches now average 20–30 minutes during peak hours due to mobile-order overflow; (2) prices for a medium latte rose from 38 EGP to 62 EGP over two years (+63%), now perceived as expensive against new third-wave specialty entrants (Brewing Lab, Carbon Coffee) and the much-cheaper Costa Egypt (45 EGP); (3) the menu has expanded to 87 items including breakfast, lunch, vegan, and a pastry counter — front-of-house staff are taking 30% longer per ticket. Quarterly same-store sales have dropped -7% in Q1 2026. The board has authorized a 40 million EGP turnaround budget.

(a) Define the main problem and its nature. (4 marks)

Model (~90 words): The main problem is brand-promise dilution combined with positioning drift, of a systemic, multi-cause nature (not a single broken lever). Cilantro originally positioned as a premium, relaxed "third place" for urban professionals, but three simultaneous operational + product + pricing decisions have eroded that promise. The drop in customer satisfaction is the symptom; the root cause is that the actual store experience no longer delivers what customers were promised. The nature is strategic, not tactical — a discount or a new TV ad will not fix it. [Slide: Kotler Ch.1 — Customer Satisfaction = Perceived Value vs Expectations] [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — Positioning]

(b) Analyze the problem from a marketing point of view. (4 marks)

Model (~100 words): Three marketing concepts are at the root simultaneously: (1) STP — positioning drift: Cilantro's actual experience no longer matches its stated positioning (Ch.7). (2) Product — line/mix over-extension: menu sprawl from 32 → 87 SKUs violates the product-line management principles in Ch.8; product-mix width grew without strategic justification. (3) Pricing — value-perception gap: price rose 63% but perceived value dropped — Ch.10 value-based pricing logic is violated (price ceiling = customer perception, which has fallen). Plus a brand-equity erosion layer underneath (Ch.8 branding). This is a 4Ps-wide problem that needs a 4Ps-wide solution. [Slide: Kotler Ch.1 + Ch.7 + Ch.8 + Ch.10]

(c) Suggest solutions across the marketing mix. (12 marks)

Model (~280 words):

Product (Ch.8) — 4 marks: Prune the menu from 87 to ~35 SKUs (line pruning). Eliminate sandwiches/lunch (PLC decline anyway). Keep specialty coffee (signature), Egyptian pastries (signature), seasonal breakfast lines. Restore the augmented product layer — barista craft, ambient experience, free Wi-Fi at premium tables. Redesign packaging in premium kraft with origin-story panel (packaging is one of Dr. Alaa's verbatim emphasis points). Introduce sub-brand "Cilantro Reserve" for hand-poured premium drinks. [Slide: Kotler Ch.8 — Product Line + Branding + Packaging] [Lec 4 @ 1:12:57]

Price (Ch.10) — 3 marks: Hold list prices at current levels — cutting signals brand demotion. Switch from cost-plus back to value-based pricing. Add price segmentation: morning loyalty card (15% off pre-pay), afternoon dine-in full price. Psychological pricing: end items at 9 EGP (59 instead of 62). Avoid penetration cuts — that destroys the premium. [Slide: Kotler Ch.10 — Value-Based + Segmented + Psychological Pricing]

Place (Ch.14) — 2 marks: Selective distribution (current 50 branches is right; resist franchise-style mass expansion). Add a direct-to-consumer subscription channel (delivery in target neighborhoods) to capture share without compromising store experience.

Promotion (Ch.11) — 2 marks: PR-led "Back to Cilantro" narrative (Egyptian heritage, slow growth). Direct & Digital Instagram-first storytelling. Limited Sales Promotion (loyalty cards only — no flash sales). No mass-TV ads (off-brand for premium).

STP adjustment (Ch.7) — 1 mark: Concentrated targeting on urban A/B-class professionals 25–45 seeking a "third place." Disengage from price-sensitive grab-and-go (let Costa Egypt have it). Re-write positioning statement: "To urban Egyptian professionals who want a calm premium café, Cilantro is the Egyptian-rooted third place that combines world-class coffee craft with the warmth of home."

Case B: Vodafone Egypt — Prepaid-Youth Churn Crisis (20 marks)

Vodafone Egypt has been the market leader in mobile telecoms since the 2000s. For three consecutive quarters in 2025–2026, however, net prepaid additions have turned negative — Vodafone is losing more prepaid subscribers per quarter than it gains. The bleed is concentrated in the youth segment (16–25), where churn is running at 28% annual (vs 12% for the adult segment). Customers are switching to Orange Egypt and Etisalat Misr. Internal data shows: (1) the youth segment perceives Vodafone as "the parents' network" — premium but boring; (2) discount campaigns (50% off recharges, "freebie weekends") have not stopped churn and are eating into margins; (3) Orange has launched a youth-only "Yalla Shabab" bundle (TikTok data + social + free WhatsApp) at lower headline price than Vodafone's equivalent; (4) Vodafone's flagship retail stores look the same as in 2015 and target everyone equally — no youth-specific positioning. CFO has cut the marketing budget by 15% for the next year, demanding ROI on every spend.

(a) Define the main problem and its nature. (4 marks)

Model (~90 words): The main problem is segment-level positioning failure, of a structural, segment-specific nature (not a pricing problem despite how it presents). Vodafone is losing the youth segment not because price is too high but because the value proposition for that segment is wrong: Vodafone's brand is positioned for the whole market, perceived as "the parents' network" by youth, and the product bundle does not match youth-specific needs (TikTok data, social usage, group plans). The discount-spiral response is making it worse — discounts commoditize without repositioning. STP failure, dressed up as a churn problem. [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — Segment-level Positioning] [Slide: Kotler Ch.1 — Customer Satisfaction]

(b) Analyze the problem from a marketing point of view. (4 marks)

Model (~100 words): Four concepts converge: (1) STP failure at the segment level: Vodafone is using undifferentiated targeting when it needs differentiated targeting with a distinct sub-brand for youth (Ch.7). (2) Brand-equity drift within the youth segment (Ch.8 branding): Vodafone is now associated with parents — a positioning erosion identical to Cilantro's pattern. (3) Pricing trap: discount-led response without repositioning is the classic Ch.10 mistake of using price to fix a non-price problem (Kotler explicitly warns against this). (4) Promotion mismatch (Ch.11): mass-media IMC does not reach TikTok-native youth. The Orange "Yalla Shabab" sub-brand demonstrates the right answer. [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 + Ch.8 + Ch.10 + Ch.11]

(c) Suggest solutions across the marketing mix. (12 marks)

Model (~290 words):

STP first (Ch.7) — 3 marks: Switch from undifferentiated to differentiated targeting. Segment the prepaid base by age + usage pattern + lifestyle. Create a distinct youth sub-segment (16–25, social-heavy, multi-app users). Reposition with a youth-only sub-brand — call it "Vodafone Generation" or "Vodafone Shabab" — that is visibly different from the mainstream Vodafone red. New positioning statement: "To Egyptian young people who live on TikTok, Instagram, and WhatsApp, Vodafone Generation is the only network that bundles social + entertainment + groups at a price that fits a student's budget." [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — STP + Positioning Statement]

Product (Ch.8) — 3 marks: Build a sub-brand product line specifically for youth — bundles of unlimited social + TikTok data + WhatsApp + Discord + low-price voice. Apply product-line filling to the prepaid range, not stretching (don't dilute the mainstream Vodafone tier). Brand-name selection matters here — Dr. Alaa's verbatim emphasis: a distinct sub-brand name signals to youth that "this is for you." [Slide: Kotler Ch.8 — Branding + Line Decisions] [Lec 4 @ 1:11:06]

Price (Ch.10) — 2 marks: Stop the discount spiral on the mainstream brand. Use value-based + segmented pricing for the sub-brand: 15–20% lower than mainstream prepaid, but justified by a distinct bundle (so the comparison is to Orange's "Yalla Shabab," not to Vodafone's mainstream).

Place (Ch.14) — 2 marks: Selective distribution in youth-facing locations — co-brand corners in malls near schools/universities, on-campus kiosks, partner retailers (e.g., Battery + Tea coffeeshops). Reduce reliance on mainstream Vodafone stores that signal "parents' network." Maintain administered VMS with retail partners.

Promotion (Ch.11) — 2 marks: TikTok-first IMC, micro-influencer led. PR: launch event with youth artists. Sales Promotion: free-trial week, refer-a-friend bonus. Direct & Digital: WhatsApp Business loyalty channel. Personal Selling: campus brand ambassadors. AVOID mass-TV (off-brand for sub-target). [Slide: Kotler Ch.11 — 5 Tools + IMC]

END OF EXAM — BEST WISHES

Self-grading rubric: For each question, award full marks if (a) framework is correctly named, (b) it's applied to the case specifics (not generic), (c) at least one slide/lecture citation is given, and (d) Dr. Alaa's wording style ("analyze / segment / position / adapt / advise") shapes your answer. Target ≥60/80 to be confident.

📁 Adapted Cases

Each case includes a full ~200-word scenario, three sub-questions in Dr. Alaa's template format (define-problem / analyze / solve-across-mix), and complete model answers with citations.

F. Adapted Cases — Full Versions (10 cases for deep practice)

Each case uses one of two template structures:

  • Cases 1–8 — Dr. Alaa Strategic-Marketing template: scenario → (a) define problem + nature → (b) analyze from marketing point of view → (c) solutions across marketing mix. This matches Dr. Alaa's mock-paper Q3 format.
  • Cases 9–10 — Wide-rubric 25-mark template: scenario → Q1 Cultural / Consumer-Environment → Q2 Segmentation & Targeting → Q3 Positioning Strategy → Q4 Marketing Mix Adaptation → Q5 Communication Strategy (5 marks each, mirrors the structural depth of Prof Alaa's International Marketing July 2025 final Q1 — but is anchored in Egyptian-only scenarios and uses the Strategic Marketing chapters only (Ch.6/7/8/10/11/13/14), NOT international-marketing material).

The mock-paper Q3 used 2 of cases 1–4; the other 6 are extra study cases. Cases 5–8 were chosen to fill chapter-coverage gaps: Case 5 (Juhayna) drills Ch.9 New-Product-Development; Case 6 (Pharaoh Cement) drills Ch.10 pricing in a B2B/Ch.6 buyer context; Case 7 (Talabat) drills Ch.13 services marketing & the 7Ps; Case 8 (Danone Egypt) drills brand-portfolio strategy in an inflation/currency-shock environment using the team's own Danone research base. Cases 9–10 add wide-rubric drill: Case 9 (Spinneys Bio) = FMCG retail private-label launch (Ch.6/7/8/10/11/14); Case 10 (Onebank by Banque Misr) = services 7Ps for a digital-bank launch targeting Egyptian youth (Ch.7/8/10/11/13).

Case 1 — Cilantro Egypt (extended scenario; adapted from Starbucks Case.docx)

Full scenario (~210 words):

Cilantro is an Egyptian premium-café chain founded in 2001, headquartered in Cairo, with 50 branches in Cairo, Alexandria, and Giza. Cilantro's original positioning was "the Egyptian premium third place" — a relaxed coffeehouse for urban professionals to work, meet, and linger.

Over 2023–2026, three trends have eroded customer satisfaction. First, mobile-order volume now exceeds in-store volume by 60%, causing 20–30 minute waits during peak hours; baristas can't keep up. Second, prices for a medium latte rose from 38 EGP (2023) to 62 EGP (2026), a 63% increase. Customers compare unfavorably to Costa Egypt (45 EGP) and even more so to third-wave entrants Brewing Lab and Carbon Coffee (55 EGP for hand-poured premium). Third, the menu expanded from 32 items in 2023 to 87 items in 2026 — full breakfast, lunch sandwiches, vegan range, pastries — and front-of-house complexity has grown linearly.

The brand-perception index has dropped from 71 to 57 (-20%). Same-store sales fell -7% in Q1 2026. The board has approved a 40 million EGP turnaround budget. Cilantro's CMO has 6 months to deliver a "Back to Cilantro" plan.

(a) Define the main problem and its nature. (~150 words)

Model: Cilantro's main problem is strategic positioning drift driven by simultaneous operational, pricing, and product-mix decisions — a systemic, 4Ps-wide problem, not a single broken lever. The nature of the problem is slow-burn brand erosion: every individual decision (more menu items, higher prices, mobile-order rollout) was a defensible tactical move in isolation, but collectively they pulled the actual customer experience away from the original positioning ("relaxed premium third place"). What customers perceive today (slow queue, expensive coffee, fragmented menu, transactional experience) no longer matches what the brand promised (calm, premium, work-friendly). The result is customer-satisfaction collapse (-20% in three years) because perceived value has dropped below customer expectations — the textbook Ch.1 satisfaction equation. The nature is strategic, not tactical: a flash sale or a single new ad will not reverse this. [Slide: Kotler Ch.1 — Customer Satisfaction] [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — Positioning]

(b) Analyze from a marketing point of view. (~160 words)

Model: Three marketing concepts are at the root simultaneously: (1) Ch.7 STP — positioning drift: actual experience no longer matches stated positioning. The brand-promise dilution is textbook positioning failure. (2) Ch.8 Product — line/mix over-extension: menu sprawl from 32→87 violates product-line management principles. Product-mix width grew without strategic justification; this is the Kotler-warned trap of confusing breadth growth with relevance growth. (3) Ch.10 Pricing — value-perception gap: price rose 63% but perceived value dropped. This violates value-based pricing's core rule (price ceiling = customer perception of value, which has fallen). Plus a brand-equity erosion layer underneath (Ch.8 branding) — the augmented-product layer (ambient experience, barista craft) has weakened, and the brand's competitive moat with it. The interaction effect is multiplicative: a 4Ps-wide problem requires a 4Ps-wide solution, not single-lever fixes. [Slide: Kotler Ch.1 + Ch.7 + Ch.8 + Ch.10]

(c) Solutions across the marketing mix. (~310 words)

Model:

Product (Ch.8): Prune the menu from 87 to ~35 SKUs — line pruning, not line filling. Cut sandwiches/lunch (PLC decline anyway). Keep specialty coffee, Egyptian pastries, seasonal breakfast. Restore the augmented product layer: barista training program, certified-origin sourcing, in-store ambient experience improvements (better Wi-Fi, premium-tier seating, named drink personalization). Redesign packaging in premium kraft with origin-story panel — packaging is one of Dr. Alaa's verbatim emphasis points ("باكيجينج مهم جدا"). Introduce sub-brand "Cilantro Reserve" for hand-poured premium drinks — reinforce brand-name selection emphasis from Lec 4. [Slide: Kotler Ch.8 — Product Line + Branding + Packaging] [Lec 4 @ 1:11:06 + 1:12:57]

Price (Ch.10): Hold list prices — cutting signals brand demotion. Switch from cost-plus back to value-based pricing. Add price segmentation: morning loyalty card (15% off pre-pay), afternoon dine-in full price. Psychological pricing: end items at 9 EGP. Bundle pricing: weekend "brunch bundle" priced 15% below sum-of-parts. AVOID penetration cuts — that destroys premium positioning. [Slide: Kotler Ch.10 — Value-Based + Segmented + Bundle Pricing]

Place (Ch.14): Maintain selective distribution (50 branches is right; resist franchise expansion). Add direct-to-consumer subscription channel (delivery in target neighborhoods) to capture share without compromising in-store experience. VMS: Corporate (Cilantro owns all branches) — keep it; do not move to franchise.

Promotion (Ch.11): PR-led "Back to Cilantro" narrative (Egyptian heritage, coffee craft, slow growth philosophy). Direct & Digital Instagram-first storytelling (baristas, sourcing trips). Limited Sales Promotion (loyalty cards only — no flash sales). Personal Selling through trained barista ambassadors. No mass-TV — off-brand for premium.

STP adjustment (Ch.7): Concentrated targeting on urban A/B-class professionals 25–45. Disengage from grab-and-go (let Costa Egypt have it). Rewrite positioning statement: "To urban Egyptian professionals who want a calm, premium café to work and meet, Cilantro is the Egyptian-rooted third place that combines world-class coffee craft with the warmth of home." Value proposition: more benefit at the same price. [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — STP + 5 Targeting Factors]

Case 2 — Carrefour vs Almarai (المراعي) (Dr. Alaa's verbatim in-class case)

Why this case matters most: Dr. Alaa spent ~3 minutes in class detailing this exact conflict. EXAM_SIGNAL.md flags it as a top-5 mini-case candidate.

Full scenario (~200 words):

Almarai (المراعي) is a Saudi-headquartered dairy giant with annual revenue ~$3.8B, the largest dairy brand in the GCC. Carrefour Egypt is a French-owned hypermarket retailer with 60+ stores across Egypt and a private-label dairy line ("Carrefour Frais") that competes directly with Almarai's premium-fresh milk SKUs.

The relationship has become a textbook vertical-channel-conflict case. Carrefour Egypt's category manager has been quietly demoting Almarai shelf presence — fewer facings, lower-eye-level positioning, no in-store promotional support — while expanding Carrefour Frais facings. Carrefour also requires Almarai to pay slot fees of 80,000 EGP per SKU per year + an additional 5% trade-margin contribution for "preferred shelf positioning."

Almarai's regional VP has three options: (1) absorb the slot fees + reduced margins (preserve volume), (2) pull Almarai products from Carrefour entirely and shift to selective distribution via independent supermarkets + dairy stores + direct-to-consumer, or (3) build a counter-promotion campaign emphasizing Almarai's brand equity (advertising, IMC) to force Carrefour to renegotiate.

Dr. Alaa's class observation: "the retail chains are happy when Almarai isn't present" — implying Almarai has been weakened in this channel relative to Carrefour's house brand.

(a) Define the main problem and its nature. (~150 words)

Model: Almarai's main problem is vertical channel conflict driven by retailer power asymmetry — a structural, channel-level problem (not a brand or pricing problem). The nature is that the dominant downstream channel partner (Carrefour) is exploiting its Administered-VMS power to extract economic rent from the upstream manufacturer (Almarai) and to favor its own private label. The visible symptoms — slot fees, shelf demotion, margin pressure — are downstream of one root cause: Almarai depends more on Carrefour than Carrefour depends on Almarai, so Carrefour can rewrite the terms unilaterally. The conflict is vertical (different channel levels: manufacturer vs retailer), NOT horizontal. The problem is also escalating: as Carrefour Frais grows, Carrefour's incentive to retain Almarai shrinks. Without intervention, Almarai will face progressively worse terms or eventual delisting. [Slide: Kotler Ch.14 — Vertical Channel Conflict + VMS] [Lec 6 @ 1:05:18 + 1:00:48]

(b) Analyze from a marketing point of view. (~160 words)

Model: Three Ch.14 concepts converge: (1) VMS power asymmetry — Administered VMS in action: Carrefour does not own Almarai (would be Corporate VMS) and has no franchise contract (would be Contractual) but exerts dominance via size + scale + brand + sales-data ownership. This is the textbook Administered VMS pattern Dr. Alaa enumerates verbatim. (2) Channel power sources: Carrefour wields all five — Reward (shelf positioning), Coercive (delisting threat), Legitimate (60+ stores), Expert (sales data), Referent (brand attracts shoppers) — and Almarai counters with only one (Brand referent / consumer loyalty). The imbalance is the analytical core. (3) Push vs Pull strategy (Ch.11) interaction: Almarai's current strategy is push-heavy (selling through Carrefour) which leaves it vulnerable. A pull strategy — building consumer demand directly — would weaken Carrefour's leverage because Carrefour cannot risk frustrating its own customers who walk in demanding Almarai by name. [Slide: Kotler Ch.14 — VMS + Channel Power] [Slide: Kotler Ch.11 — Push vs Pull]

(c) Solutions across the marketing mix. (~290 words)

Model: Hybrid strategy — option (3) primary + selective elements of (2). Do NOT accept option (1) (capitulating is brand-damaging long-term).

Promotion (Ch.11) — primary lever: Pivot to Pull strategy. Invest heavily in mass-IMC (TV, digital, point-of-sale) so consumers walk into Carrefour demanding Almarai by name. Pull strategy neutralizes Administered-VMS power because Carrefour cannot risk demanding-consumer disappointment. Use the 5 promotion-mix tools from the Slide-12 IMC diagram: heavy Advertising (TV + digital), strong PR ("the Almarai farm-to-table story"), targeted Sales Promotion (in-store sampling), Personal Selling for retail partners, Direct Marketing (lecture-color: WhatsApp loyalty + digital coupons). [Slide 12 IMC diagram + Slide 28 Push vs Pull]

Place (Ch.14) — strategic restructure: Diversify the channel mix. Open direct-to-consumer subscription (home delivery in major Egyptian cities). Grow independent supermarket + traditional grocery + dairy-store presence — these are smaller channels but they reduce Carrefour's leverage to ~40% of category volume instead of ~70%. Maintain Carrefour presence but renegotiate from a position of strength once alternatives are running. VMS evolution: shift from pure Administered (Carrefour-dominated) to a Hybrid Multichannel system (direct + administered + selective independents). [Slide: Kotler Ch.14 — Multichannel + Selective Distribution]

Product (Ch.8): Strengthen brand equity to support the pull strategy. Sub-brand premium-fresh line ("Almarai Pure" or similar) to differentiate from Carrefour Frais. Premium packaging (Dr. Alaa emphasis: packaging is critical for the Ch.8 axis).

Price (Ch.10): Hold premium price in mainstream channel. Add trade promotion realignment with Carrefour (renegotiated slot fees) only AFTER pull and alternative channels are working.

STP (Ch.7): Reaffirm differentiated targeting with a distinct premium-fresh tier vs Carrefour Frais's commodity tier — defend the premium customer segment, do not chase Carrefour Frais down-market.

Case 3 — Vodafone Egypt Prepaid-Youth Churn (NEW problem case)

Full scenario (~210 words):

Vodafone Egypt has been the market leader in mobile telecoms since the 2000s — ~37% subscriber share, premium brand reputation, the largest retail footprint. For three consecutive quarters in 2025–2026, however, net prepaid additions have turned negative — Vodafone is losing more prepaid subscribers per quarter than it gains. The bleed is concentrated in the youth segment (ages 16–25), where churn is running at 28% annually (vs 12% for the adult segment).

Customers are switching primarily to Orange Egypt and to a lesser extent Etisalat Misr. Internal customer-research data shows: (1) the youth segment perceives Vodafone as "the parents' network" — premium but boring; (2) discount campaigns (50% off recharges, "freebie weekends") have not stopped the bleed and are eating into margins; (3) Orange has launched a youth-only sub-brand "Yalla Shabab" (TikTok unlimited data + social bundle + free WhatsApp) at a lower headline price than Vodafone's equivalent prepaid bundle; (4) Vodafone's flagship retail stores look the same as in 2015 and target everyone equally — no youth-specific positioning, no youth-targeted communication.

The CFO has just cut the marketing budget by 15% for the next year, demanding ROI on every spend. The CMO has 9 months to stop the youth churn and produce one quarter of positive net adds.

(a) Define the main problem and its nature. (~140 words)

Model: Vodafone's main problem is segment-level positioning failure, of a structural, segment-specific nature — NOT a pricing problem despite how it presents itself in the data. Vodafone is losing the youth segment not because price is too high but because the value proposition for that segment is wrong: the brand is positioned for the whole market (undifferentiated targeting), perceived as "the parents' network" by youth, and the product bundle doesn't match youth-specific needs (TikTok data, social, group plans). The discount-spiral response is making it worse — discounts commoditize without repositioning. The problem is STP-rooted, dressed up as a churn problem. Worse, every quarter without action allows Orange Yalla Shabab to deepen its lead and make recovery harder (segment lock-in effect). [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — Segment-level Positioning]

(b) Analyze from a marketing point of view. (~160 words)

Model: Four concepts converge to explain the bleed: (1) Ch.7 STP failure at the segment level — Vodafone uses undifferentiated targeting when it needs differentiated with a distinct sub-brand for youth. The Nido Nestle model is exactly the right template: different products for different demographic segments. (2) Ch.8 Brand-equity drift within the youth segment — Vodafone is now associated with parents, a positioning erosion identical to Cilantro's pattern but on a segment-specific axis. (3) Ch.10 Pricing trap — Kotler explicitly warns against using price as the lever to fix a non-price problem; this is what discount campaigns are doing here. (4) Ch.11 Promotion mismatch — mass-media IMC channels (TV, FM radio) do not reach TikTok-native youth; the medium itself signals "this isn't for you." Orange's "Yalla Shabab" sub-brand demonstrates the correct competitive answer. [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 + Ch.8 + Ch.10 + Ch.11]

(c) Solutions across the marketing mix. (~310 words)

Model:

STP first (Ch.7): Switch from undifferentiated to differentiated targeting. Re-segment the prepaid base by demographic (age) + psychographic (lifestyle) + behavioral (app usage). Define a distinct youth segment (16–25, social-heavy, multi-app, students or early-career, group-plan-likely). Create a youth-only sub-brand — call it "Vodafone Generation" or "Vodafone Shabab" — visibly different from the mainstream Vodafone red. New positioning statement: "To Egyptian young people who live on TikTok, Instagram, and WhatsApp, Vodafone Generation is the only network that bundles social + entertainment + groups at a price that fits a student's budget." [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — STP + Differentiated Targeting + Positioning Statement]

Product (Ch.8): Build a sub-brand product line specifically for youth — bundles of unlimited TikTok/Instagram/Snapchat data + WhatsApp + free group voice + low-price voice/SMS. Use product-line filling within the prepaid range (do not stretch into postpaid). Brand-name selection is critical here — Dr. Alaa's verbatim emphasis: distinct sub-brand name signals to youth that "this is for you." Don't just rename existing bundles; build new ones from the segment's needs. [Slide: Kotler Ch.8 — Branding + Line Decisions] [Lec 4 @ 1:11:06]

Price (Ch.10): Stop the discount spiral on the mainstream brand. Use value-based + segmented pricing for the sub-brand: 15–20% lower than mainstream prepaid, but justified by a distinct bundle. The comparison point becomes Orange's "Yalla Shabab," not Vodafone's mainstream — neutralizing the cannibalization risk.

Place (Ch.14): Selective distribution in youth-facing locations — co-brand corners in malls near schools/universities, on-campus kiosks, partner retailers (e.g., Battery + Tea coffeeshops). Reduce reliance on mainstream Vodafone stores that signal "parents' network." Maintain administered VMS with retail partners.

Promotion (Ch.11): TikTok-first IMC, micro-influencer led. PR: launch event with youth artists. Sales Promotion: free-trial week, refer-a-friend bonus, gamified loyalty. Direct & Digital: WhatsApp Business loyalty channel. Personal Selling: campus brand ambassadors. AVOID mass-TV (off-brand for sub-target). Apply the 6-step IMC process rigorously: identify (youth), objectives (move from awareness → preference → switch from Orange), message (rational + emotional), media (TikTok primary), source (influencers > corporate), feedback (NPS + switch rate). [Slide: Kotler Ch.11 — 5 Tools + 6-Step Process]

Case 4 — El-Cairo Footwear — Distribution Mismatch (NEW problem case)

Full scenario (~210 words):

El-Cairo Footwear is a family-owned Egyptian shoe manufacturer founded in Shoubra in 1985. The company makes ~300,000 pairs/year of leather shoes at a modern factory using imported Italian-style designs. Product quality is genuinely good — independent industry reviewers rate the leather and construction comparable to mid-tier Spanish brands (e.g., Pikolinos) and far above the typical low-tier Egyptian shoe shops.

The owner wants to position El-Cairo Footwear as a premium Egyptian-made brand at 800–1,200 EGP per pair (premium for Egyptian context, mass-affordable globally). However, after 5 years of trying: - Carrefour Egypt, Spinneys, and Seoudi all refused to stock the brand — too expensive for their mass-market positioning. - The brand ends up sold mostly in low-tier traditional shops in Shoubra, Embaba, and Old Cairo at 600–900 EGP — customers in these channels balk at "Egyptian shoe for 900 EGP," so retailers discount further. - A few pairs have made it into upscale boutiques in Zamalek and Maadi, but volume is tiny (~2% of total). - Marketing budget has been ~5M EGP/year, used mostly on Facebook ads — almost no premium-channel work. - The owner has 30M EGP earmarked for a turnaround.

(a) Define the main problem and its nature. (~140 words)

Model: El-Cairo Footwear's main problem is a channel-positioning mismatch of a strategic, multi-lever nature: a genuinely premium product is being sold through mass / low-tier channels, which simultaneously (i) caps perceived value at the lowest channel's tier ("a Shoubra shoe brand can't be premium"), (ii) traps the brand in a price-discount spiral that destroys margin, and (iii) prevents premium customers from ever discovering the brand because they don't shop in those channels. The nature is distribution-intensity mismatch — the company is using intensive distribution (whatever shop will stock it) when it should be using selective distribution (curated upscale boutiques + own brand stores + selective premium department stores). Every other marketing decision is compromised by this mistake. [Slide: Kotler Ch.14 — Distribution Intensity]

(b) Analyze from a marketing point of view. (~160 words)

Model: Three Ch.14 + Ch.7 concepts converge: (1) Distribution-intensity mismatch (Ch.14): the textbook rule is premium goods → selective or exclusive distribution; mass goods → intensive. El-Cairo Footwear violates this by using intensive distribution for a premium product. (2) STP positioning failure (Ch.7): positioning is set in the customer's mind by the channels where they encounter the product. A "premium Egyptian-made shoe" sold next to 200-EGP shoes in Shoubra cannot be perceived as premium, no matter what the box says. (3) Brand-equity erosion (Ch.8): every Shoubra shop sale at a discounted price further commoditizes the brand. The chain is causal: wrong channel → wrong perceived positioning → wrong perceived value → forced discounts → brand commoditization → margin loss. Push vs pull (Ch.11) also contributes: there's no pull strategy to create premium customer demand. [Slide: Kotler Ch.14 + Ch.7 + Ch.8 + Ch.11]

(c) Solutions across the marketing mix. (~310 words)

Model:

Place (Ch.14) — primary lever: Restructure to selective distribution. Specific actions: - Pull out of low-tier shops over 12 months (manage the inventory transition). - Open 6–8 own-brand premium stores in Maadi, Zamalek, City Stars, Mall of Egypt, Mall of Arabia — the customer must encounter El-Cairo in a premium environment. - Negotiate with upscale boutiques (e.g., Diwan-adjacent retail), with selective placement in Spinneys Madinaty / Spinneys Zamalek (premium-tier branches, not mass branches). - Build a direct-to-consumer e-commerce channel with premium delivery experience. - VMS form: Hybrid — Corporate VMS for own stores, Administered VMS for selective premium partners. NOT Contractual (franchise model is wrong for early premium-brand stage). [Slide: Kotler Ch.14 — VMS + Distribution Intensity + Multichannel]

STP (Ch.7) — second lever: Concentrated targeting on urban A/B-class professionals 25–50 who want Egyptian-made premium goods (national-pride segment + premium-quality segment). NOT differentiated (too small to span tiers). New positioning statement: "To Egyptian professionals who want premium handcrafted shoes that rival European brands at half the price, El-Cairo Footwear is the only premium Egyptian-made leather brand."

Product (Ch.8): Strengthen the augmented product — premium packaging (Dr. Alaa's verbatim emphasis), shoe-tree boxes, free annual leather-care service, lifetime resoling. Brand-name redesign — "El-Cairo" works as Egyptian-pride name; reinforce with sub-brands for occasion ("El-Cairo Office," "El-Cairo Weekend"). [Lec 4 @ 1:12:57]

Price (Ch.10): Hold list price at 800–1,200 EGP — do NOT cut. In the new channels, customer-perceived value will rise to meet the price (because the channel anchors the perception). Value-based pricing. Eliminate discount-spiral by enforcing minimum-retail-price agreements with all selective partners.

Promotion (Ch.11): Pull strategy to create demand. Egyptian-heritage IMC narrative (PR + Direct & Digital), premium-magazine advertising (e.g., Business Today Egypt), influencer partnerships with Egyptian professionals/style-leaders. Avoid Facebook mass-market ads — they signal the wrong segment.

Case 5 — Juhayna High-Protein Yogurt — New-Product Development (NEW problem case, fills Ch.9 gap)

Why this case matters: None of cases 1–4 stress the Ch.9 New-Product-Development 8-step process or PLC introduction-stage tactics. Dr. Alaa is likely to test NPD as a process question; this case forces the full pipeline.

Full scenario (~215 words):

Juhayna (EGX:JUFO) is the Egyptian dairy market leader — H1 2025 revenue +23% YoY to ~USD 293.5mn, dairy = 76% of mix, ~400k baqala outlet reach, and aggressive export growth (+205% in 2024). The R&D director has identified the global functional / high-protein / microbiome growth vector (the three vectors driving the world's USD 144bn yogurt category to ~USD 245bn by 2035) as Juhayna's next bet.

The proposal: launch "Juhayna Pro", a 20-gram-protein single-serve yogurt cup aimed at gym-going Egyptian young professionals 25–40, priced at a 60% premium to mainstream Juhayna yogurt. Internally, however, the team is moving too fast: marketing has already designed the cup, signed an Egyptian football celebrity, and booked 30s TV slots for Ramadan — before any concept test, no test market, no defined target persona, and no PLC plan for what happens after the launch buzz fades. A junior brand manager privately objects that "we're doing step 7 before step 2." The CEO has earmarked 80M EGP and given a 12-month launch deadline.

The HiPro brand from Danone Egypt already sits in this functional-protein niche, and Almarai's Lamar is rumored to be preparing a competing SKU.

(a) Define the main problem and its nature. (~140 words)

Model: The main problem is process discipline failure in New-Product Development — a procedural, sequence-of-steps problem, not a creative or budget problem. Juhayna's launch plan skips the front-end of the Kotler Ch.9 8-step NPD process (idea generation → idea screening → concept development & testing → marketing strategy → business analysis → product development → test marketing → commercialization) and jumps from rough idea (step 1) straight to commercialization-style tactics (step 8: celebrity, TV booking, packaging finalisation). The nature is front-end vs back-end imbalance: the back-end activities (advertising, distribution) are sprinting while the front-end activities (concept testing, target definition, business analysis, test market) have been silently skipped. This is the textbook NPD-failure pattern Kotler warns about; without intervention, the launch becomes a high-cost, high-risk gamble against entrenched competitors (Danone HiPro). [Slide: Kotler Ch.9 — NPD 8-step Process]

(b) Analyze from a marketing point of view. (~165 words)

Model: Four chapters converge: (1) Ch.9 NPD process — the 8 steps exist precisely to de-risk launches; skipping steps 2–7 raises product-failure probability dramatically. The concept test (step 3) would have revealed whether Egyptian consumers see 20g protein as a benefit; the business analysis (step 5) would have stress-tested the 60%-premium price point against Egypt's 47% food inflation; the test market (step 7) would have caught distribution and consumption-frequency issues. (2) Ch.9 PLC — Introduction stage: this is a textbook introduction-stage product (low awareness, no repeat purchase, high promotional cost per unit). Treating it like a maturity-stage product (big celebrity, broad TV) wastes spend. (3) Ch.7 STP — the target persona is undefined, so the IMC message cannot be sharp. (4) Ch.10 Pricing — the 60%-premium price was set without value-based testing in an inflationary environment, risking immediate sticker-shock rejection. [Slide: Kotler Ch.9 + Ch.7 + Ch.10]

(c) Solutions across the marketing mix. (~305 words)

Model:

Process first (Ch.9 NPD): Pause the launch by 4 months, run the missing steps in compressed parallel form. Step 3 concept test: 200-respondent online + in-mall test in Cairo/Alex with three concept boards (pure-protein / probiotic-protein / pre-workout-protein) — let the consumer pick. Step 5 business analysis: build the 3-year sales/profit forecast at the tested price points, sensitivity-test against +30% input-cost shock. Step 7 test market: launch in 1 city (Alexandria) for 8 weeks BEFORE national rollout — measure trial, repeat, channel velocity. The 80M EGP budget is unchanged but is re-sequenced: front-end research first, then full national TV. [Slide: Kotler Ch.9 — 8-Step NPD]

Product (Ch.8 + Ch.9 PLC): After concept test, lock the winning variant; build the augmented product (QR-code → personalised nutrition tracker, free first-month gym partner offer). Packaging with explicit protein-callout (Dr. Alaa verbatim emphasis — packaging is critical). Plan the full PLC roadmap upfront: introduction (single SKU, premium), growth (line-fill: chocolate, mango), maturity (price tiers + channel expansion to baqala), decline (reformulate or harvest). [Slide: Kotler Ch.8 + Ch.9 — PLC]

Price (Ch.10): Value-based pricing calibrated to test-market data, NOT cost-plus + 60%. Probable landing zone: 30–45% premium, not 60%. Skimming vs penetration choice: market-skimming is justified (premium positioning, low elasticity for fitness segment), but only if business-analysis numbers support it. [Slide: Kotler Ch.10 — New-product pricing strategies]

Place (Ch.14): Selective distribution in introduction stage (Carrefour, Spinneys, Seoudi, gym chains, Talabat/Mrsool grocery) — NOT baqala until growth stage. Avoids the wrong-channel-anchoring trap from Case 4 (El-Cairo Footwear).

Promotion (Ch.11): Reallocate from broad TV → differentiated IMC for fitness segment: Instagram/TikTok fitness creators, gym point-of-sale sampling, PR via nutritionist endorsements, micro-personal-selling at gyms. Use the celebrity sparingly (PR launch event only), not as the backbone.

STP (Ch.7): Concentrated targeting on fitness-active urban 25–40 in introduction stage; widen to differentiated in growth stage.

Case 6 — Pharaoh Cement — B2B Pricing War (NEW problem case, fills Ch.6 B2B + Ch.10 cost-plus gap)

Why this case matters: All 4 original cases are B2C. The exam template can swap in a B2B scenario; this case drills Ch.6 business-buyer behavior + Ch.10 pricing strategies in commodity competition.

Full scenario (~210 words):

Pharaoh Cement is a mid-tier Egyptian cement producer (Suez plant, 2.8M tons/year capacity), founded 1998. The Egyptian cement market is structurally over-supplied (national capacity ≈ 84M tons against demand ≈ 50M tons after the 2024–2026 construction slowdown), and the top 3 producers (Lafarge, Suez Cement, Arabian Cement) routinely undercut. Pharaoh's average plant-gate price has dropped from 2,100 EGP/ton in 2023 to 1,450 EGP/ton in Q1 2026, while the energy / clinker / packaging input cost rose 38% over the same period due to currency devaluation. Margin has collapsed from 19% to 2%.

Pharaoh's pricing approach is purely cost-plus: total cost per ton + a fixed 8% margin — and the sales team negotiates downward from there with every contractor and ready-mix concrete (RMC) plant. The B2B customers (large building contractors, RMC operators, government-project buyers) routinely run formal RFQ tenders comparing 4–5 cement suppliers on price only.

The owner is considering: (1) shutting one kiln to cut capacity, (2) following Lafarge's price down (race to the bottom), or (3) repositioning Pharaoh as a differentiated specialty-cement supplier (sulfate-resistant, low-heat, white cement) for premium B2B segments at a higher margin. The CFO has 6 months before covenants are breached.

(a) Define the main problem and its nature. (~145 words)

Model: The main problem is strategic mis-pricing in a commodity-trap B2B market — a pricing-strategy + segmentation problem, NOT a cost problem. Pharaoh uses cost-plus pricing in a market where (a) supply over capacity demand (so price is set by the marginal seller, not by cost), and (b) every customer is a professional B2B buyer running competitive RFQ tenders (Ch.6 business-buyer behavior — formal multi-vendor evaluation). Cost-plus in this context guarantees margin erosion: when input costs rise, Pharaoh raises price → loses the tender; when competitors cut, Pharaoh follows → margin collapses. The deeper nature is undifferentiated competing in a market where the only escape is differentiation or consolidation. Without a strategic pricing/positioning reset, the cost-plus + commodity-position combo will continue to compress margin every quarter until covenants break. [Slide: Kotler Ch.10 — Cost-based vs Value-based Pricing] [Slide: Kotler Ch.6 — Business-Buyer Behavior]

(b) Analyze from a marketing point of view. (~160 words)

Model: Four concepts converge: (1) Ch.10 Three pricing approaches — cost-based vs value-based vs competition-based. Pharaoh is using the worst combination for its market (cost-based in a competition-based market). The correct move is value-based pricing for differentiated SKUs and competition-based pricing for commodity SKUs, on a segmented basis. (2) Ch.6 Business-Buyer Behavior — B2B buyers (contractors, RMC, government) use formal buying-center processes; in commodity tenders the buying center reduces decision criteria to "price + spec compliance," eliminating any room for differentiation unless the seller actively creates new value dimensions (technical service, JIT delivery, specialty grades, financing terms). (3) Ch.7 STP — Pharaoh treats all B2B buyers identically (undifferentiated), missing that government infrastructure projects and specialty-construction buyers value sulfate-resistance and low-heat properties, NOT lowest price. (4) Ch.8 Product — augmented product (technical advisory, on-site testing, just-in-time delivery) is absent, leaving only the core commodity. [Slide: Kotler Ch.6 + Ch.7 + Ch.10]

(c) Solutions across the marketing mix. (~310 words)

Model: Choose option (3) — strategic repositioning — primarily; selectively use option (1) by mothballing one of two kilns to right-size capacity to the differentiated portfolio. Reject option (2) (price-following = continued margin death).

STP first (Ch.7): Switch from undifferentiated to differentiated targeting. Segment B2B buyers into: (i) commodity RMC + small contractors (price-only) — keep but cap exposure to 40% of volume; (ii) infrastructure / government / Suez Canal expansion projects (sulfate-resistant grade); (iii) specialty civil/industrial projects (low-heat, white cement). Position Pharaoh as "the Egyptian specialty-cement partner" for segments (ii) + (iii), defending margin via differentiation. [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — STP + Differentiated Targeting]

Product (Ch.8): Build a 3-grade portfolio — Pharaoh Standard (OPC), Pharaoh SR (sulfate-resistant), Pharaoh LH (low-heat). Augmented product: on-site technical advisor program, free initial mix-design service, JIT delivery commitment, certificate of conformity with EU EN-197 for export-grade. Brand the specialty range distinctly. [Slide: Kotler Ch.8 — Product Levels + Branding]

Price (Ch.10): Tiered pricing across grades. Standard OPC: competition-based, match Lafarge ±2%. SR + LH: value-based pricing at +25–35% premium, justified by performance specs and project lifetime savings (less rebar corrosion, lower thermal cracking). Add geographical pricing (Cairo zone vs Upper Egypt vs export), discount pricing for volume + payment-term contracts, and psychological/promotional pricing for end-of-quarter clearing of standard grade. [Slide: Kotler Ch.10 — Pricing strategies + adjustments]

Place (Ch.14): Selective distribution for specialty grades — direct sales to the buying centers of major contractors (Orascom, Hassan Allam, ACC). For commodity grade, maintain authorised-dealer network. VMS: Administered VMS (Pharaoh leads its dealer network with technical training + co-marketing).

Promotion (Ch.11): Personal selling is the primary tool in B2B (Kotler-default for high-ticket / technical sale). Build a 15-person technical sales force, each owning 3–5 key accounts. Direct marketing: technical white papers, project case-studies emailed to engineers. PR: technical conferences. Sales promotion: free site testing for the first project. Minimal advertising (B2B doesn't need mass-IMC). Apply the 6-step IMC process.

Case 7 — Talabat Egypt — Service-Quality Crisis (NEW problem case, fills Ch.13 services gap)

Why this case matters: None of cases 1–6 use the Ch.13 services-marketing 7Ps or the 4 service characteristics (Intangibility, Inseparability, Variability, Perishability). Dr. Alaa flagged services marketing in lecture; this case drills the full services framework.

Full scenario (~210 words):

Talabat Egypt is the leading food + grocery + q-commerce delivery platform (~5M monthly active users, ~40k registered riders, ~28k restaurant partners). Over Q4 2025 and Q1 2026, customer-satisfaction scores collapsed from NPS +42 to NPS -8 in four months. The complaint patterns:

(1) Service delivery variability — promised "30-minute" arrives in 65–90 minutes during peak hours; cold food; missing items. (2) Service-encounter failures — rider conduct complaints up 4× (rudeness, refusing to climb to upper floors, asking customers to come downstairs). (3) Recovery failures — customer-service chatbot replies in 6+ hours, refund decisions take 72 hours, no human escalation path. (4) Restaurant-partner side — kitchens are dropping out of the platform because Talabat raised commission to 30% (from 22%) without corresponding service support; restaurant-partner NPS is -22. (5) Competitive entry — Mrsool Egypt and Elmenus Now are running aggressive recruitment of restaurant partners with 18% commission.

Egyptian regulators have opened a market-conduct review after viral TikTok videos of failed deliveries. The MD has 4 months to restore customer + partner NPS and a 60M EGP recovery budget.

(a) Define the main problem and its nature. (~145 words)

Model: The main problem is a service-quality system failure, of a structural, multi-actor services-marketing nature — NOT a tech problem and NOT a pricing problem. The breakdown happens at every interface of the service triangle: company ↔ employee (riders inadequately trained/incentivised), company ↔ customer (broken brand promise of 30-min delivery), and employee ↔ customer (the live moment of truth at the door). The underlying mechanism is the 4 services characteristics biting all at once — Intangibility (customer can't pre-inspect), Inseparability (rider IS the brand at point of contact), Variability (no two deliveries identical), Perishability (an unfilled rider slot is lost forever). Plus a two-sided platform collapse: restaurant-partner attrition feeds customer attrition (fewer restaurants → worse selection → fewer orders → fewer rider hours → worse service). The nature is self-reinforcing services-failure spiral requiring simultaneous customer-side + partner-side intervention. [Slide: Kotler Ch.13 — Services Marketing + 4 Service Characteristics]

(b) Analyze from a marketing point of view. (~165 words)

Model: Five concepts converge: (1) Ch.13 4 services characteristics — each is unmanaged (Variability: no rider script; Inseparability: rider = brand but is undertrained; Intangibility: no service guarantee; Perishability: no surge-management). (2) Ch.13 7Ps for services — beyond 4Ps, the missing P-leverage is in People (rider training, hiring), Process (service blueprint for the 30-min promise), Physical evidence (delivery bag quality, rider uniform, packaging). (3) Ch.13 Service-Profit Chain — internal service quality (Talabat's treatment of riders) drives employee satisfaction drives external service quality drives customer satisfaction drives profit. Talabat broke link 1 (rider economics) which propagated to link 5 (NPS collapse). (4) Ch.1 Customer Satisfaction equation — perceived performance dropped below expectations. (5) Ch.11 IMC — the brand promise ("30 min") was over-claimed in advertising, then the operation failed to deliver — classic over-promise/under-deliver service-marketing trap. [Slide: Kotler Ch.13 + Ch.1 + Ch.11]

(c) Solutions across the marketing mix. (~315 words)

Model: 7Ps response plan, not 4Ps:

Product / Service (Ch.13): Redefine the service offer. Replace the absolute "30 min" promise with a tiered service guarantee: "Talabat Express" (30 min, +10 EGP fee, applies only inside 3-km zones at off-peak), "Talabat Standard" (60 min, free), "Talabat Scheduled" (pick your slot). Add a service-recovery guarantee: if late by >15 min, automatic 20-EGP credit. The differentiated service-tier model controls variability + perishability simultaneously. [Slide: Kotler Ch.13 — Service offer + 4 Characteristics]

People (Ch.13 — services-specific P): Rider training program (2-day onboarding + monthly refresh: customer interaction, building access protocol, handover script). Rider economics reset — raise base + performance bonuses tied to NPS, not just order count. The service-profit chain explicitly: fix internal service quality first. [Slide: Kotler Ch.13 — Service-Profit Chain]

Process (Ch.13 — services-specific P): Publish a service blueprint mapping every step from order-place → kitchen ack → rider assign → pickup → drop. Build a surge-pricing + surge-staffing mechanism for peak hours (perishability response). Customer-service: replace 6-hour chatbot with 5-min human SLA for complaint flagged as "delivery failure."

Physical Evidence (Ch.13): Upgrade rider bags (insulated, branded), rider uniforms, branded sealed-tape on bags — tangibilising the intangible.

Price (Ch.10): Restaurant-partner commission realignment — drop from 30% back to 24% but make it tiered (lower for high-volume partners). Customer-side: introduce price-segmented service tiers (Express +10 EGP). Use value-based pricing, not platform-monopoly extraction. [Slide: Kotler Ch.10]

Place (Ch.14): Multichannel distribution — beyond the app, partner with Carrefour for in-store grocery integration, and pilot dark-stores in dense neighborhoods for q-commerce sub-15-min vertical.

Promotion (Ch.11): STOP over-claiming. Pull "30 min guaranteed" ads. Replace with PR-led service-recovery story ("Talabat 2.0 — we listened"). Direct & Digital — WhatsApp Business for VIPs. Sales Promotion — credit-back for complaints, not free deliveries. Personal Selling — relationship managers for top 500 restaurant partners. [Slide: Kotler Ch.11 — 5 Tools, 6-Step IMC]

STP (Ch.7): Pivot to differentiated targeting with the new tiered service offer matched to segments (premium / standard / scheduled).

Case 8 — Danone Egypt — Activia Premium-Positioning Under Inflation (NEW problem case, built from the team's Danone research base)

Why this case matters: This case is directly built from the team's own Danone primary research (presentation_danon/research_summary.md). All facts (USD 144bn global yogurt size, Danone Egypt Obour plant, 47% Egyptian food inflation, Juhayna +23% H1 2025 revenue) are from the team's verified sources [3][5][11][15][16][17][22][31][32][33]. Drills Ch.7 STP repositioning + Ch.8 brand-portfolio strategy + Ch.10 pricing under macro-shock.

Full scenario (~225 words):

Danone Egypt entered in 2006 via its Obour Industrial Zone plant (40,000 m², 8–9 lines, 120k tons/year capacity, also exports to Libya, Mauritius, Kenya). The Egyptian portfolio spans Activia, Danone, Danio, Danonino, HiPro, Danette, Dango, Aptamil, Bebelac — a wide brand portfolio combining premium-fresh-dairy and specialised-nutrition. Danone became the world's first global B Corp in November 2025; Danone Egypt was Egypt's first B Corp in 2019.

Three structural shocks are eroding Danone Egypt's premium position over 2024–2026:

(1) Macro shock — Egyptian food inflation hit ~47% YoY and the EGP devalued ~38% in March 2024. Imported probiotic cultures, milk-protein concentrate, and packaging costs surged. Premium yogurt prices at retail jumped 35–50%. (2) Competitor pressure — Juhayna (the local leader) used scale to absorb input shocks better, raised prices only ~30% post-devaluation, kept its baqala (small-grocery) reach of ~400k outlets, and grew H1 2025 revenue +23% YoY. Almarai-owned Beyti (PepsiCo exit, Feb 2023) is also re-investing in mainstream-dairy share. (3) Channel mix problem — Danone wins in modern trade (Carrefour, Spinneys, q-commerce) but is structurally under-represented in baqala where ~70% of Egyptian dairy volume still moves. The "premium positioning vs purchasing-power collapse" tension is acute. Danone Egypt's brand-portfolio team has 9 months to restore unit-share and margin.

(a) Define the main problem and its nature. (~150 words)

Model: Danone Egypt's main problem is a premium-positioning vs purchasing-power collapse — a strategic STP + brand-portfolio problem of a macro-shock-induced, multi-segment nature. Functional / health-focused premium positioning (Activia, HiPro) is genuine brand equity built over a decade, but Egypt's 47% food inflation + 38% currency devaluation have compressed the addressable premium segment — many former premium-buyers have traded down to mainstream Juhayna. Simultaneously, Danone's brand portfolio is structurally top-heavy (most SKUs sit in the premium tier), leaving no defended mid-tier to catch the down-traders. The nature is not a brand-equity problem (Activia equity is intact) and not purely a pricing problem (you can't price away a 47% inflation gap). The root is missing portfolio tiers + missing baqala distribution — a structural STP-and-channel mismatch with the new macro reality. [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — STP] [Slide: Kotler Ch.8 — Brand Portfolio]

(b) Analyze from a marketing point of view. (~170 words)

Model: Five concepts converge: (1) Ch.7 STP — segment elasticity under macro shock: the "premium-functional-dairy" segment shrunk from ~18% to ~11% of urban A/B-class buyers as real incomes fell. Targeting strategy must shift from concentrated premium to differentiated multi-tier. (2) Ch.8 Brand portfolio architecture — Danone Egypt has a strong house of brands (Activia, HiPro, Danette, Dango etc.) but lacks a fighter brand in mainstream. Compare Nestlé Egypt's portfolio approach. (3) Ch.10 Pricing in inflationary environments — premium pricing cannot be defended by cost-plus pass-through; needs segmented pricing + sub-brand fighter at lower tier. (4) Ch.14 Channel/distribution mismatch — modern-trade-heavy strategy mis-aligns with where 70% of category volume actually sits (baqala). (5) Ch.11 Promotion mismatch — global B Corp / sustainability narrative is real equity but does not move price-elastic Egyptian shoppers in 2026; needs locally re-anchored value-equity messaging (Gawda + Omda social programs). [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 + Ch.8 + Ch.10 + Ch.11 + Ch.14]

(c) Solutions across the marketing mix. (~315 words)

Model:

STP first (Ch.7): Shift from concentrated (premium-only) to differentiated (multi-tier) targeting. Re-segment: (i) Premium-functional (urban A/B, health-conscious) — defend with Activia + HiPro; (ii) Mid-tier mainstream (urban C, family-buyer) — NEW Danone Egypt fighter sub-brand at Juhayna-parity price; (iii) Specialised-nutrition (Aptamil, Bebelac infant + medical) — protect, this segment is inflation-resilient (parents won't trade down on baby nutrition). Rewrite positioning per tier — don't try to make Activia mean "premium + affordable" simultaneously (positioning suicide). [Slide: Kotler Ch.7 — STP + Differentiated Targeting]

Product (Ch.8): Add a fighter sub-brand — e.g., "Danone Yomy" (Egyptian-Arabic name signals locality) — positioned at the Juhayna parity tier. Use the existing Obour-plant lines to make it (operational lift is small). Brand-portfolio discipline: keep Activia / HiPro premium message untouched (no discounts, no value-tier extensions — that would dilute equity). Packaging for fighter brand uses lower-cost format (pouch instead of cup) — explicit cost-engineering tied to format choice. Dr. Alaa-style brand-name selection emphasis: the fighter sub-brand needs a name that signals "for everyone" without contaminating the parent. [Slide: Kotler Ch.8 — Brand Architecture + Packaging]

Price (Ch.10): Segmented pricing across portfolio tiers. Premium tier: hold list price, use value-based pricing anchored to functional/probiotic benefits (Lec 5 framing). Fighter brand: competition-based pricing matched to Juhayna. Specialised-nutrition: maintain premium (parental inelasticity). Add promotional pricing selectively in modern trade for end-of-quarter clearance, NEVER in premium tier. [Slide: Kotler Ch.10 — Three pricing approaches + price adjustments]

Place (Ch.14): Multichannel push into baqala for the fighter brand only (don't dilute Activia by putting it in 200-EGP-cart baqala stores — Case 4 lesson). VMS: Administered with baqala distributors (existing Juhayna playbook). Maintain selective distribution for premium SKUs. Sustain modern-trade + q-commerce dominance. [Slide: Kotler Ch.14 — Multichannel + Distribution Intensity]

Promotion (Ch.11): Dual-narrative IMC. Premium tier: keep functional/health/B-Corp narrative for urban A/B. Fighter brand: separate IMC plan — local-Egyptian storytelling, Gawda / Omda social programs as proof of local roots, mass-reach TV + radio + WhatsApp loyalty. Apply 6-step IMC process per tier. PR: lean into B-Corp-first-in-Egypt (2019) credential to differentiate from Juhayna on sustainability axis.

Case 9 — Spinneys Bio — Private-Label Organic Launch (NEW; 5-question, 25-mark rubric; Egyptian Strategic Marketing chapters)

Format note: This case uses the 5-named-sub-question / 25-mark rubric (Cultural / Segmentation+Targeting / Positioning / Marketing Mix / Communication) — same structural depth as Prof Alaa's International Marketing July 2025 final Q1 (Fit Food → India). But the scenario is Egyptian-only and the model answers cite Strategic Marketing chapters only (Ch.6 / 7 / 8 / 10 / 11 / 14).
